[108 | 2026-08-28T16:17:45.848Z | assistant | None]
A useful way to read the theorem is that it rules out three degenerate limits at once: the features do not freeze, the activation does not become merely affine on the visited distribution, and the limiting dynamics do not require retaining the whole finite-width history. The price is that the state must be richer than just the marginal laws of the neurons.

[184 | 2026-08-28T16:20:26.976Z | assistant | None]
The theorem says that this specially scaled two-hidden-layer network has a genuine, deterministic infinite-width training dynamics—not merely a frozen-kernel limit.

The finite network is

\[
H_i=\arctan(A_i),\qquad
S_j=\sum_i B_{ji}H_i,\qquad
G_j=\arctan(S_j),\qquad
f_n=\sum_j c_jG_j,
\]

trained on the single target \(1\). The initialization is tuned so that \(A_i,S_j=O(1)\), while \(f_n(0)\to0\). Although every per-step learning coefficient tends to zero, training is observed over \(O(n^2)\) steps per unit time.

The key normalization is

\[
C=nc,\qquad X=F(A),\qquad F(a)=a+\frac{a^3}{3}.
\]

Writing \(J=F^{-1}\) and \(q(x)=1/(1+x^2)=\arctan'(x)\), the limiting evolution is

\[
\begin{aligned}
A&=J(X), & H&=\arctan(A), & S&=BH,\\
G&=\arctan(S), &D&=Cq(S), &e&=\langle C,G\rangle-1,
\end{aligned}
\]

\[
\boxed{
\dot X=-2eB^*D,\qquad
\dot B=-2e\,D\otimes H,\qquad
\dot C=-2eG.
}
\]

These are exactly the usual backpropagation operations:

- \(C\) learns from the final features \(G\).
- \(D=Cq(S)\) is the error signal gated through the second activation.
- \(B\) receives a rank-one update \(D\otimes H\).
- \(B^*D\) propagates the signal back to the first layer.

The \(X\)-coordinate is not a linearization. Since \(F'(a)=1/q(a)\), it exactly cancels the factor \(q(A)\) in the first-layer equation, producing estimates that remain uniform as \(n\to\infty\).

The unusual phrase “action-law flow” is important. The marginal distributions of \(A\), \(S\), and \(C\) do not determine the future: knowing \(S=BH\) does not determine \(B^*D\). The limiting state therefore remembers the joint laws and inner products of all quantities generated by applying \(B\), \(B^*\), and the nonlinearities. That richer state is autonomous and restartable: its current value, without its history or initialization array, determines its future.

The convergence claim is correspondingly strong. Along the full width sequence:

- exact discrete GD converges in probability, uniformly on every fixed time interval;
- the empirical distributions of the complete paths \(A_i(\cdot)\) and \(S_j(\cdot)\) converge in \(W_2(C([0,T]))\);
- the output, loss, three scaled kernel blocks, and hidden-layer velocity energies also converge.

Why is this genuinely feature learning? At limiting initialization \(C(0)=0\), so the readout moves first:

\[
C(t)=2tG_0+O(t^2).
\]

This creates a backpropagated signal of order \(t\), giving

\[
A(t)-A_0=O(t^2),\qquad
B(t)-B_0=O(t^2),\qquad
S(t)-S_0=O(t^2),
\]

with strictly positive leading mean-square coefficients. Consequently,

\[
K^A(t)=\alpha t^2+o(t^2),\qquad
K^B(t)=\beta t^2+o(t^2),\qquad \alpha,\beta>0,
\]

and

\[
K(t)=K(0)+\gamma t^2+o(t^2),\qquad \gamma>0.
\]

Thus both hidden representations move and the tangent kernel changes; this rules out a frozen-feature/constant-NTK interpretation. Meanwhile,

\[
L(t)=1-4k_3t+o(t),\qquad k_3>0,
\]

so the loss immediately decreases.

“Nonlinear” also has a precise meaning: for each hidden preactivation \(Z\),

\[
\inf_{a,b}\mathbb E\!\left[
\bigl(\arctan Z-a-bZ\bigr)^2
\right]>0
\]

for sufficiently short time. Thus \(\arctan\) has not effectively collapsed to an affine function on the distributions visited by the flow.

The scope is deliberately narrow: one training example, arctangent activation, and these exact scalings. It does not prove convergence to a global minimizer or cover arbitrary datasets. The infinite-width convergence holds on every finite interval, while the strict feature-learning and nonlinearity conclusions are proved only for a sufficiently short interval. The sole research-level external input is the fixed finite reused-Gaussian tensor-program theorem; I found no material internal contradiction in how it is used.

The relevant parts are the [theorem statement](/tmp/L2_FULL_AUDITED_PROOF.md:3), the [limiting action flow](/tmp/L2_FULL_AUDITED_PROOF.md:257), and the [short-time feature-learning calculation](/tmp/L2_FULL_AUDITED_PROOF.md:903).

[194 | 2026-08-28T16:21:27.318Z | user | None]
<skill>
<name>teach-technical-math</name>
<path>/etc/codex/skills/teach-technical-math/SKILL.md</path>
---
name: teach-technical-math
description: Teach the mathematical content of technical documents—papers, reports, notes, appendices, and linked revisions—through rigorous, self-contained, one-concept-at-a-time lessons. Use when the user supplies or references one or more sources and wants to understand their definitions, equations, derivations, proofs, models, limiting constructions, evidence, assumptions, contradictions across versions, or open gaps over multiple turns; also use when they say “next block” or ask to rebuild a confusing part of such a lesson. Reconcile source versions before teaching, preserve stable typed notation, derive rather than paraphrase, and distinguish exact results from formal, heuristic, empirical, conjectural, and open claims.
---

# Teach Mathematics from Technical Documents

Act as a mathematical and theoretical teacher, not a summarizer. Teach a motivated non-specialist at genuine mathematical depth. Expose the simple structure beneath advanced formalism without suppressing difficult steps.

## Prepare from the sources

Read every source the user identifies in full mathematical detail before teaching source-dependent claims. If a source is inaccessible, incomplete, or missing cited material, state the limitation and do not imply that it was read.

Build an internal source ledger:

- record each source's identity, date, and status, such as preliminary, revised, audited, or final;
- map notation and assumption changes across versions;
- identify the precise objects, problem, model, regime, observables, main claims, constructions, arguments, evidence, and unresolved gaps;
- separate compatible claims from incompatible ones;
- determine which claims survived later corrections or audits;
- retain theorem, equation, section, and page locations when available.

Do not dump this ledger as an opening summary. Use it to teach the requested block faithfully. Attribute source claims and distinguish them from your own interpretation.

Maintain an internal lesson ledger across turns: stable notation, completed blocks, the last point the user clearly understood, unresolved confusion, and the next logical block. Minimally recall any earlier definition needed in the current response.

## Control the pace

Teach exactly one natural conceptual block per response. If the user specifies a starting point, begin there. Otherwise begin with the document's objects, assumptions, and concrete setup.

Use this domain-neutral progression as a guide:

1. objects, assumptions, and setup;
2. exact concrete or finite formulation;
3. meaning and type of the central variables or maps;
4. derivation of the main relations;
5. proof or mechanism architecture;
6. obstruction, non-closure, or source of difficulty;
7. proposed abstraction, approximation, or limiting construction;
8. assumptions connecting the construction to the original problem;
9. theoretical and empirical evidence;
10. remaining gaps and falsifying cases.

Adjust the sequence to the sources. Do not jump ahead merely because later material is related. When the user says “next block,” continue with only the next logically necessary idea. When the user is confused, stop advancing and rebuild from the last understood point.

Write each ordinary lesson turn as a compact, seamless blackboard explanation: a few short paragraphs, only the equations needed now, few or no bullets, and normally no headings. Do not begin or end with a broad recap. Stop naturally after the current block and wait for the user before moving deeper.

## Construct each block

Use this internal order without displaying it as a template:

1. **Minimal recall:** restate only the definitions needed now.
2. **New object:** define one new object and give its mathematical type.
3. **Derivation:** obtain its relation from the preceding setup.
4. **Meaning:** explain the role of every term or condition.
5. **Analogue or example:** use one structurally faithful simple case when it genuinely helps, then return to the exact mathematics.
6. **Role:** connect the object to the document's argument.
7. **Status:** state what is established and what remains unproved.

Make the block self-contained for its present conceptual burden, not for the entire project.

## Enforce notation and type discipline

Use a small, stable notation. Prefer the source's symbols unless they collide or obscure meaning. If renaming is necessary, state the mapping explicitly.

Define every symbol before using it. For each object, state as relevant:

- its domain, codomain, or ambient space;
- whether it is a scalar, vector, matrix, function, operator, random variable, measure, index, or coordinate;
- whether it is fixed, sampled, or evolving;
- what each index means;
- its dependence on other variables or parameters.

Distinguish carefully between:

- one function of an entire vector and a vector of coordinatewise functions;
- an index and a coordinate;
- a fixed label or parameter and an evolving state;
- a representative element and a probability law over elements;
- a mathematical state and a numerical representation of that state.

When writing an integral, identify the integration variable, its space, the measure, and what the integral averages or accumulates. When a distribution is specified only at initialization, do not imply that an evolved quantity retains that distribution merely because it remains a function of the initial label.

Do not introduce shorthand unless the explicit expression is genuinely cumbersome and recurs often. Never reuse one symbol for two roles.

## Derive rather than paraphrase

For every central equation or implication:

1. state the definition or prior relation from which it follows;
2. show the relevant algebraic, differential, variational, probabilistic, logical, or limiting step;
3. identify the origin and action of every term;
4. explain the closest elementary analogue when useful;
5. state why the relation matters to the main result.

Do not make the user reverse-engineer a displayed formula. Avoid phrases such as “clearly,” “standard,” or “it follows” when they conceal a nontrivial step.

When invoking an external theorem, state the exact form needed, list its hypotheses, verify them in the present setting, and explain the resulting conclusion. Check a primary or otherwise authoritative source before relying on a recent, specialized, or uncertain result.

When teaching a long proof, devote one block to its architecture before teaching the difficult steps. Explain how the claims, cases, or lemmas fit together without pretending that the architecture is itself the proof.

Use analogies only when they preserve the relevant mathematical structure. State where the analogy stops and return immediately to the exact object.

## Relate levels of description

Begin with the document's concrete objects and exact relations. Introduce an abstract, aggregate, continuum, asymptotic, or numerical description only after showing how it arises from the concrete one.

Whenever the source changes levels, identify:

- the map from the original objects to the new description;
- what information is retained and discarded;
- which step is an exact identity;
- which step uses a theorem and its assumptions;
- which step is formal, heuristic, empirical, or conjectural.

If a proposed state or summary evolves autonomously, distinguish internal closure from fidelity to the original system. An autonomous reduced description may still omit information that changes the original system's future.

If the source uses truncation or approximation, distinguish pointwise or static approximation from the stronger control required by an evolving or iterative system. Examine separately:

- error that leaves the retained representation;
- feedback from omitted components;
- amplification or accumulation of error;
- the time, parameter, or regime over which stability is claimed.

When the source calls a construction “finite,” state precisely whether this means finitely many coordinates, finitely many fields, a finite-dimensional state, a finite cutoff, or merely a finite numerical discretization.

## Label epistemic status

Classify each important source-dependent claim using the most accurate category:

- exact identity;
- proved theorem under stated assumptions;
- rigorous result for a restricted or finite setting;
- formal argument;
- heuristic mechanism;
- empirical or numerical evidence;
- conjectural identification;
- open implication;
- counterexample or negative result.

Do not silently promote a claim. Express each important assumption as a missing implication:

1. what is assumed;
2. what conclusion it would justify;
3. what connecting step is not established;
4. what observation or counterexample would refute it.

Internal consistency of a proposed theory does not prove that it represents the target object.

## Evaluate evidence and alternatives

For every numerical comparison, identify:

- the systems or quantities compared;
- what the reference actually represents;
- the metric or norm;
- the scale of the predicted effect and the discrepancy;
- discretization, optimization, rounding, and sampling uncertainty as applicable;
- the alternative explanation ruled out;
- what the experiment leaves unresolved.

Do not infer convergence or a scaling law from too few resolutions. Treat numerical thresholds as decision conventions unless the source proves a mathematical boundary.

When the user requests a hostile or devil's-advocate analysis, include only alternatives that engage the declared target and make a distinct mathematical or quantitative prediction. Do not infer global behavior from a local or instantaneous property without proving the connecting step. Do not use possible error cancellation as a complete explanation unless it predicts the observed pattern.

## Repair confusion completely

When the user identifies an undefined symbol, ambiguity, missing derivation, or conceptual gap:

- acknowledge the failure directly;
- do not defend the prior wording;
- do not append a one-sentence patch;
- rewrite the entire relevant block self-containedly;
- preserve compactness and stable notation.

Treat confusion as evidence that the explanation needs rebuilding, not that the mathematics is inherently inaccessible.

Before sending each block, verify silently that it is faithful to the sources, introduces only one conceptual burden, defines every needed symbol, derives rather than paraphrases, states the status of important claims, stays in the user's language, avoids unnecessary notation and formula dumps, and is compact enough to absorb in one sitting.

</skill>

[202 | 2026-08-28T16:21:52.092Z | assistant | None]
I’m using the named technical-math teaching skill, so I’ll build this as a sequence of self-contained blackboard blocks. This first block will give only the proof architecture—with one stable set of canonical symbols—and then pause before we derive Bridge 1.

[218 | 2026-08-28T16:22:44.375Z | assistant | None]
Fix a finite time horizon \(T>0\). The proof’s central goal is

\[
\boxed{\text{exact width-}n\text{ GD trajectory}}
\quad\longrightarrow\quad
\boxed{\text{unique continuum action-law trajectory}}
\]

uniformly for \(0\le t\le T\), in probability.

We will use these symbols throughout:

- \(\theta_n^{\mathrm{GD}}(t)\): the linearly interpolated exact GD state.
- \(\theta_n(t)\): the corresponding finite-width continuous gradient flow.
- \(\theta_n^\delta(t)\): explicit Euler applied to that flow with a fixed mesh \(\delta>0\).
- \(\Theta^\delta(t)\): Euler applied to the continuum flow with the same mesh.
- \(\Theta(t)\): the exact continuum action-law flow.

All these states use the exact transformed variables

\[
C=nc,\qquad X=F(A),\qquad F(a)=a+\frac{a^3}{3}.
\]

Thus a finite-width state is the triple

\[
\theta_n=(X_n,B_n,C_n),
\]

where \(X_n,C_n\in\mathbb R^n\) and \(B_n\in\mathbb R^{n\times n}\). The continuum state is

\[
\Theta=(X,B,C),
\]

where \(X\) and \(C\) are square-integrable random coordinates and \(B\) is a bounded operator between two Hilbert spaces.

The entire convergence proof is the following sandwich:

\[
\theta_n^{\mathrm{GD}}
\;\approx\;
\theta_n
\;\approx\;
\theta_n^\delta
\;\longrightarrow\;
\Theta^\delta
\;\approx\;
\Theta.
\]

The order of limits is essential: first \(n\to\infty\) while \(\delta\) is fixed, and only afterward \(\delta\downarrow0\).

### 1. Rewrite the finite network as a dimension-free flow

Direct differentiation of the finite network gives an exact ODE for \((A,B,C)\). The factor \(q(A)=1/(1+A^2)\) appearing in the \(A\)-equation is removed by \(X=F(A)\), because

\[
F'(A)=1+A^2=\frac1{q(A)}.
\]

The resulting equations are exactly

\[
\dot X=-2eB^\top D,\qquad
\dot B=-2e\,D\otimes_n H,\qquad
\dot C=-2eG,
\]

where

\[
A=F^{-1}(X),\quad H=\arctan(A),\quad S=BH,
\]

\[
G=\arctan(S),\quad D=Cq(S),\quad
e=\frac1n\sum_{j=1}^n C_jG_j-1.
\]

This is an identity at every finite width, not an approximation.

### 2. Prove estimates that do not deteriorate with width

Use the normalized vector norm

\[
\|v\|_n^2=\frac1n\sum_{i=1}^n v_i^2
\]

and the matrix operator norm. The state distance is

\[
\|X-\widetilde X\|_n+
\|B-\widetilde B\|_{\mathrm{op}}+
\|C-\widetilde C\|_n.
\]

On every finite time interval, the proof establishes width-independent bounds on \(B\), \(C\), and the vector field. It then proves a width-independent Lipschitz estimate

\[
\|V_n(\theta)-V_n(\widetilde\theta)\|
\le L_T\|\theta-\widetilde\theta\|,
\]

where \(V_n\) is the preceding ODE vector field and \(L_T\) does not depend on \(n\).

Consequently, finite-width Euler approximation satisfies

\[
\sup_{t\le T}
\|\theta_n^\delta(t)-\theta_n(t)\|
\le C_T\delta,
\]

again with \(C_T\) independent of \(n\). This uniformity is what later permits the mesh to be removed after taking the width limit.

### 3. Construct the continuum flow before taking the limit

The continuum state cannot consist only of the marginal laws of \(A\) and \(S\). The velocity of \(X\) contains \(B^*D\), and the law of \(BH\) does not determine the law of \(B^*D\).

The proof therefore constructs two canonical Hilbert spaces:

\[
\mathcal H_R\quad\text{for first-layer quantities},\qquad
\mathcal H_L\quad\text{for second-layer quantities}.
\]

The initial random matrix becomes a bounded operator

\[
B_0:\mathcal H_R\to\mathcal H_L,
\]

with its genuine Hilbert adjoint \(B_0^*\). On these fixed spaces the continuum equations are

\[
\dot X=-2eB^*D,\qquad
\dot B=-2e\,D\otimes H,\qquad
\dot C=-2eG,
\]

where now

\[
(D\otimes H)v=D\langle H,v\rangle_{\mathcal H_R}.
\]

A contraction argument proves local existence and uniqueness. Energy and norm bounds prevent finite-time blow-up, giving a global solution. Because the future uses only the current \(X,C,B,B^*\) actions, the flow is autonomous and restartable.

### 4. Identify the width limit at a fixed Euler mesh

Fix \(\delta>0\). Then the number of Euler steps before time \(T\),

\[
N=\left\lceil\frac{T}{\delta}\right\rceil,
\]

is finite and independent of \(n\).

The actual Euler scheme is still not directly a fixed tensor program: its scalar coefficients contain empirical inner products that depend on width. The proof therefore introduces an auxiliary population oracle, denoted here by \(\bar\theta_n^\delta\). It replaces those empirical coefficients with the corresponding deterministic inner products from the continuum Euler trajectory.

Because \(\delta\) and \(N\) are fixed, this oracle uses only finitely many applications of \(B_0\) and \(B_0^\top\). The reused-Gaussian tensor-program theorem therefore applies and gives

\[
\bar\theta_n^\delta\longrightarrow\Theta^\delta
\]

for every finite collection of observable probes.

The oracle is not silently identified with the real Euler scheme. Their difference consists of explicit empirical Gram-matrix errors. The tensor theorem makes those errors tend to zero, and a discrete Gronwall estimate gives

\[
\sup_{k\le N}
\|\theta_{n,k}^\delta-\bar\theta_{n,k}^\delta\|
\longrightarrow0.
\]

Therefore, at every fixed mesh,

\[
\theta_n^\delta\longrightarrow\Theta^\delta.
\]

This is the only point where the research-level tensor-program theorem enters.

### 5. Remove the mesh

The triangle inequality gives, schematically,

\[
\|\theta_n-\Theta\|
\le
\underbrace{\|\theta_n-\theta_n^\delta\|}_{\le C_T\delta}
+
\underbrace{\|\theta_n^\delta-\Theta^\delta\|}_{\to0\text{ as }n\to\infty}
+
\underbrace{\|\Theta^\delta-\Theta\|}_{\le C_T\delta}.
\]

First fix \(\delta\) and let \(n\to\infty\). The middle term disappears. Then let \(\delta\downarrow0\). The two outer terms disappear.

This proves convergence of the finite-width continuous flow:

\[
\theta_n\longrightarrow\Theta.
\]

The convergence topology records every finite collection of quantities produced by nested applications of \(B\), \(B^*\), and the coordinatewise nonlinearities. This is precisely enough information to determine the action law.

### 6. Return from continuous flow to exact discrete GD

The \(B\)- and \(C\)-updates of exact GD are already Euler updates for the transformed ODE. Only the \(X=F(A)\) update has an extra defect.

Because \(F\) is cubic, that defect can be calculated exactly rather than bounded by an unspecified Taylor remainder. Accumulating it over the interval gives

\[
\sup_{t\le T}
\|\theta_n^{\mathrm{GD}}(t)-\theta_n(t)\|
\le
C_T\bigl(
\eta_n+\eta_n\sqrt n+\eta_n^2n
\bigr).
\]

With \(\eta_n=n^{-2}\), the right-hand side is

\[
C_T\left(n^{-2}+n^{-3/2}+n^{-3}\right)\longrightarrow0.
\]

Combining this with the previous bridge proves

\[
\theta_n^{\mathrm{GD}}\longrightarrow\Theta.
\]

Path-law convergence, kernel convergence, and positive feature motion are not automatic consequences of this state convergence. They require three final upgrades: time-equicontinuity for whole paths, truncation and uniform tail control for the unbounded backpropagated field, and the explicit small-time expansion that proves both hidden layers actually move. Those form the final proof block after the convergence mechanism above.

let's talk about the high level goal first, can you explain the main coal super clearly&#x20;


[229 | 2026-08-28T16:24:03.597Z | assistant | None]
The main goal is:

\[
\boxed{\text{Replace an increasingly large trained network by one exact, width-independent learning system.}}
\]

For width \(n\), the network contains \(n^2+2n\) parameters. As \(n\to\infty\), tracking every parameter becomes meaningless. The proof seeks a continuum state \(\Theta(t)\) that describes what the whole network population is doing at training time \(t\).

This continuum description must satisfy three requirements.

First, it must be faithful to exact GD. If the finite network is trained using the stated width-dependent step sizes, then its complete trajectory—not merely its final output—must approach \(\Theta(t)\). This includes the distributions of hidden neurons, the output, loss, tangent-kernel blocks, and hidden-layer velocities.

Second, it must be closed. Knowing \(\Theta(t)\) at the present time must determine its future without remembering the original finite matrix or the entire training history. This is what “autonomous and restartable” means.

Third, it must be nontrivial. Many infinite-width limits become degenerate:

- the hidden features freeze and only the last layer learns;
- the tangent kernel becomes constant;
- or the nonlinear activation behaves effectively like an affine function.

This theorem aims to rule out all three. It proves that both hidden layers move, all three parameter blocks contribute, the kernel changes with time, the loss decreases, and the arctangent remains genuinely nonlinear under the evolving hidden distributions.

The central difficulty is that ordinary neuron distributions are not enough. Forward propagation uses \(BH\), while backpropagation uses \(B^*D\). Because the same matrix \(B\) is reused in both directions, training creates correlations that are lost if we retain only the marginal distributions of the hidden neurons. The “action law” is the richer continuum state that remembers exactly these operator correlations.

So the theorem is not primarily trying to solve the optimization problem or prove that the loss eventually reaches zero. Its main achievement is more foundational:

\[
\boxed{
\text{Exact finite neural-network training has a rigorous, closed, nonlinear infinite-width dynamics.}
}
\]

That is the object the rest of the proof constructs and then verifies.

[5 | 2026-08-28T16:24:22.352Z | user | None]
let's talk about the high level goal first, can you explain the main coal super clearly&#x20;, both on the high conceptual level and what that means substantively mathematically

[14 | 2026-08-28T16:24:49.514Z | assistant | None]
I’m using the technical-math teaching workflow again because you’re asking to reset the lesson at the theorem’s actual objective. I’ll stay entirely at that level here: what problem the theorem solves, the exact mathematical success criterion, and what it deliberately does not claim.

[23 | 2026-08-28T16:25:25.382Z | assistant | None]
The theorem’s main goal is not merely to compute the limiting output \(f(t)\). It is to prove that the entire training process of a wide, two-hidden-layer network approaches a well-defined infinite-width dynamical system in which both hidden layers genuinely learn.

## Conceptual goal

For every width \(n\), training is:

- random, because the parameters are randomly initialized;
- discrete, because it uses gradient-descent steps;
- very high-dimensional, with \(n^2\) middle-layer weights;
- width-dependent, because initialization and learning rates change with \(n\).

We ask whether, as \(n\to\infty\), this complicated random system settles into a predictable macroscopic evolution.

There are several ways this could fail:

1. The dynamics might blow up or have no width limit.
2. Only the output weights might learn while the hidden features freeze.
3. The activation might effectively become affine, producing a disguised linear model.
4. A limiting formula might exist but fail to be autonomous: predicting its future might require remembering the entire initialization or training history.
5. Different subsequences of widths might produce different limits.

The theorem rules out all five failures for this particular network and scaling. Its conceptual conclusion is:

\[
\boxed{
\text{Wide-network training self-averages into one closed, deterministic,
nonlinear feature-learning flow.}
}
\]

“Deterministic” here means deterministic at the level of laws. The limiting variables can be represented as random variables, but their joint law and its evolution are fixed rather than depending on the particular finite-width initialization.

## Exact mathematical goal

Let \(k\) be the GD iteration and set

\[
t=k\eta_n,\qquad \eta_n=n^{-2}.
\]

Thus observing a fixed macroscopic time \(t\) means running approximately \(tn^2\) GD steps.

The proof uses the exact transformed state

\[
\Theta_n^{\mathrm{GD}}(t)
=
\bigl(X_n^{\mathrm{GD}}(t),B_n^{\mathrm{GD}}(t),C_n^{\mathrm{GD}}(t)\bigr),
\]

where

\[
C_n=nc_n,\qquad
X_n=F(A_n),\qquad
F(a)=a+\frac{a^3}{3}.
\]

Here:

- \(X_n(t)\in\mathbb R^n\) represents the first-layer preactivations through an invertible change of coordinates;
- \(B_n(t)\in\mathbb R^{n\times n}\) is the middle weight matrix;
- \(C_n(t)\in\mathbb R^n\) is the naturally rescaled readout vector.

The primary theorem is the existence of a deterministic continuum state \(\Theta(t)\) such that, for every finite \(T\),

\[
\sup_{0\le t\le T}
d_{\mathrm{act}}\!\left(
\Theta_n^{\mathrm{GD}}(t),\Theta(t)
\right)
\longrightarrow 0
\quad\text{in probability}.
\]

Explicitly, this means that for every \(\varepsilon>0\),

\[
\mathbb P\!\left(
\sup_{0\le t\le T}
d_{\mathrm{act}}\!\left(
\Theta_n^{\mathrm{GD}}(t),\Theta(t)
\right)>\varepsilon
\right)
\longrightarrow0.
\]

The distance \(d_{\mathrm{act}}\) is richer than a distance between ordinary neuron distributions. It tests the joint laws and inner products of every finite collection of quantities generated from the current state using:

- the middle operator \(B\);
- its adjoint \(B^*\);
- the activation and derivative gates;
- finite compositions of those operations.

This richer state is necessary because knowing the law of

\[
S=BH
\]

does not determine the law of

\[
B^*D.
\]

But \(B^*D\) determines the next first-layer velocity. Therefore the marginal laws of the neurons alone do not contain enough information to predict training.

The continuum state evolves autonomously:

\[
\dot X=-2eB^*D,\qquad
\dot B=-2e\,D\otimes H,\qquad
\dot C=-2eG,
\]

with all the derived quantities \(A,H,S,G,D,e\) determined by the current \((X,B,C)\). Autonomy is expressed by the semigroup identity

\[
\Phi_t\bigl(\Phi_s(\Theta_0)\bigr)
=
\Phi_{t+s}(\Theta_0).
\]

So if training is stopped at time \(s\), the action law at that moment contains everything needed to restart it. Neither the original matrix nor the past trajectory must be attached as hidden memory.

The theorem then makes the convergence tangible through the neuron paths. Define the empirical law of the entire first-layer trajectories by

\[
\Lambda_n^A
=
\frac1n\sum_{i=1}^n
\delta_{A_{n,i}(\cdot)}.
\]

This is a probability measure on \(C([0,T])\), the space of continuous real-valued paths. The theorem proves

\[
\Lambda_n^A\longrightarrow \mathcal L(A(\cdot))
\quad\text{in }W_2(C([0,T])),
\]

and similarly

\[
\frac1n\sum_{j=1}^n\delta_{S_{n,j}(\cdot)}
\longrightarrow \mathcal L(S(\cdot)).
\]

Thus it is not merely saying that the neuron distributions converge separately at each time. It says that the distributions of their complete time trajectories converge, including temporal correlations and second moments.

Finally, the theorem must show that the resulting limit is nontrivial. For sufficiently small \(T>0\), it proves

\[
\int_0^T\mathbb E|\dot A(t)|^2\,dt>0,
\qquad
\int_0^T\mathbb E|\dot S(t)|^2\,dt>0.
\]

Therefore both hidden layers really move. It also proves positive displacement:

\[
\mathbb E|A(T)-A(0)|^2>0,\qquad
\mathbb E|S(T)-S(0)|^2>0.
\]

All three parameter blocks contribute positively to training, and the total tangent kernel satisfies

\[
K(t)\neq K(0)
\]

for small nonzero \(t\). This rules out a frozen-kernel limit.

Nonlinearity is expressed by

\[
\inf_{a,b\in\mathbb R}
\mathbb E\left[
\bigl(\arctan Z(t)-a-bZ(t)\bigr)^2
\right]>0
\]

for each hidden preactivation \(Z=A,S\). Hence the activation cannot be replaced, even in mean square under the evolving hidden law, by an affine function.

So the substantive target has three parts:

\[
\boxed{
\begin{array}{c}
\text{Fidelity: exact finite GD converges to the proposed limit;}\\[2mm]
\text{Closure: the current limiting state determines its own future;}\\[2mm]
\text{Nondegeneracy: both hidden layers move and remain genuinely nonlinear.}
\end{array}}
\]

This is a finite-time scaling-limit theorem. It does not claim that the loss eventually reaches zero, that training finds a global optimizer, or that the result already extends to arbitrary datasets and architectures.

[40 | 2026-08-28T16:28:36.688Z | assistant | None]
Yes—the previous notation inherited the proof’s internal bookkeeping and obscured the network. The teaching workflow calls for rebuilding the whole block when notation causes confusion, so I’m replacing it permanently with layer-indexed \(z^{(\ell)},W^{(\ell)},\phi\), and standard backpropagation symbols.

[48 | 2026-08-28T16:29:16.808Z | assistant | None]
Yes. I will no longer use \(A,H,S,G,B,C,D,P,e\) as the teaching notation. Those are proof-internal names, not good neural-network notation.

From now on, layer indices are superscripts:

\[
z^{(\ell)}=\text{preactivation at layer }\ell,\qquad
\phi(z^{(\ell)})=\text{activation},
\]

\[
W^{(\ell)}=\text{weights entering layer }\ell,
\qquad
\phi(u)=\arctan u.
\]

The proof’s original symbols translate as

\[
A=z^{(1)},\quad
H=\phi(z^{(1)}),\quad
B=W^{(2)},\quad
S=z^{(2)},\quad
G=\phi(z^{(2)}),\quad
c=W^{(3)}.
\]

The input and target are both the scalar \(1\). Therefore

\[
z_i^{(1)}=W_i^{(1)}\cdot1=W_i^{(1)}.
\]

So although first-layer weights and first-layer preactivations are numerically identical here, we will call them \(z_i^{(1)}\) when discussing the network computation.

The complete network is

\[
z_i^{(1)}=W_i^{(1)},
\]

\[
z_j^{(2)}
=
\sum_{i=1}^n
W_{ji}^{(2)}\phi\!\left(z_i^{(1)}\right),
\]

\[
f_n
=
\sum_{j=1}^n
W_j^{(3)}\phi\!\left(z_j^{(2)}\right),
\]

with loss

\[
L_n=(f_n-1)^2.
\]

The initialization is

\[
W_i^{(1)}(0)\sim N(0,1),
\]

\[
W_{ji}^{(2)}(0)\sim N\!\left(0,\frac1n\right),
\]

\[
W_j^{(3)}(0)\sim N\!\left(0,\frac1{n^4}\right),
\]

independently.

Because the output weights are unusually small, the natural output-layer variable is the rescaled weight

\[
\overline W_j^{(3)}=nW_j^{(3)}.
\]

Then the output becomes the normalized population average

\[
f_n
=
\frac1n\sum_{j=1}^n
\overline W_j^{(3)}
\phi\!\left(z_j^{(2)}\right).
\]

Define the scalar prediction residual

\[
r_n=f_n-1.
\]

Define the second-layer backpropagated signal by

\[
\delta_j^{(2)}
=
\overline W_j^{(3)}
\phi'\!\left(z_j^{(2)}\right),
\]

and the first-layer backpropagated signal by

\[
\delta_i^{(1)}
=
\phi'\!\left(z_i^{(1)}\right)
\sum_{j=1}^n
W_{ji}^{(2)}\delta_j^{(2)}.
\]

With the theorem’s layerwise learning-rate scaling, the associated continuous-time equations are

\[
\frac{d}{dt}z_i^{(1)}
=
-2r_n\,\delta_i^{(1)},
\]

\[
\frac{d}{dt}W_{ji}^{(2)}
=
-\frac{2r_n}{n}\,
\delta_j^{(2)}
\phi\!\left(z_i^{(1)}\right),
\]

\[
\frac{d}{dt}\overline W_j^{(3)}
=
-2r_n\phi\!\left(z_j^{(2)}\right).
\]

These are just backpropagation written with the normalization appropriate for width \(n\).

The theorem’s primary mathematical goal can now be stated cleanly:

\[
\boxed{
\begin{array}{c}
\text{As }n\to\infty,\text{ the exact discrete-GD evolution of}\\[1mm]
\bigl(z_n^{(1)},W_n^{(2)},\overline W_n^{(3)}\bigr)\\[1mm]
\text{converges to a unique deterministic continuous-time law-level evolution.}
\end{array}}
\]

The convergence is uniform on every fixed time interval. Symbolically, if \(d_{\mathrm{action}}\) denotes the appropriate law-level distance, then for every \(T<\infty\) and \(\varepsilon>0\),

\[
\mathbb P\!\left[
\sup_{0\le t\le T}
d_{\mathrm{action}}\!\left(
\bigl(z_n^{(1)}(t),W_n^{(2)}(t),\overline W_n^{(3)}(t)\bigr),
\bigl(z^{(1)}(t),W^{(2)}(t),\overline W^{(3)}(t)\bigr)
\right)>\varepsilon
\right]
\longrightarrow0.
\]

This is not ordinary convergence of three marginal distributions. The limiting state must retain how \(W^{(2)}\) acts in both directions:

\[
v\longmapsto W^{(2)}v,
\qquad
u\longmapsto \bigl(W^{(2)}\bigr)^*u.
\]

The reason is visible in backpropagation. The forward preactivation uses

\[
z^{(2)}=W^{(2)}\phi(z^{(1)}),
\]

while the first-layer update uses

\[
\bigl(W^{(2)}\bigr)^*\delta^{(2)}.
\]

Knowing only the distributions of \(z^{(1)}\) and \(z^{(2)}\) does not determine that backward quantity. The “action law” records exactly these forward and backward operator correlations.

The second goal is to show that the limiting dynamics are not degenerate. For some \(T_*>0\), the theorem proves

\[
\int_0^T
\mathbb E\left|
\frac{d}{dt}z^{(1)}(t)
\right|^2dt>0,
\]

and

\[
\int_0^T
\mathbb E\left|
\frac{d}{dt}z^{(2)}(t)
\right|^2dt>0
\]

for every \(0<T\le T_*\). Thus both hidden-layer preactivations genuinely move.

It also proves that the activation remains genuinely nonlinear at each layer:

\[
\inf_{a,b\in\mathbb R}
\mathbb E\left[
\left(
\phi\!\left(z^{(\ell)}(t)\right)
-a-bz^{(\ell)}(t)
\right)^2
\right]>0,
\qquad \ell=1,2.
\]

Finally, all three weight layers contribute positively to the tangent kernel, the total kernel changes with time, and

\[
L(T)<L(0)=1.
\]

So, in canonical neural-network language, the goal is:

\[
\boxed{
\text{derive the true infinite-width backpropagation dynamics and prove that
both hidden layers continue to perform nonlinear feature learning.}
}
\]

[59 | 2026-08-28T16:33:56.287Z | assistant | None]
Yes. The proof gives a precise criterion, but it is spread across the “current cyclic spaces” construction and the record metric \(d_{\mathrm{act}}\). I’m using the teaching workflow to translate that criterion into the canonical \(z^{(\ell)},W^{(\ell)}\) notation and point to the exact passages.

[75 | 2026-08-28T16:34:49.241Z | assistant | None]
Yes. It is written precisely in Sections 4.2 and 5.3. The criterion is not operator-norm convergence of the matrices \(W_n^{(2)}\). It is convergence of every finite observable generated by repeatedly applying \(W_n^{(2)}\) and \((W_n^{(2)})^*\).

Let

\[
\mathcal H_1=L^2(\Omega_1),\qquad
\mathcal H_2=L^2(\Omega_2)
\]

represent the first- and second-layer populations. In the limit,

\[
W^{(2)}:\mathcal H_1\to\mathcal H_2,
\qquad
(W^{(2)})^*:\mathcal H_2\to\mathcal H_1.
\]

A first-layer quantity is an element of \(\mathcal H_1\); a second-layer quantity is an element of \(\mathcal H_2\). They cannot be placed in the same coordinatewise joint distribution because their neuron indices belong to different populations.

Starting with \(z^{(1)}\) and the rescaled output weights \(\overline W^{(3)}\), construct probes recursively:

\[
v\in\mathcal H_1
\quad\Longrightarrow\quad
W^{(2)}v\in\mathcal H_2,
\]

\[
u\in\mathcal H_2
\quad\Longrightarrow\quad
(W^{(2)})^*u\in\mathcal H_1.
\]

At either layer, one may also apply the required coordinatewise functions, such as \(\phi,\phi'\), products occurring in backpropagation, and finite linear combinations.

For example, the following are successive probes:

\[
\phi(z^{(1)})\in\mathcal H_1,
\]

\[
z^{(2)}
=
W^{(2)}\phi(z^{(1)})
\in\mathcal H_2,
\]

\[
\delta^{(2)}
=
\overline W^{(3)}\phi'(z^{(2)})
\in\mathcal H_2,
\]

\[
(W^{(2)})^*\delta^{(2)}
\in\mathcal H_1,
\]

\[
W^{(2)}
\left[
\phi'(z^{(1)})(W^{(2)})^*\delta^{(2)}
\right]
\in\mathcal H_2.
\]

The construction allows every finite nesting of this kind.

For any finite list of first-layer probes \(v^1,\ldots,v^m\), the finite-width empirical joint law is

\[
\mu_{n,t}^{v^1,\ldots,v^m}
=
\frac1n\sum_{i=1}^n
\delta_{
\left(
v_{n,i}^1(t),\ldots,v_{n,i}^m(t)
\right)
}.
\]

The criterion requires

\[
\mu_{n,t}^{v^1,\ldots,v^m}
\longrightarrow
\mathcal L\bigl(v^1(t),\ldots,v^m(t)\bigr)
\]

in \(W_2(\mathbb R^m)\), uniformly in \(t\in[0,T]\), in probability.

There is an identical requirement for every finite list of second-layer probes. In addition, all same-layer inner products must converge:

\[
\frac1n\sum_{i=1}^n
v_{n,i}(t)\widetilde v_{n,i}(t)
\longrightarrow
\langle v(t),\widetilde v(t)\rangle_{\mathcal H_1},
\]

and

\[
\frac1n\sum_{j=1}^n
u_{n,j}(t)\widetilde u_{n,j}(t)
\longrightarrow
\langle u(t),\widetilde u(t)\rangle_{\mathcal H_2}.
\]

A finite collection of these joint laws and inner products is called a record. If \(d_R\) measures the difference between two states on record \(R\), the proof enumerates all finite records \(R_1,R_2,\ldots\) and defines

\[
d_{\mathrm{act}}
=
\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}
2^{-k}
\left(1\wedge d_{R_k}\right).
\]

Therefore,

\[
d_{\mathrm{act}}\to0
\]

means that every fixed finite nested forward/backward computation has the correct limiting joint law and inner products. This is defined explicitly in [equations (5.12)–(5.13)](/tmp/L2_FULL_AUDITED_PROOF.md:476).

The main convergence statement is

\[
\sup_{0\le t\le T}
d_{\mathrm{act}}\bigl(
\text{width-}n\text{ state at }t,
\text{limiting state at }t
\bigr)
\longrightarrow0
\]

in probability. This is proved at [equation (5.18)](/tmp/L2_FULL_AUDITED_PROOF.md:541).

There is also an equivalent basis-free criterion. Two limiting action states are considered identical when there are unitary maps

\[
U_1:\mathcal K_1\to\widetilde{\mathcal K}_1,
\qquad
U_2:\mathcal K_2\to\widetilde{\mathcal K}_2
\]

between their probe-generated subspaces such that

\[
U_2W^{(2)}
=
\widetilde W^{(2)}U_1,
\]

\[
U_1(W^{(2)})^*
=
(\widetilde W^{(2)})^*U_2,
\]

and the unitaries carry all root variables and coordinatewise functions to their counterparts. This appears in [lines 311–338](/tmp/L2_FULL_AUDITED_PROOF.md:311).

So “retain the action of \(W^{(2)}\) in both directions” means:

\[
\boxed{
\text{retain the laws and inner products of every finite computation built
using }W^{(2)},(W^{(2)})^*,\phi,\phi',\text{ and the current variables}.
}
\]

It does not retain irrelevant parts of the operator. The proof explicitly discards the action of \(W^{(2)}\) outside the probe-generated subspaces, because those directions can never influence the future dynamics.

can you retate everything for someone who doesn't know and udnerstand functional space and analysis nor hilbert spaces, etc, but still you have to explain the core mathematics and deep substantive things


[90 | 2026-08-28T16:41:24.390Z | assistant | None]
I’m rebuilding the action-law explanation from finite vectors and ordinary matrix multiplication only. The functional-analysis language will appear only as a final translation; the actual criterion can be understood through concrete “computational recipes,” empirical averages, and matrix transposes.

[98 | 2026-08-28T16:42:10.090Z | assistant | None]
At every finite width, there are only vectors, matrices, sums, and ordinary probability distributions. The functional-analysis language is packaging for what happens when the vector length \(n\) tends to infinity.

Let

\[
W=W^{(2)}\in\mathbb R^{n\times n}
\]

be the weights between the two hidden layers. Forward propagation uses

\[
z^{(2)}=W\phi(z^{(1)}).
\]

Backpropagation first forms

\[
\delta^{(2)}
=
\overline W^{(3)}\odot\phi'(z^{(2)}),
\]

where \(\odot\) means coordinatewise multiplication, and then sends it backward through

\[
W^\top\delta^{(2)}.
\]

The first-layer velocity is

\[
\frac{d}{dt}z^{(1)}
=
-2r\,
\phi'(z^{(1)})\odot
W^\top\delta^{(2)},
\]

where \(r=f-1\) is the output error.

So training queries the same matrix in two directions:

\[
\text{forward: }v\mapsto Wv,
\qquad
\text{backward: }u\mapsto W^\top u.
\]

## Why marginal distributions are insufficient

Suppose

\[
a=\phi(z^{(1)})=
\begin{pmatrix}1\\0\end{pmatrix},
\qquad
d=\delta^{(2)}=
\begin{pmatrix}1\\0\end{pmatrix}.
\]

Consider

\[
W_{\mathrm I}
=
\begin{pmatrix}
1&0\\
0&0
\end{pmatrix},
\qquad
W_{\mathrm{II}}
=
\begin{pmatrix}
1&1\\
0&0
\end{pmatrix}.
\]

Their forward actions agree:

\[
W_{\mathrm I}a
=
W_{\mathrm{II}}a
=
\begin{pmatrix}1\\0\end{pmatrix}.
\]

Therefore both networks can have exactly the same current first-layer activations and exactly the same second-layer preactivations.

But their backward actions differ:

\[
W_{\mathrm I}^{\top}d
=
\begin{pmatrix}1\\0\end{pmatrix},
\qquad
W_{\mathrm{II}}^{\top}d
=
\begin{pmatrix}1\\1\end{pmatrix}.
\]

Hence their next first-layer velocities differ.

This proves the substantive point:

\[
\boxed{
\text{The current distributions of }z^{(1)}\text{ and }z^{(2)}
\text{ do not determine the future.}
}
\]

The state must also retain enough information about how \(W\) and \(W^\top\) act on the vectors produced during training.

## What “retain the action” means

Think of a probe as a finite computational recipe. A probe may perform operations such as:

1. Start with \(z^{(1)}\) or \(\overline W^{(3)}\).
2. Apply \(\phi\) or \(\phi'\) coordinatewise.
3. Add or multiply vectors coordinatewise.
4. Send a first-layer vector forward using \(W\).
5. Send a second-layer vector backward using \(W^\top\).
6. Repeat these operations a fixed finite number of times.
7. Measure empirical distributions or normalized dot products.

For example:

\[
v_0=\phi(z^{(1)}),
\]

\[
u_0=Wv_0=z^{(2)},
\]

\[
u_1=\overline W^{(3)}\odot\phi'(u_0)
=\delta^{(2)},
\]

\[
v_1=W^\top u_1,
\]

\[
v_2=\phi'(z^{(1)})\odot v_1,
\]

\[
u_2=Wv_2.
\]

Here \(v_0,v_1,v_2\) are first-layer vectors, while \(u_0,u_1,u_2\) are second-layer vectors. A probe can continue nesting these operations, but every individual probe must contain only finitely many steps.

## The exact convergence criterion

Take any fixed finite collection of first-layer probes

\[
v_n^1(t),\ldots,v_n^m(t)\in\mathbb R^n.
\]

Their empirical joint distribution is the distribution obtained by selecting a neuron index \(i\) uniformly from \(\{1,\ldots,n\}\) and observing

\[
\bigl(v_{n,i}^1(t),\ldots,v_{n,i}^m(t)\bigr).
\]

Written as a probability measure, it is

\[
\frac1n\sum_{i=1}^n
\delta_{\left(v_{n,i}^1(t),\ldots,v_{n,i}^m(t)\right)}.
\]

Convergence means that for every reasonable continuous test function \(\psi\),

\[
\frac1n\sum_{i=1}^n
\psi\!\left(
v_{n,i}^1(t),\ldots,v_{n,i}^m(t)
\right)
\longrightarrow
\mathbb E\,
\psi\!\left(V^1(t),\ldots,V^m(t)\right).
\]

The limiting random variables \(V^1,\ldots,V^m\) describe the limiting population statistics of those probes.

Second moments must also converge:

\[
\frac1n\sum_{i=1}^n
|v_{n,i}^k(t)|^2
\longrightarrow
\mathbb E|V^k(t)|^2.
\]

This prevents a small number of extremely large coordinates from being invisible to the limiting distribution. Weak distributional convergence together with this second-moment control is what the proof calls \(W_2\) convergence.

The identical condition is imposed on every finite collection of second-layer probes.

The proof also explicitly tracks normalized dot products:

\[
\frac1n\sum_{i=1}^n
v_{n,i}(t)\widetilde v_{n,i}(t)
\longrightarrow
\mathbb E\!\left[
V(t)\widetilde V(t)
\right],
\]

and, for second-layer probes,

\[
\frac1n\sum_{j=1}^n
u_{n,j}(t)\widetilde u_{n,j}(t)
\longrightarrow
\mathbb E\!\left[
U(t)\widetilde U(t)
\right].
\]

These dot products record angles, correlations, and squared sizes. They are essential because the rank-one weight update contains precisely such quantities after repeated time steps.

The convergence must hold uniformly over time. For every fixed probe statistic \(Q_n(t)\), limiting statistic \(Q(t)\), finite \(T\), and \(\varepsilon>0\),

\[
\mathbb P\left(
\sup_{0\le t\le T}
|Q_n(t)-Q(t)|>\varepsilon
\right)
\longrightarrow0.
\]

## Packaging all probes into one criterion

There are infinitely many possible finite recipes. The proof lists them as

\[
R_1,R_2,R_3,\ldots
\]

and lets \(\operatorname{error}(R_k)\) measure the disagreement on recipe \(R_k\). It then defines

\[
d_{\mathrm{action}}
=
\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}
2^{-k}
\min\left\{1,\operatorname{error}(R_k)\right\}.
\]

The weights \(2^{-k}\) make the infinite sum finite. Mathematically,

\[
d_{\mathrm{action}}\to0
\]

is equivalent to saying:

> For every fixed finite recipe, its empirical joint laws, second moments, and dot products converge to the corresponding limiting quantities.

The theorem proves

\[
\sup_{0\le t\le T}
d_{\mathrm{action}}
\longrightarrow0
\quad\text{in probability}.
\]

This is the precise meaning of convergence of the matrix action.

## One additional boundedness requirement

Finite probes alone could miss a direction on which \(W\) becomes enormous. The proof therefore also requires a uniform amplification bound:

\[
\|Wv\|_n\le M_T\|v\|_n
\]

for every vector \(v\), with high probability, uniformly over width and \(0\le t\le T\). Here

\[
\|v\|_n^2=\frac1n\sum_i v_i^2.
\]

Equivalently,

\[
\|W\|_{\mathrm{op}}
=
\sup_{v\ne0}
\frac{\|Wv\|_n}{\|v\|_n}
\le M_T.
\]

This rules out uncontrolled amplification hiding outside the finitely inspected probes.

## Why this determines the future

Every term in the training equations is itself one of these finite recipes:

\[
z^{(2)}=W\phi(z^{(1)}),
\]

\[
\delta^{(2)}
=
\overline W^{(3)}\odot\phi'(z^{(2)}),
\]

\[
\frac{d}{dt}z^{(1)}
=
-2r\,\phi'(z^{(1)})\odot W^\top\delta^{(2)},
\]

\[
\frac{d}{dt}W
=
-\frac{2r}{n}\,
\delta^{(2)}\phi(z^{(1)})^\top.
\]

Therefore, if two limiting states give the same answer to every finite probe, they give the same current velocities. After a short time, their new states again give the same probe answers. Repeating this argument determines the whole future.

The limit does not retain every imaginable action of \(W\). It retains only its action on vectors reachable from the current neural-network variables through forward propagation, backpropagation, coordinatewise nonlinearities, and their finite repetitions. Any direction that can never be reached by these operations can never affect training and is discarded.

This is what the proof calls the current cyclic space. The “Hilbert spaces” are simply a rigorous home for all limiting probe vectors, their dot products, and the two operations corresponding to \(W\) and \(W^\top\).

The formal definition is in [Section 5.3](/tmp/L2_FULL_AUDITED_PROOF.md:464), especially equations (5.12)–(5.13). Uniform convergence is equation [(5.18)](/tmp/L2_FULL_AUDITED_PROOF.md:541), and the statement that only future-reachable directions are retained is in [Section 4.2](/tmp/L2_FULL_AUDITED_PROOF.md:311).

Yes—my wording was incomplete. The condition is required separately for both first-layer probes \(v\) and second-layer probes \(u\). I’ll rewrite the criterion with \(W^{(2)}\) kept everywhere and make explicit why there is no naive mixed \(i\)-with-\(j\) empirical law.

[115 | 2026-08-28T16:49:27.864Z | assistant | None]
My displayed equation showed only the first-layer condition. The complete criterion requires two separate families of convergence statements.

Let

\[
v_n^1(t),\ldots,v_n^m(t)\in\mathbb R^n
\]

be first-layer probes, indexed by first-layer neurons \(i\). Let

\[
u_n^1(t),\ldots,u_n^r(t)\in\mathbb R^n
\]

be second-layer probes, indexed by second-layer neurons \(j\).

They are connected by

\[
v_n\longmapsto W_n^{(2)}v_n,
\]

which produces a second-layer probe, and

\[
u_n\longmapsto \bigl(W_n^{(2)}\bigr)^\top u_n,
\]

which produces a first-layer probe.

For every finite list of first-layer probes and every bounded continuous test function \(\psi:\mathbb R^m\to\mathbb R\), the requirement is

\[
\frac1n\sum_{i=1}^n
\psi\!\left(
v_{n,i}^1(t),\ldots,v_{n,i}^m(t)
\right)
\longrightarrow
\mathbb E\,
\psi\!\left(
V^1(t),\ldots,V^m(t)
\right).
\]

Separately, for every finite list of second-layer probes and every bounded continuous \(\chi:\mathbb R^r\to\mathbb R\),

\[
\frac1n\sum_{j=1}^n
\chi\!\left(
u_{n,j}^1(t),\ldots,u_{n,j}^r(t)
\right)
\longrightarrow
\mathbb E\,
\chi\!\left(
U^1(t),\ldots,U^r(t)
\right).
\]

Both statements hold uniformly for \(0\le t\le T\), in probability. To obtain \(W_2\), rather than merely weak convergence, the corresponding second moments must also converge:

\[
\frac1n\sum_{i=1}^n\sum_{a=1}^m
|v_{n,i}^a(t)|^2
\longrightarrow
\mathbb E\sum_{a=1}^m|V^a(t)|^2,
\]

and

\[
\frac1n\sum_{j=1}^n\sum_{b=1}^r
|u_{n,j}^b(t)|^2
\longrightarrow
\mathbb E\sum_{b=1}^r|U^b(t)|^2.
\]

Same-layer inner products are also recorded:

\[
\frac1n\sum_{i=1}^n
v_{n,i}\widetilde v_{n,i}
\longrightarrow
\mathbb E[V\widetilde V],
\]

\[
\frac1n\sum_{j=1}^n
u_{n,j}\widetilde u_{n,j}
\longrightarrow
\mathbb E[U\widetilde U].
\]

There is generally no empirical joint law of the form

\[
\frac1n\sum_{i=1}^n\delta_{(v_{n,i},u_{n,i})}.
\]

Although both layers happen to have width \(n\), index \(i\) in the first layer and index \(j\) in the second layer label different neuron populations. Pairing first-layer neuron \(i\) with second-layer neuron \(i\) would be arbitrary and would change under separate neuron relabelings.

Instead, cross-layer dependence is recorded through \(W_n^{(2)}\). For example, these are legitimate same-layer joint laws:

\[
\frac1n\sum_{j=1}^n
\delta_{\left(u_{n,j},\,(W_n^{(2)}v_n)_j\right)}
\]

on the second layer, and

\[
\frac1n\sum_{i=1}^n
\delta_{\left(v_{n,i},\,((W_n^{(2)})^\top u_n)_i\right)}
\]

on the first layer.

Cross-layer scalar interactions are likewise captured by the adjoint identity

\[
\frac1n
u_n^\top W_n^{(2)}v_n
=
\frac1n
\left((W_n^{(2)})^\top u_n\right)^\top v_n.
\]

The left side is a second-layer inner product; the right side is the identical quantity expressed as a first-layer inner product.

Thus the complete criterion is

\[
\boxed{
\begin{array}{l}
\text{joint-law and second-moment convergence for every finite list of}\\
\text{first-layer probes }v;\\[1mm]
\text{the same convergence for every finite list of second-layer probes }u;\\[1mm]
\text{with the two families connected by }W^{(2)}\text{ and }(W^{(2)})^\top.
\end{array}}
\]

The proof states this as “same-side probe tuples” in [lines 484–487](/tmp/L2_FULL_AUDITED_PROOF.md:484). “Same-side” means separately within the first-layer and second-layer populations—not only the \(v\)-side.

[141 | 2026-08-28T16:52:01.803Z | assistant | None]
We restart with one question:

> As both hidden layers become infinitely wide, what does the entire exact GD training process converge to, and does that limit still perform genuine nonlinear feature learning?

## 1. The finite-width system

There is one input \(x=1\) and one target \(y=1\). Both hidden layers have width \(n\). The activation is

\[
\phi(s)=\arctan(s).
\]

Because \(x=1\), the first-layer preactivation equals its weight:

\[
z_{n,i}^{(1)}=W_{n,i}^{(1)},\qquad 1\le i\le n.
\]

The second-layer preactivation is

\[
z_{n,j}^{(2)}
=
\sum_{i=1}^n
W_{n,ji}^{(2)}
\phi\!\left(z_{n,i}^{(1)}\right),
\qquad 1\le j\le n.
\]

The scalar output is

\[
f_n
=
\sum_{j=1}^n
W_{n,j}^{(3)}
\phi\!\left(z_{n,j}^{(2)}\right),
\]

and the loss is

\[
L_n=(f_n-1)^2.
\]

The independent initialization is

\[
W_{n,i}^{(1)}(0)\sim N(0,1),
\]

\[
W_{n,ji}^{(2)}(0)\sim N\!\left(0,\frac1n\right),
\]

\[
W_{n,j}^{(3)}(0)\sim N\!\left(0,\frac1{n^4}\right).
\]

Thus \(z^{(1)}\) and \(z^{(2)}\) are order-one quantities, while the initial output tends to zero:

\[
f_n(0)\longrightarrow0,
\qquad
L_n(0)\longrightarrow1.
\]

Gradient descent uses

\[
W_n^{(\ell)}(k+1)
=
W_n^{(\ell)}(k)
-
\eta_n\lambda_{\ell,n}
\nabla_{W_n^{(\ell)}}L_n(k),
\]

where

\[
\eta_n=n^{-2}
\]

and

\[
\lambda_{1,n}=n,\qquad
\lambda_{2,n}=1,\qquad
\lambda_{3,n}=n^{-1}.
\]

Macroscopic time is

\[
t=k\eta_n=\frac{k}{n^2}.
\]

Therefore, reaching a fixed time \(t\) requires approximately \(tn^2\) GD iterations. The theorem takes the width limit and the small-step limit together.

It is useful to rescale only the output weights:

\[
\overline W_{n,j}^{(3)}
=
nW_{n,j}^{(3)}.
\]

Then

\[
f_n
=
\frac1n\sum_{j=1}^n
\overline W_{n,j}^{(3)}
\phi\!\left(z_{n,j}^{(2)}\right).
\]

## 2. The primary objective: identify the entire limiting dynamics

The main objective is to construct a unique deterministic law-level evolution

\[
\left(
z^{(1)}(t),
W^{(2)}(t),
\overline W^{(3)}(t)
\right),
\qquad t\ge0,
\]

that faithfully describes exact finite-width GD as \(n\to\infty\).

Here “deterministic law-level evolution” means that the limiting population distributions and correlations are deterministic. A representative neuron remains a random variable because different neurons have different initial labels, but the distribution of those labels and their evolution no longer depend on the particular finite-width random sample.

The desired convergence is

\[
\boxed{
\text{exact discrete GD at width }n
\longrightarrow
\text{one unique continuous-time population evolution}.
}
\]

This must hold:

- along the full sequence \(n\to\infty\), not merely a subsequence;
- in probability over the random initialization;
- uniformly for \(0\le t\le T\), for every fixed \(T<\infty\);
- for the hidden representations and matrix-mediated backpropagation, not only for \(f_n(t)\).

## 3. What convergence of the complete state means

The matrix \(W_n^{(2)}\) changes dimension with \(n\), so its entries cannot converge to the entries of one fixed matrix. Instead, the theorem tests every fixed finite computation that training can perform with \(W_n^{(2)}\).

A first-layer probe is a vector indexed by first-layer neurons. Denote such probes by

\[
v_n^1,\ldots,v_n^m\in\mathbb R^n.
\]

A second-layer probe is a vector indexed by second-layer neurons. Denote these by

\[
u_n^1,\ldots,u_n^r\in\mathbb R^n.
\]

Starting with \(z_n^{(1)}\) and \(\overline W_n^{(3)}\), probes are generated using:

\[
\phi,\quad \phi',\quad
\text{coordinatewise arithmetic},
\]

\[
v_n\longmapsto W_n^{(2)}v_n,
\]

and

\[
u_n\longmapsto
\bigl(W_n^{(2)}\bigr)^\top u_n.
\]

For example, training uses the sequence

\[
v_n^0=\phi(z_n^{(1)}),
\]

\[
u_n^0=W_n^{(2)}v_n^0=z_n^{(2)},
\]

\[
u_n^1
=
\overline W_n^{(3)}
\odot\phi'(z_n^{(2)}),
\]

\[
v_n^1
=
\bigl(W_n^{(2)}\bigr)^\top u_n^1.
\]

Here \(\odot\) denotes coordinatewise multiplication.

For every fixed finite list of first-layer probes, the theorem requires

\[
\sup_{0\le t\le T}
\left|
\frac1n\sum_{i=1}^n
\psi\!\left(
v_{n,i}^1(t),\ldots,v_{n,i}^m(t)
\right)
-
\mathbb E\,
\psi\!\left(
V^1(t),\ldots,V^m(t)
\right)
\right|
\longrightarrow0
\]

in probability, for every bounded continuous test function \(\psi\). Second moments must converge as well.

Separately, for every fixed finite list of second-layer probes,

\[
\sup_{0\le t\le T}
\left|
\frac1n\sum_{j=1}^n
\chi\!\left(
u_{n,j}^1(t),\ldots,u_{n,j}^r(t)
\right)
-
\mathbb E\,
\chi\!\left(
U^1(t),\ldots,U^r(t)
\right)
\right|
\longrightarrow0
\]

in probability, again together with second moments.

All normalized same-layer inner products must also converge:

\[
\frac1n\sum_{i=1}^n
v_{n,i}(t)\widetilde v_{n,i}(t)
\longrightarrow
\mathbb E[V(t)\widetilde V(t)],
\]

\[
\frac1n\sum_{j=1}^n
u_{n,j}(t)\widetilde u_{n,j}(t)
\longrightarrow
\mathbb E[U(t)\widetilde U(t)].
\]

Finally, the matrix action must remain uniformly bounded:

\[
\|W_n^{(2)}(t)v\|_n
\le M_T\|v\|_n
\]

for all \(v\), uniformly over \(0\le t\le T\), with probability tending to one.

Together, these conditions mean:

\[
\boxed{
\text{Every fixed finite forward/backward computation performed by training
has a deterministic limit.}
}
\]

This is the document’s precise “action-law convergence.”

## 4. The closure objective

Convergence alone is insufficient. The limiting information must determine its own future.

The current limiting state contains:

- the first-layer population data;
- the second-layer population data;
- how \(W^{(2)}\) sends every training-relevant first-layer probe forward;
- how \((W^{(2)})^\top\) sends every training-relevant second-layer probe backward;
- the joint laws and inner products of all these quantities.

The training equations use only these operations. Therefore the current action law determines the current velocity. Uniqueness then determines the entire future.

If \(\Phi_t\) denotes evolution for time \(t\), restartability means

\[
\Phi_t\!\left(\Phi_s(\text{current action law})\right)
=
\Phi_{t+s}(\text{current action law}).
\]

No initialization array or previous trajectory must be retained as hidden memory.

## 5. The observable convergence objective

The theorem also requires convergence of complete neuron trajectories, not just distributions at one time.

For the first hidden layer, define

\[
\Lambda_n^{(1)}
=
\frac1n\sum_{i=1}^n
\delta_{z_{n,i}^{(1)}(\cdot)}.
\]

This is the empirical distribution of the entire paths

\[
t\longmapsto z_{n,i}^{(1)}(t).
\]

Similarly,

\[
\Lambda_n^{(2)}
=
\frac1n\sum_{j=1}^n
\delta_{z_{n,j}^{(2)}(\cdot)}.
\]

The theorem proves

\[
\Lambda_n^{(1)}
\longrightarrow
\mathcal L\!\left(z^{(1)}(\cdot)\right),
\]

\[
\Lambda_n^{(2)}
\longrightarrow
\mathcal L\!\left(z^{(2)}(\cdot)\right)
\]

in path-space \(W_2\). Concretely, this means convergence of the path distributions together with convergence of their mean squared maximum sizes. It retains temporal correlations: how a neuron’s value at one time relates to its value later.

The output and loss also converge uniformly:

\[
\sup_{0\le t\le T}|f_n(t)-f(t)|
\longrightarrow0,
\]

\[
\sup_{0\le t\le T}|L_n(t)-L(t)|
\longrightarrow0
\]

in probability.

## 6. The nondegeneracy objective

A mathematically existing limit could still be uninteresting. For example, only \(W^{(3)}\) might learn while both hidden layers remain frozen.

The theorem must therefore prove that both hidden representations move. For sufficiently small \(T>0\),

\[
\int_0^T
\mathbb E
\left|
\frac{d}{dt}z^{(1)}(t)
\right|^2dt
>0,
\]

\[
\int_0^T
\mathbb E
\left|
\frac{d}{dt}z^{(2)}(t)
\right|^2dt
>0.
\]

It also proves actual nonzero displacement:

\[
\mathbb E
\left|
z^{(1)}(T)-z^{(1)}(0)
\right|^2>0,
\]

\[
\mathbb E
\left|
z^{(2)}(T)-z^{(2)}(0)
\right|^2>0.
\]

Velocity energy rules out “it moves only at an isolated instant.” Displacement rules out “it moves but returns exactly to where it started.”

## 7. The kernel criterion for all three layers

Define the scaled contribution of layer \(\ell\) to the tangent kernel by

\[
K_n^{(\ell)}
=
\lambda_{\ell,n}
\sum_{\alpha\in W_n^{(\ell)}}
\left(
\frac{\partial f_n}
{\partial W_{n,\alpha}^{(\ell)}}
\right)^2.
\]

The total scaled kernel is

\[
K_n
=
K_n^{(1)}+K_n^{(2)}+K_n^{(3)}.
\]

For the continuous gradient flow,

\[
\frac{d}{dt}f_n
=
-2(f_n-1)K_n
\]

and therefore

\[
\frac{d}{dt}L_n
=
-4(f_n-1)^2K_n.
\]

Thus \(K_n^{(\ell)}\) measures how strongly layer \(\ell\) contributes to the instantaneous decrease of the loss.

The theorem proves convergence of all three blocks and, for sufficiently small \(T>0\),

\[
\int_0^T K^{(\ell)}(t)\,dt>0,
\qquad \ell=1,2,3.
\]

It also proves that

\[
K(t)
\]

is not constant. This distinguishes the limit from a frozen tangent-kernel regime.

## 8. The genuine-nonlinearity criterion

Movement alone does not guarantee nonlinear feature learning. The hidden variables could move only inside a regime where \(\phi\) behaves effectively like an affine function.

For each hidden layer \(\ell=1,2\), define

\[
\mathcal N^{(\ell)}(t)
=
\inf_{a,b\in\mathbb R}
\mathbb E
\left[
\left(
\phi(z^{(\ell)}(t))
-a-bz^{(\ell)}(t)
\right)^2
\right].
\]

This is the smallest mean-square error obtained by approximating the activation with an affine function of the preactivation.

The theorem proves, for sufficiently small time,

\[
\mathcal N^{(1)}(t)>0,
\qquad
\mathcal N^{(2)}(t)>0.
\]

Therefore neither hidden activation has collapsed to an affine transformation under its evolving population distribution.

The main objective is consequently the conjunction of three claims:

\[
\boxed{
\begin{array}{ll}
\textbf{Fidelity:}&
\text{exact finite-width GD converges to the proposed law-level dynamics};\\[1mm]
\textbf{Closure:}&
\text{the current limiting action law uniquely determines its future};\\[1mm]
\textbf{Nondegeneracy:}&
\text{both hidden layers move, all layers affect learning, and }\phi
\text{ remains nonlinear}.
\end{array}
}
\]

This is not a theorem about eventual global optimization. It does not prove \(L(t)\to0\) as \(t\to\infty\). It is a rigorous finite-time theorem identifying the true infinite-width training dynamics and proving that those dynamics are genuinely nonlinear and feature-learning.

[131 | 2026-08-30T11:10:39.366Z | assistant | None]
Your high-level picture is essentially right, with two important corrections:

1. The limiting system is autonomous but not finite-dimensional. It has a finite-looking set of evolution equations, but its state contains whole population laws and the action of \(W^{(2)}\) on infinitely many possible probes.

2. The theorem proves a prescribed diagonal limit with \(\eta_n=n^{-2}\), not an unrestricted two-parameter limit over every way that \(n\to\infty\) and \(\eta\to0\).

## 1. The true target

The target can be summarized as

\[
\boxed{
\text{random finite-width discrete GD}
\quad\longrightarrow\quad
\text{deterministic autonomous population gradient flow}.
}
\]

The learning-rate limit removes discrete time:

\[
k\mapsto k+1
\quad\rightsquigarrow\quad
t\mapsto \frac{d}{dt}.
\]

The width limit does not make “width continuous.” It replaces \(n\) individual neurons and an \(n\times n\) matrix by population-level random variables and an operator describing the limiting action of \(W^{(2)}\).

In canonical notation, the candidate limiting equations are

\[
z^{(2)}=W^{(2)}\phi(z^{(1)}),
\]

\[
f=\mathbb E\!\left[
\overline W^{(3)}\phi(z^{(2)})
\right],
\qquad
r=f-1,
\]

\[
\delta^{(2)}
=
\overline W^{(3)}\phi'(z^{(2)}),
\]

\[
\dot z^{(1)}
=
-2r\,\phi'(z^{(1)})
\odot
\bigl(W^{(2)}\bigr)^*\delta^{(2)},
\]

\[
\dot W^{(2)}
=
-2r\,
\delta^{(2)}
\otimes
\phi(z^{(1)}),
\]

\[
\dot{\overline W}^{(3)}
=
-2r\,\phi(z^{(2)}).
\]

Here \((W^{(2)})^*\) is the infinite-population counterpart of \((W_n^{(2)})^\top\), and

\[
\left(
\delta^{(2)}\otimes\phi(z^{(1)})
\right)v
=
\delta^{(2)}
\mathbb E\!\left[\phi(z^{(1)})v\right].
\]

These are finitely many equations, but their variables are not finitely many real numbers. The accurate description is

\[
\boxed{
\text{an autonomous infinite-dimensional action-law flow with a finite-form vector field}.
}
\]

## 2. Why observable convergence is insufficient

Suppose one only proves

\[
L_n(t)\longrightarrow L(t).
\]

That says nothing about whether \(L(t)\) is generated by a closed limiting neural-network evolution. Many different hidden states can have the same loss while producing different next derivatives.

Likewise, even convergence of the marginal laws of \(z_n^{(1)}\) and \(z_n^{(2)}\) is insufficient. The next first-layer velocity requires

\[
\bigl(W^{(2)}\bigr)^*\delta^{(2)},
\]

which is not determined by those two marginal laws.

The real structural statement must therefore be:

1. The complete finite action state converges.
2. The limiting state satisfies the proposed differential equation.
3. That differential equation has a unique solution.
4. Its right-hand side depends only on the complete current state.

The decisive mathematical statement is an integral equation

\[
\text{state}(t)
=
\text{state}(0)
+
\int_0^t
\text{vector field}\bigl(\text{state}(s)\bigr)\,ds,
\]

together with uniqueness.

That is what distinguishes an actual dynamical limit from a time-indexed family of fitted distributions.

“Markovian” here means Markovian with respect to the complete action law. It does not mean that \(L(t)\), \(f(t)\), or the marginal laws of \(z^{(1)}(t)\) and \(z^{(2)}(t)\) are themselves closed Markov states.

## 3. The technical obligations

To prove such a limit, the proof must discharge the following logically separate obligations.

### A. Derive the candidate dynamics exactly

The limiting equations cannot be guessed from simulations or loss curves. One must differentiate the finite-width loss, insert the width-dependent learning-rate multipliers, and identify the properly scaled variables.

The proof’s change of first-layer coordinate is also exact, not a linearization. Its purpose is to obtain estimates uniform in width.

### B. Specify the limiting state and convergence topology

Because \(W_n^{(2)}\) changes dimension with \(n\), entrywise convergence is meaningless.

Instead, the state records the limiting laws and normalized inner products of every fixed finite computation generated using

\[
W_n^{(2)},\qquad
(W_n^{(2)})^\top,\qquad
\phi,\qquad
\phi',
\]

and coordinatewise arithmetic.

A uniform bound on the amplification produced by \(W_n^{(2)}\) is also required. Otherwise finite probes might converge while the matrix explodes in an untested direction.

### C. Construct the limiting initialization correctly

The same random matrix \(W_n^{(2)}(0)\) is used in forward propagation and, through its transpose, in backpropagation.

Therefore

\[
(W_n^{(2)}(0))^\top
u\!\left(
W_n^{(2)}(0)v
\right)
\]

cannot be treated as multiplication by a fresh independent Gaussian matrix. The limiting initialization must retain the response correlation caused by reusing the same matrix.

This is the point where the reused-Gaussian tensor-program theorem enters.

### D. Obtain width-uniform bounds

One needs bounds, uniform in \(n\), on finite time intervals for:

- the amplification norm of \(W_n^{(2)}(t)\);
- the rescaled output weights;
- the first-layer state;
- the vector field and its Lipschitz constant.

Without these estimates, a one-step error of size \(o(1)\) could be amplified by a factor depending on \(n\), destroying convergence.

These bounds provide global finite-width gradient-flow existence and an Euler approximation error

\[
\sup_{t\le T}
\operatorname{dist}
\left(
\mathrm{Euler}_{n,\delta}(t),
\mathrm{GF}_n(t)
\right)
\le C_T\delta,
\]

where \(C_T\) does not depend on \(n\).

### E. Prove the continuum equation is genuinely well posed

Writing down the limiting equations is not enough. One must prove:

- every term is defined;
- local solutions exist;
- the solution is unique;
- solutions depend continuously on the current state;
- no finite-time blow-up occurs.

Then one must prove that two different mathematical representations of the same action law produce the same future action law.

This yields the restart identity

\[
\Phi_t\!\left(\Phi_s(\text{state})\right)
=
\Phi_{t+s}(\text{state}).
\]

This is the precise autonomy/Markov property.

### F. Prove fidelity to the finite systems

One must show the actual finite systems converge to that unique solution in the full action-state topology, not merely through selected observables.

This is where the fixed-mesh population oracle, the tensor theorem, stability, and Gronwall estimates are used.

### G. Transfer convergence to the claimed observables

Even full action-state convergence does not automatically handle every unbounded quantity.

The proof separately establishes:

- path-law convergence using time regularity and second moments;
- kernel and velocity convergence using truncation and tail control;
- output and loss convergence;
- short-time positivity proving genuine feature motion.

Thus the logic is

\[
\boxed{
\text{state convergence}
+
\text{closed unique limit equation}
\Longrightarrow
\text{faithful autonomous limit},
}
\]

followed by separate arguments for the stronger observables.

## 4. How this proof actually performs the limit

Introduce the following objects:

- \(\mathrm{GD}_{n,\eta_n}\): actual width-\(n\) discrete GD;
- \(\mathrm{GF}_n\): width-\(n\) continuous gradient flow;
- \(\mathrm{Euler}_{n,\delta}\): Euler discretization of \(\mathrm{GF}_n\) with auxiliary mesh \(\delta\);
- \(\mathrm{Euler}_{\infty,\delta}\): the limiting population Euler recursion;
- \(\mathrm{GF}_\infty\): the final autonomous action-law flow.

The proof follows the chain

\[
\boxed{
\mathrm{GD}_{n,\eta_n}
\approx
\mathrm{GF}_n
\approx
\mathrm{Euler}_{n,\delta}
\longrightarrow
\mathrm{Euler}_{\infty,\delta}
\approx
\mathrm{GF}_\infty.
}
\]

There are two distinct small time steps here:

\[
\eta_n=n^{-2}
\]

is the actual GD learning-rate step, while

\[
\delta>0
\]

is an auxiliary proof mesh. They must not be identified.

For fixed \(\delta\), the number of Euler steps on \([0,T]\) is

\[
N=\left\lceil\frac{T}{\delta}\right\rceil,
\]

which is finite and independent of \(n\). Therefore the tensor-program theorem needs to analyze only a fixed finite computation.

At this fixed mesh, the proof introduces a population oracle:

\[
\mathrm{Euler}_{n,\delta}
\approx
\mathrm{Oracle}_{n,\delta}
\longrightarrow
\mathrm{Euler}_{\infty,\delta}.
\]

The oracle replaces empirical scalar feedback by its deterministic population counterpart. Explicit Gram errors measure the difference, and discrete Gronwall shows those errors do not grow uncontrollably.

After taking \(n\to\infty\) at fixed \(\delta\), the proof sends \(\delta\downarrow0\). Schematically,

\[
\begin{aligned}
\operatorname{dist}
\left(
\mathrm{GF}_n,\mathrm{GF}_\infty
\right)
\le {}&
\operatorname{dist}
\left(
\mathrm{GF}_n,\mathrm{Euler}_{n,\delta}
\right)\\
&+
\operatorname{dist}
\left(
\mathrm{Euler}_{n,\delta},
\mathrm{Euler}_{\infty,\delta}
\right)\\
&+
\operatorname{dist}
\left(
\mathrm{Euler}_{\infty,\delta},
\mathrm{GF}_\infty
\right).
\end{aligned}
\]

The first and third terms are at most \(C_T\delta\). The middle term tends to zero as \(n\to\infty\) with \(\delta\) fixed.

The quantifier order is therefore:

1. Given an error tolerance \(\varepsilon\), choose \(\delta\) small.
2. With that \(\delta\) fixed, choose \(n\) large.
3. Conclude that the total error is below \(\varepsilon\).

Finally, exact GD is compared with finite-width gradient flow. The proof obtains

\[
\sup_{t\le T}
\operatorname{dist}
\left(
\mathrm{GD}_{n,\eta}(t),
\mathrm{GF}_n(t)
\right)
\le
C_T
\left(
\eta+\eta\sqrt n+\eta^2n
\right).
\]

For

\[
\eta=\eta_n=n^{-2},
\]

the right side tends to zero. This is the final bridge from the auxiliary continuous-time proof back to the actual discrete algorithm. See [the exact-GD estimate](/tmp/L2_FULL_AUDITED_PROOF.md:686) and the [fixed-mesh limit](/tmp/L2_FULL_AUDITED_PROOF.md:541).

## 5. What are the two limits?

To discuss limit order precisely, imagine defining

\[
\mathrm{GD}_{n,\eta}
\]

for width \(n\) and an independently chosen base step \(\eta>0\).

### Learning rate first, then width

The expression

\[
\lim_{n\to\infty}
\left(
\lim_{\eta\downarrow0}
\mathrm{GD}_{n,\eta}
\right)
\]

means:

1. Fix a finite width \(n\).
2. Send \(\eta\downarrow0\), obtaining the finite-width ODE \(\mathrm{GF}_n\).
3. Then send \(n\to\infty\).

This order is well defined here:

\[
\lim_{\eta\downarrow0}\mathrm{GD}_{n,\eta}
=
\mathrm{GF}_n,
\]

and the document proves

\[
\mathrm{GF}_n
\longrightarrow
\mathrm{GF}_\infty.
\]

Thus the document supplies

\[
\boxed{
\lim_{n\to\infty}
\left(
\lim_{\eta\downarrow0}
\mathrm{GD}_{n,\eta}
\right)
=
\mathrm{GF}_\infty.
}
\]

### Width first, then learning rate

The opposite order is

\[
\lim_{\eta\downarrow0}
\left(
\lim_{n\to\infty}
\mathrm{GD}_{n,\eta}
\right).
\]

For fixed \(\eta>0\), the inner limit—if it exists—would be an infinite-width discrete-time action-law recursion. It would not yet be a gradient flow because the time step remains nonzero.

One would then have to send \(\eta\downarrow0\) and prove that this discrete population recursion approaches \(\mathrm{GF}_\infty\).

This order is mathematically meaningful, but it is not proved in this document for exact GD. The fixed-\(\delta\) population Euler argument is closely related, but \(\delta\) discretizes the already-defined gradient-flow ODE; it is not the theorem’s exact raw GD at a fixed learning rate.

## 6. What the theorem actually calls the “joint” limit

The theorem fixes

\[
\eta_n=n^{-2}
\]

before taking \(n\to\infty\). Therefore its formal statement is the diagonal limit

\[
\boxed{
\mathrm{GD}_{n,n^{-2}}
\longrightarrow
\mathrm{GF}_\infty.
}
\]

It does not prove the unrestricted joint limit

\[
\lim_{\substack{n\to\infty\\\eta\downarrow0}}
\mathrm{GD}_{n,\eta}
=
\mathrm{GF}_\infty,
\]

which would mean convergence along every sequence

\[
n_k\to\infty,\qquad \eta_k\downarrow0.
\]

Indeed, the proved discretization estimate contains \(\eta\sqrt n\). It is therefore not uniform over all possible simultaneous routes. This does not prove that an unrestricted joint limit fails; it means this proof does not establish one.

For the exact-GD-to-gradient-flow comparison alone, the estimate would vanish along schedules satisfying

\[
\eta_n\sqrt n\longrightarrow0.
\]

The theorem chooses the much smaller step \(\eta_n=n^{-2}\).

## 7. Can a joint limit exist while neither iterated limit exists?

Yes. That statement is mathematically meaningful, provided “joint limit” means the unrestricted two-parameter limit.

Consider the scalar example

\[
a_{n,\eta}
=
\eta(-1)^n
+
\frac1n\sin\!\left(\frac1\eta\right).
\]

Because

\[
|a_{n,\eta}|
\le
\eta+\frac1n,
\]

we have

\[
a_{n,\eta}\longrightarrow0
\]

whenever \(n\to\infty\) and \(\eta\downarrow0\), along every simultaneous route.

But for fixed \(n\),

\[
\frac1n\sin\!\left(\frac1\eta\right)
\]

oscillates as \(\eta\downarrow0\), so the learning-rate-first inner limit does not exist.

For fixed \(\eta>0\),

\[
\eta(-1)^n
\]

oscillates as \(n\to\infty\), so the width-first inner limit also does not exist.

There is no contradiction: a joint limit examines only the corner where both parameters move toward their limiting values. An iterated limit first examines an entire boundary direction where one parameter remains fixed.

For this neural-network theorem, however, the learning-rate-first limit does exist at every fixed width. So the “neither iterated limit exists” possibility is a valid general phenomenon, but it is not what happens here.

The clean classification is:

\[
\boxed{
\begin{array}{ll}
\text{Diagonal limit:}&\eta=\eta_n\text{ is prescribed};\\
\text{Iterated limit:}&\text{one parameter is sent to its limit first};\\
\text{Unrestricted joint limit:}&\text{all simultaneous routes must converge}.
\end{array}
}
\]

The audited proof establishes the prescribed diagonal limit, the learning-rate-first iterated route, and the autonomy of the resulting action-law flow. It does not establish the exact-GD width-first route or an unrestricted two-parameter joint limit.
