[47 | 2026-08-30T11:14:38.803Z | assistant | None]
Your high-level picture is essentially right, with one correction:

\[
\boxed{\text{The limiting system is autonomous, but not finite-dimensional in the usual sense.}}
\]

It has a finite list of named evolving objects,

\[
z^{(1)},\qquad W^{(2)},\qquad \overline W^{(3)},
\]

and finitely many evolution equations. But \(z^{(1)}\) and \(\overline W^{(3)}\) describe whole neuron populations, while \(W^{(2)}\) acts on infinitely many possible population-level probes. So the accurate description is:

\[
\boxed{\text{a closed, autonomous, infinite-dimensional action-law flow with finitely many equations.}}
\]

## What the candidate limiting system is

At the population level, define

\[
z^{(2)}
=
W^{(2)}\phi(z^{(1)}),
\]

\[
\delta^{(2)}
=
\overline W^{(3)}
\odot\phi'(z^{(2)}),
\]

and

\[
r
=
\mathbb E_2\!\left[
\overline W^{(3)}\phi(z^{(2)})
\right]-1.
\]

Here \(\mathbb E_1\) and \(\mathbb E_2\) mean population averages over first- and second-layer neurons, respectively.

The proposed continuum equations are

\[
\frac{d}{dt}z^{(1)}
=
-2r\,
\phi'(z^{(1)})
\odot
\bigl(W^{(2)}\bigr)^*
\delta^{(2)},
\]

\[
\frac{d}{dt}W^{(2)}
=
-2r\,
\delta^{(2)}\otimes\phi(z^{(1)}),
\]

\[
\frac{d}{dt}\overline W^{(3)}
=
-2r\,\phi(z^{(2)}).
\]

The rank-one operator in the middle equation means

\[
\left(
\delta^{(2)}\otimes\phi(z^{(1)})
\right)v
=
\delta^{(2)}
\,
\mathbb E_1\!\left[
\phi(z^{(1)})v
\right].
\]

At finite width, \(\bigl(W^{(2)}\bigr)^*\) is simply \(\bigl(W_n^{(2)}\bigr)^\top\).

The theorem’s goal is not merely to show that a curve satisfying these equations can be written down. It must prove three logically separate facts:

1. These equations define a genuine autonomous dynamical system.
2. Exact finite-width, finite-step GD converges to that system.
3. Important observables such as loss, kernels, and hidden paths converge as consequences of state convergence.

None of these three facts alone implies the other two.

## The technical obligations

### 1. Derive the candidate equations exactly

The continuum equations cannot simply be guessed from a limiting loss curve.

One begins with the finite-width loss, differentiates it with respect to every weight, includes the width-dependent layer multipliers, and obtains the finite-width gradient-flow equations exactly.

The document introduces one invertible coordinate change for \(z^{(1)}\) to obtain uniform estimates, but that is an exact change of variables, not a linearization.

This obligation establishes that the proposed vector field genuinely comes from the original scaled gradient descent.

### 2. Define a state rich enough to determine the future

The marginal distributions of

\[
z^{(1)},\qquad z^{(2)},\qquad \overline W^{(3)}
\]

are not sufficient. The next derivative of \(z^{(1)}\) requires

\[
\bigl(W^{(2)}\bigr)^*\delta^{(2)},
\]

which is not determined by those marginal distributions.

The state must therefore retain every fixed finite forward/backward computation generated using

\[
W^{(2)},\qquad
\bigl(W^{(2)}\bigr)^*,\qquad
\phi,\qquad
\phi',
\]

together with coordinatewise arithmetic, joint empirical laws, and normalized inner products.

This collection is the action law. A uniform bound on how strongly \(W^{(2)}\) can amplify vectors is also required.

Without this step, the proposed “state” could omit information that changes its own future.

### 3. Construct the limiting initial action

The matrices \(W_n^{(2)}(0)\) have changing dimensions, so they cannot converge entry by entry to a fixed matrix.

Instead, one must construct the limits of all finite recipes such as

\[
W_n^{(2)}v_n,
\qquad
\bigl(W_n^{(2)}\bigr)^\top u_n,
\qquad
W_n^{(2)}
\phi'\!\left(
\bigl(W_n^{(2)}\bigr)^\top u_n
\right).
\]

The limiting joint laws must be mutually consistent. One must also prove that the limiting backward operation really is the transpose-like operation corresponding to the same forward \(W^{(2)}\), not multiplication by a fresh independent Gaussian matrix.

This reused-transpose dependence is one of the main technical difficulties.

### 4. Prove internal well-posedness

Writing

\[
\dot{\text{state}}=V(\text{state})
\]

does not yet produce a dynamical system.

One must prove:

- every term in \(V\) is defined;
- local solutions exist;
- solutions are unique;
- solutions depend continuously on the current state;
- no finite-time blow-up occurs.

The proof obtains local existence and uniqueness from a Lipschitz/contraction argument. It obtains global continuation from norm estimates and the loss-dissipation identity

\[
\frac{d}{dt}L(t)
=
-4r(t)^2K(t)
\le0.
\]

Thus the candidate is an actual solution of a differential equation, not merely a time-indexed family of fitted probability laws.

### 5. Prove closure and restartability

Even uniqueness on one chosen mathematical representation is not enough. Different representations may encode the same action law.

One must prove:

> If two current states give identical answers to every finite forward/backward probe, then their vector fields also give identical answers to every probe.

Uniqueness then implies that their future action laws remain identical.

If \(\Phi_t\) denotes evolution for time \(t\), this gives

\[
\Phi_t\!\left(\Phi_s(\text{current action law})\right)
=
\Phi_{t+s}(\text{current action law}).
\]

This is the precise deterministic Markov property. The full action law is Markovian; the scalar loss and the marginal hidden distributions generally are not.

### 6. Obtain estimates uniform in width

A finite-width ODE may be perfectly well posed for each \(n\) while its stability constant explodes as \(n\to\infty\).

The proof therefore needs width-independent bounds on quantities such as

\[
\|W_n^{(2)}(t)\|_{\mathrm{op}}
\]

and a width-independent Lipschitz estimate for the finite-width vector field.

These estimates ensure that:

- small errors are not amplified uncontrollably as \(n\to\infty\);
- Euler approximation errors are \(O(\delta)\) uniformly in \(n\);
- finite-width trajectories remain in a common bounded region.

This uniformity is the bridge between finite-dimensional ODE theory and the width limit.

### 7. Identify the width limit

There are two standard strategies for this kind of problem:

\[
\text{compactness}
\;+\;
\text{identify every subsequential limit}
\;+\;
\text{uniqueness},
\]

or a direct coupling showing that finite and limiting systems are close.

This proof uses a direct coupling. It introduces an auxiliary time mesh \(\delta>0\) and compares

\[
\text{finite-width continuous flow}
\]

to

\[
\text{finite-width Euler at mesh }\delta,
\]

then to a finite-width population oracle, then to continuum Euler, and finally to the continuum flow:

\[
\boxed{
\begin{aligned}
\text{finite-width flow}
&\approx
\text{finite-width Euler}_{\delta}\\
&\approx
\text{population oracle}_{n,\delta}\\
&\longrightarrow
\text{continuum Euler}_{\delta}\\
&\approx
\text{continuum flow}.
\end{aligned}}
\]

The oracle replaces empirical scalar feedback terms with their deterministic continuum values. The differences are explicit empirical Gram errors, controlled by a tensor-program theorem and discrete Gronwall.

### 8. Compare exact discrete GD with finite-width gradient flow

This is the gradient-flow limit.

Let \(\eta\) denote the actual base GD step. The proof obtains, schematically,

\[
\sup_{0\le t\le T}
d\!\left(
\text{exact GD}_{n,\eta}(t),
\text{finite-width GF}_n(t)
\right)
\le
C_T
\left(
\eta+\eta\sqrt n+\eta^2n
\right).
\]

For the theorem’s prescribed step

\[
\eta_n=n^{-2},
\]

the right side tends to zero.

Combining this with the width limit of the finite-width gradient flow gives

\[
\text{exact GD}_{n,\eta_n}
\longrightarrow
\text{continuum action flow}.
\]

### 9. Transfer convergence to observables

Loss convergence is weaker than state convergence. Two states can have the same loss now and different future derivatives.

Conversely, structural action-law convergence does not automatically imply convergence of every unbounded quantity. The proof separately establishes:

- output and loss convergence;
- path-law convergence for \(z^{(1)}(\cdot)\) and \(z^{(2)}(\cdot)\);
- tangent-kernel convergence;
- mean-square velocity convergence.

The last two require additional tail estimates because backpropagated signals are not uniformly bounded.

## The two physical limits

Let

\[
\mathcal S_{n,\eta}
\]

denote the complete exact-GD action state for width \(n\) and base step \(\eta\).

There are four different limit statements one might make.

### Learning-rate first, then width

First fix \(n\) and send \(\eta\downarrow0\):

\[
\lim_{\eta\downarrow0}\mathcal S_{n,\eta}
=
\mathcal S_n^{\mathrm{GF}}.
\]

This produces finite-width continuous gradient flow.

Then send \(n\to\infty\):

\[
\lim_{n\to\infty}
\mathcal S_n^{\mathrm{GF}}
=
\mathcal S.
\]

Thus

\[
\boxed{
\lim_{n\to\infty}
\left(
\lim_{\eta\downarrow0}\mathcal S_{n,\eta}
\right)
=
\mathcal S.
}
\]

This order is mathematically meaningful and is supported by the proof.

### Width first, then learning rate

First fix a nonzero \(\eta\) and ask whether

\[
\mathcal S_{\infty,\eta}
=
\lim_{n\to\infty}\mathcal S_{n,\eta}
\]

exists.

If it does, \(\mathcal S_{\infty,\eta}\) is an infinite-width discrete-time recurrence, not yet a gradient flow. One would then ask whether

\[
\lim_{\eta\downarrow0}
\mathcal S_{\infty,\eta}
=
\mathcal S.
\]

This order is perfectly meaningful, but it is not proved in this document. Establishing it would require width estimates appropriate to fixed nonzero \(\eta\).

### A specified diagonal limit

Choose one schedule \(\eta=\eta_n\) and take both limits simultaneously:

\[
\mathcal S_{n,\eta_n}\longrightarrow\mathcal S.
\]

The theorem proves the particular diagonal

\[
\boxed{\eta_n=n^{-2}.}
\]

A diagonal statement only concerns that schedule. It does not automatically say what happens for \(\eta_n=n^{-1}\), \(\eta_n=n^{-1/2}\), or any other route.

### An unrestricted joint limit

The strongest statement would be

\[
\lim_{\substack{n\to\infty\\\eta\downarrow0}}
\mathcal S_{n,\eta}
=
\mathcal S.
\]

This means convergence along every sequence

\[
n_k\to\infty,\qquad \eta_k\downarrow0.
\]

Equivalently, for every \(\varepsilon>0\), all sufficiently large \(n\) and all sufficiently small \(\eta\) must give a state within \(\varepsilon\) of \(\mathcal S\).

The document does not prove this unrestricted joint limit. Its GD-versus-GF estimate contains

\[
\eta\sqrt n,
\]

which is not small along every route to \(n=\infty,\eta=0\). This does not prove that the unrestricted joint limit fails; it means the available estimate is not uniform enough to establish it.

## Why order can matter

Consider the scalar example

\[
a_{n,\eta}
=
\frac{n\eta}{1+n\eta}.
\]

Learning-rate first gives

\[
\lim_{n\to\infty}
\left(
\lim_{\eta\downarrow0}a_{n,\eta}
\right)
=0.
\]

Width first gives

\[
\lim_{\eta\downarrow0}
\left(
\lim_{n\to\infty}a_{n,\eta}
\right)
=1.
\]

Different diagonals also give different answers:

\[
\eta_n=n^{-2}
\quad\Longrightarrow\quad
a_{n,\eta_n}\to0,
\]

\[
\eta_n=n^{-1}
\quad\Longrightarrow\quad
a_{n,\eta_n}\to\frac12,
\]

\[
\eta_n=n^{-1/2}
\quad\Longrightarrow\quad
a_{n,\eta_n}\to1.
\]

This is what nonuniform interaction between the two parameters looks like.

Even agreement of both iterated limits does not guarantee an unrestricted joint limit. For

\[
b_{n,\eta}
=
\frac{n\eta}{1+(n\eta)^2},
\]

both iterated limits equal \(0\), but

\[
b_{n,1/n}=\frac12.
\]

## Can a joint limit exist when neither iterated limit exists?

Yes—provided “joint” means the unrestricted two-parameter limit. The sentence is mathematically well defined.

For example, let

\[
c_{n,\eta}
=
\eta(-1)^n
+
\frac1n\sin\!\left(\frac1\eta\right).
\]

Because

\[
|c_{n,\eta}|
\le
\eta+\frac1n,
\]

we have

\[
c_{n,\eta}\longrightarrow0
\]

along every route with \(n\to\infty\) and \(\eta\downarrow0\).

But for fixed \(\eta>0\), the \(n\)-limit fails because of \(\eta(-1)^n\). For fixed \(n\), the \(\eta\)-limit fails because of \(n^{-1}\sin(1/\eta)\). Thus neither iterated construction is defined, despite the unrestricted joint limit existing.

The final nuance is that the auxiliary mesh \(\delta\) in the proof is not the physical learning rate \(\eta\). The proof fixes \(\delta\), takes \(n\to\infty\), and then sends \(\delta\downarrow0\) only because the tensor-program theorem applies to a fixed finite number of Euler operations. Separately, it compares actual GD with step \(\eta_n=n^{-2}\) to finite-width gradient flow.

So the document proves:

\[
\boxed{
\text{a prescribed physical diagonal limit }
\eta_n=n^{-2},
}
\]

using

\[
\boxed{
\text{an auxiliary proof order }
\delta\text{ fixed}\;\to\;n\to\infty\;\to\;\delta\downarrow0.
}
\]

Those are related but logically different limit procedures.

[24 | 2026-08-30T11:31:15.011Z | assistant | None]
Think of the theorem as trying to validate a new training simulator. The original simulator is a random width-\(n\) network updated in discrete steps of size \(\eta\). The proposed replacement is a deterministic population-level system evolving continuously in time. The theorem must show both that the replacement can run by itself and that it actually reproduces the original simulator as \(n\to\infty\) and \(\eta\to0\).

The limiting system has only three named evolving objects,

\[
z^{(1)},\qquad W^{(2)},\qquad \overline W^{(3)},
\]

but it is not finite-dimensional in the strict sense. This is like a PDE with only three fields: finitely many equations, but each field contains infinitely many degrees of freedom. Here \(z^{(1)}\) describes a whole first-layer population, \(\overline W^{(3)}\) describes a whole second-layer population, and \(W^{(2)}\) describes how population-level vectors are transported between the two layers.

Given the current state, define

\[
z^{(2)}=W^{(2)}\phi(z^{(1)}),
\]

\[
\delta^{(2)}
=
\overline W^{(3)}\odot\phi'(z^{(2)}),
\]

and

\[
r
=
\mathbb E\!\left[
\overline W^{(3)}\phi(z^{(2)})
\right]-1.
\]

The proposed continuous dynamics are

\[
\dot z^{(1)}
=
-2r\,
\phi'(z^{(1)})
\odot
\bigl(W^{(2)}\bigr)^*\delta^{(2)},
\]

\[
\dot W^{(2)}
=
-2r\,
\delta^{(2)}\otimes\phi(z^{(1)}),
\]

\[
\dot{\overline W}^{(3)}
=
-2r\,\phi(z^{(2)}).
\]

These are just forward propagation and backpropagation written for neuron populations. The symbol \(\bigl(W^{(2)}\bigr)^*\) is the limiting version of the finite matrix transpose \(\bigl(W_n^{(2)}\bigr)^\top\). The middle update is the population version of a rank-one outer-product update.

To prove that this is the right replacement, the first obligation is to derive these equations exactly from finite-width gradient flow. Otherwise they could simply be plausible equations whose loss happens to resemble the finite network’s loss. The proof differentiates the actual finite loss, includes all width-dependent learning-rate factors, and obtains the finite version of these equations without approximation. It uses an invertible change of the first-layer coordinate only to obtain estimates that do not deteriorate with width; this does not alter the underlying dynamics.

Next, one must specify what “the current population state” contains. The distributions of \(z^{(1)}\), \(z^{(2)}\), and \(\overline W^{(3)}\) are not enough. Two networks can have the same current hidden distributions and the same loss but different values of

\[
\bigl(W^{(2)}\bigr)^*\delta^{(2)},
\]

and therefore different next first-layer updates.

The limiting state consequently remembers the result of every fixed finite training computation built from

\[
W^{(2)},\qquad
\bigl(W^{(2)}\bigr)^*,\qquad
\phi,\qquad
\phi',
\]

and coordinatewise arithmetic. It records the population distributions, squared sizes, and correlations of those computations. One can view this as retaining a black-box description of \(W^{(2)}\): we do not retain all its entries, but we retain its answer to every forward or backward query that training can generate. This is what the proof calls the action law.

Once that state has been chosen, the next obligation is to show that the displayed equations can actually be run. Given one current action law, every term on the right must be defined, there must be a solution for some future interval, that solution must be unique, and it must not blow up later. In less formal language: the current state must have exactly one future, and small errors in the current state must not instantly produce huge errors.

Uniqueness is also what turns the equations into a genuinely autonomous or Markovian system. If two mathematical representations answer every training-relevant forward/backward query identically, then they have the same derivatives. Uniqueness then gives them the same future. Training can therefore be stopped at time \(s\), its past discarded, and restarted from the complete current action law. This is much stronger than constructing a family of fitted distributions indexed by time.

But a perfectly well-defined autonomous system might still have nothing to do with the finite network. The proof must next establish fidelity: exact finite training must approach this system. The central comparison is

\[
\begin{aligned}
\text{exact finite GD}
&\approx
\text{finite-width continuous gradient flow}\\
&\approx
\text{finite-width Euler approximation}\\
&\approx
\text{population Euler approximation}\\
&\approx
\text{continuum flow}.
\end{aligned}
\]

Each approximation has a distinct purpose, but they form one chain.

First, the proof obtains bounds that hold uniformly in \(n\). This matters because an error of size \(10^{-6}\) is useless if the dynamics can amplify it by a factor growing like \(n\). Uniform bounds show that the middle matrix cannot amplify vectors arbitrarily and that the finite-width vector field has a width-independent stability constant.

The proof then introduces an auxiliary mesh \(\delta\) and applies Euler’s method to the already continuous finite-width gradient flow. For fixed \(\delta\) and a fixed time interval \([0,T]\), there are only about \(T/\delta\) update steps. That is a fixed finite computation, so the tensor-program law of large numbers can identify its width limit.

There is one complication: the real finite Euler scheme feeds empirical averages back into later updates. The proof introduces a population oracle that replaces those empirical averages by their limiting population values. The tensor theorem identifies the oracle’s width limit, ordinary concentration makes the empirical-average errors vanish, and a repeated stability estimate shows that the real Euler scheme remains close to the oracle.

Finally, the uniform Euler error is \(O(\delta)\) at both finite and infinite width. Therefore one first fixes \(\delta\), lets \(n\to\infty\), and then lets \(\delta\to0\). This identifies the width limit of finite-width continuous gradient flow.

Separately, the actual GD step is denoted by \(\eta\). The proof estimates

\[
\sup_{0\le t\le T}
\operatorname{distance}
\left(
\text{GD}_{n,\eta}(t),
\text{GF}_n(t)
\right)
\le
C_T\left(
\eta+\eta\sqrt n+\eta^2n
\right).
\]

With the theorem’s prescribed choice

\[
\eta_n=n^{-2},
\]

this error vanishes. Combining the two comparisons proves that exact discrete GD approaches the autonomous continuum system.

Loss convergence is then a consequence, not the main definition of success. The proof first controls the complete action state and then separately checks that output, loss, hidden trajectories, tangent kernels, and mean-square velocities are continuous enough to inherit convergence. Some of these checks require extra tail estimates because backpropagated signals can be large even though \(\phi=\arctan\) is bounded.

This also clarifies the two limits. Let \(\mathcal S_{n,\eta}\) denote the complete training state at width \(n\) and step size \(\eta\). Taking the learning-rate limit first means

\[
\lim_{n\to\infty}
\left(
\lim_{\eta\to0}\mathcal S_{n,\eta}
\right).
\]

For each fixed \(n\), discrete GD becomes finite-width continuous gradient flow; then the width tends to infinity. This order is meaningful and is supported by the proof.

Taking width first means

\[
\lim_{\eta\to0}
\left(
\lim_{n\to\infty}\mathcal S_{n,\eta}
\right).
\]

The inner limit would be an infinite-width discrete-time training rule at fixed nonzero \(\eta\). That is also a meaningful question, but this document does not prove that the inner limit exists or that subsequently sending \(\eta\to0\) produces the same continuum flow.

The theorem itself proves a specified diagonal limit:

\[
\mathcal S_{n,\eta_n}\longrightarrow\mathcal S,
\qquad
\eta_n=n^{-2}.
\]

This means width grows and the step shrinks according to one prescribed relationship. It is weaker than an unrestricted joint limit, which would require convergence along every possible sequence

\[
n_k\to\infty,\qquad \eta_k\to0.
\]

The proved error contains \(\eta\sqrt n\), so it is not uniform over every such route. This does not show that the unrestricted joint limit fails; it only shows that this proof does not establish it.

Order dependence is easy to see in the scalar example

\[
a_{n,\eta}=\frac{n\eta}{1+n\eta}.
\]

Sending \(\eta\to0\) first gives \(0\), while sending \(n\to\infty\) first gives \(1\). Along \(\eta_n=1/n\), the value is always \(1/2\). Thus the two iterated limits and a diagonal can all describe different regimes.

The statement “a joint limit exists even though neither iterated limit exists” is also mathematically meaningful, provided “joint” means convergence along every simultaneous route. For example,

\[
c_{n,\eta}
=
\eta(-1)^n+\frac1n\sin(1/\eta)
\]

satisfies

\[
|c_{n,\eta}|\le \eta+\frac1n,
\]

so \(c_{n,\eta}\to0\) whenever \(n\to\infty\) and \(\eta\to0\) together. Yet for fixed \(\eta\), the \(n\)-limit oscillates, and for fixed \(n\), the \(\eta\)-limit oscillates. Thus neither iterated limit exists.

The last distinction is crucial: the auxiliary mesh \(\delta\) is not the physical GD step \(\eta\). Fixing \(\delta\), taking width to infinity, and then removing \(\delta\) is a proof technique for identifying the width limit of continuous gradient flow. The physical theorem is the diagonal statement \(\eta_n=n^{-2}\).

[68 | 2026-08-30T16:48:05.192Z | assistant | None]
# Headline result

Let the initial particle pairs

\[
(p_i,z_{i,0}),\qquad i=1,\ldots,n,
\]

be independent copies of a random pair \((P,Z_0)\), where \(P\in\{-1,1\}\). The label \(p_i\) never changes; only \(z_{i,k}\) is trained.

Define

\[
\phi(z)=\arctan z,
\qquad
f_{n,k}=\frac1n\sum_{i=1}^n p_i\phi(z_{i,k}),
\qquad
r_{n,k}=f_{n,k}-y.
\]

Exact finite-width GD is

\[
z_{i,k+1}
=
z_{i,k}
-
\eta_n p_i r_{n,k}\phi'(z_{i,k}).
\]

Represent it in continuous training time \(t=k\eta_n\), holding each iterate constant until the next update. Linear interpolation gives the same limit.

Assume only

\[
\eta_n\longrightarrow0.
\]

The limiting population state is the distribution

\[
\mu_t=\operatorname{Law}(P,X(t)),
\]

where

\[
F(z)=z+\frac{z^3}{3},
\qquad
X(0)=F(Z_0),
\qquad
Z(t)=F^{-1}(X(t)).
\]

Given the current population law \(\mu_t\), compute its residual

\[
r(t)=\mathbb E[P\phi(Z(t))]-y,
\]

and evolve each population particle by

\[
\frac{dX(t)}{dt}=-P\,r(t).
\]

This is autonomous: the current law determines the current residual, and the residual determines the next movement. No externally supplied trajectory or past history is needed once the current law is known.

For every fixed \(T\) and every fixed bounded function \(\psi(p,z)\) that is Lipschitz in \(z\),

\[
\boxed{
\sup_{0\le t\le T}
\left|
\frac1n\sum_{i=1}^n
\psi(p_i,z_i^{\mathrm{GD}}(t))
-
\mathbb E[\psi(P,Z(t))]
\right|
\longrightarrow0
\quad\text{in probability}.
}
\]

This is uniform in time for each fixed \(\psi\). Choosing

\[
\psi(p,z)=p\phi(z)
\]

gives convergence of the prediction, and therefore of the residual and normalized loss.

---

## 1. Raw finite GD versus transformed finite flow

Let

\[
q(z)=\phi'(z)=\frac1{1+z^2},
\qquad
X=F(z)=z+\frac{z^3}{3}.
\]

Since

\[
F'(z)=1+z^2=\frac1{q(z)},
\]

the raw continuous gradient flow

\[
\dot z_i=-p_i r_nq(z_i)
\]

becomes exactly

\[
\dot X_i=-p_i r_n.
\]

Thus the nonlinear coordinate change is exact in continuous time.

It is not exact for discrete GD. Let

\[
v_i=-p_i r_n.
\]

One GD step is

\[
z_i^+=z_i+\eta_nq(z_i)v_i.
\]

Because \(F\) is cubic,

\[
\begin{aligned}
F(z_i^+)
={}&F(z_i)+\eta_n v_i
\\
&+\eta_n^2z_iq(z_i)^2v_i^2
+\frac{\eta_n^3}{3}q(z_i)^3v_i^3.
\end{aligned}
\]

The first two terms are transformed Euler. The remaining terms are the nonlinear-coordinate defect.

Set

\[
R=\frac{\pi}{2}+|y|.
\]

Since \(|r_n|\le R\), the one-step coordinate defect is at most

\[
\eta_n^2
\left(R^2+\frac{R^3}{3}\right).
\]

The exact continuous flow also differs from one ideal Euler step by at most

\[
\frac R2\eta_n^2.
\]

If \(E_k\) is the largest transformed-coordinate difference between GD and the finite flow at time \(k\eta_n\), then

\[
E_{k+1}
\le
(1+\eta_n)E_k
+
\eta_n^2
\left(
R^2+\frac{R^3}{3}+\frac R2
\right).
\]

Because \(E_0=0\), discrete Gronwall gives

\[
\max_{k\eta_n\le T}E_k
\le
C_1(T,y)\eta_n,
\]

where \(C_1(T,y)\) does not depend on \(n\).

Therefore

\[
\text{raw finite GD}
\approx
\text{finite transformed flow}
\]

whenever \(\eta_n\to0\).

---

## 2. Replace the finite flow by coarse Euler

Choose an auxiliary proof mesh \(\delta>0\), unrelated to \(\eta_n\).

Finite transformed Euler is

\[
X^n_{i,m+1}
=
X^n_{i,m}-\delta p_i r^n_m,
\]

where

\[
r^n_m
=
\frac1n\sum_j
p_j\phi\!\left(F^{-1}(X^n_{j,m})\right)-y.
\]

The transformed vector field is bounded by \(R\) and is 1-Lipschitz in the largest coordinate difference. Ordinary Euler analysis therefore gives

\[
\sup_{t\le T}
\max_i
|X_i^{n,\mathrm{flow}}(t)-X_i^{n,\mathrm{Euler}}(t)|
\le
C_2(T,y)\delta,
\]

uniformly in \(n\).

Thus the actual GD trajectory is within

\[
C_1(T,y)\eta_n+C_2(T,y)\delta
\]

of coarse finite Euler.

The purpose of \(\delta\) is that the number of coarse updates

\[
M=\left\lceil\frac T\delta\right\rceil
\]

is fixed while \(n\to\infty\).

---

## 3. Pop Euler and the finite oracle

Pop Euler evolves one representative random particle:

\[
r_m
=
\mathbb E\!\left[
P\phi(F^{-1}(X_m))
\right]-y,
\]

\[
X_{m+1}=X_m-\delta P r_m.
\]

The finite oracle starts from the same finite initialization as finite Euler but uses the pop residual:

\[
\bar X_{i,m+1}
=
\bar X_{i,m}-\delta p_i r_m.
\]

The oracle also has an empirical residual,

\[
\bar r^n_m
=
\frac1n\sum_i
p_i\phi(F^{-1}(\bar X_{i,m}))-y,
\]

but it does not use this number in its update.

Let

\[
E_m
=
\max_i|X^n_{i,m}-\bar X_{i,m}|.
\]

Subtracting the finite-Euler and oracle updates gives

\[
E_{m+1}
\le
E_m+\delta|r^n_m-r_m|.
\]

Insert \(\bar r^n_m\):

\[
|r^n_m-r_m|
\le
|r^n_m-\bar r^n_m|
+
|\bar r^n_m-r_m|.
\]

Because \(\phi\circ F^{-1}\) is 1-Lipschitz,

\[
|r^n_m-\bar r^n_m|\le E_m.
\]

Hence

\[
E_{m+1}
\le
(1+\delta)E_m
+
\delta|\bar r^n_m-r_m|.
\]

Since \(E_0=0\),

\[
\max_{m\le M}E_m
\le
(e^T-1)
\max_{m\le M}|\bar r^n_m-r_m|.
\]

The finite/oracle difference is therefore controlled entirely by the oracle’s residual sampling error.

---

## 4. Oracle sampling versus pop Euler

The pop residuals \(r_0,\ldots,r_{M-1}\) are deterministic once the initial population law is fixed.

Each complete oracle trajectory is therefore a deterministic function of its own initialization:

\[
(p_i,\bar X_{i,0})
\longmapsto
(p_i,\bar X_{i,0},\ldots,\bar X_{i,M}).
\]

Because the initial pairs are independent copies, the complete oracle trajectories are independent copies of the pop Euler trajectory.

At every step \(m\),

\[
p_i\phi(F^{-1}(\bar X_{i,m}))
\]

are therefore independent copies of

\[
P\phi(F^{-1}(X_m)).
\]

Hoeffding’s inequality and a union bound give, with probability at least \(1-\alpha/2\),

\[
\max_{m\le M}|\bar r^n_m-r_m|
\le
\pi
\sqrt{
\frac{\log(4(M+1)/\alpha)}{2n}
}.
\]

Consequently,

\[
\max_{m\le M}E_m
\le
(e^T-1)\pi
\sqrt{
\frac{\log(4(M+1)/\alpha)}{2n}
}.
\]

There is a second, distinct sampling error. Let \(\psi\) be the observable in the headline result, and define the length of its range by

\[
B_\psi=\sup\psi-\inf\psi.
\]

The same argument gives, with probability at least \(1-\alpha/2\),

\[
\max_{m\le M}
\left|
\frac1n\sum_i
\psi(p_i,\bar Z_{i,m})
-
\mathbb E[\psi(P,Z_m)]
\right|
\le
B_\psi
\sqrt{
\frac{\log(4(M+1)/\alpha)}{2n}
},
\]

where

\[
\bar Z_{i,m}=F^{-1}(\bar X_{i,m}),
\qquad
Z_m=F^{-1}(X_m).
\]

The first sampling error controls how residual error changes the finite dynamics. The second directly controls the empirical \(\psi\)-avg.

For the whole trajectory, define

\[
\Gamma_i
=
(p_i,\bar Z_{i,0},\ldots,\bar Z_{i,M}).
\]

These are independent copies of

\[
\Gamma=(P,Z_0,\ldots,Z_M).
\]

Thus, for every integrable trajectory observable \(\Psi\),

\[
\frac1n\sum_i\Psi(\Gamma_i)
\longrightarrow
\mathbb E[\Psi(\Gamma)].
\]

No independence across time is asserted or needed; independence is across complete particle trajectories.

---

## 5. Remove the pop Euler mesh

The pop flow is

\[
\dot X(t)=-P r(t),
\qquad
r(t)=\mathbb E[P\phi(F^{-1}(X(t)))]-y.
\]

To prove existence and uniqueness concretely, write

\[
X(t)=X_0-Ps(t).
\]

Then the scalar accumulated residual satisfies

\[
\frac{ds(t)}{dt}
=
\mathbb E\!\left[
P\phi(F^{-1}(X_0-Ps(t)))
\right]-y.
\]

The right-hand side is bounded by \(R\) and is 1-Lipschitz in \(s\). Therefore this scalar ODE has a unique global solution. This proves that the pop flow is well-defined.

Pop Euler is ordinary Euler for the same scalar equation. If \(s_m\) is its discrete accumulated residual, then

\[
\max_{m\delta\le T}|s_m-s(m\delta)|
\le
\frac R2(e^T-1)\delta.
\]

Because

\[
X_m=X_0-Ps_m,
\qquad
X(t)=X_0-Ps(t),
\]

the same estimate holds for \(X_m-X(m\delta)\). Holding Euler constant between grid points adds at most \(R\delta\). Therefore

\[
\sup_{t\le T}
|X^{\mathrm{pop\ Euler}}(t)-X^{\mathrm{pop\ flow}}(t)|
\le
C_2(T,y)\delta.
\]

No oracle is needed here: pop Euler and pop flow already live on the same population state and use the same vector field.

---

## 6. Add the five errors

At any time \(t\), the triangle inequality gives

\[
\begin{aligned}
&
\left|
\frac1n\sum_i\psi(p_i,z_i^{\mathrm{GD}}(t))
-
\mathbb E[\psi(P,Z(t))]
\right|
\\
&\le
|\text{finite GD avg}-\text{finite flow avg}|
&&\text{(actual step)}
\\
&\quad+
|\text{finite flow avg}-\text{finite Euler avg}|
&&\text{(finite mesh)}
\\
&\quad+
|\text{finite Euler avg}-\text{oracle avg}|
&&\text{(feedback)}
\\
&\quad+
|\text{oracle avg}-\text{pop Euler avg}|
&&\text{(sampling of }\psi\text{)}
\\
&\quad+
|\text{pop Euler avg}-\text{pop flow avg}|
&&\text{(pop mesh)}.
\end{aligned}
\]

Suppose \(\psi\) is \(L_\psi\)-Lipschitz in \(z\). Since \(F^{-1}\) is 1-Lipschitz, the five terms are bounded respectively by

\[
L_\psi C_1(T,y)\eta_n,
\]

\[
L_\psi C_2(T,y)\delta,
\]

\[
L_\psi(e^T-1)\pi
\sqrt{
\frac{\log(4(M+1)/\alpha)}{2n}
},
\]

\[
B_\psi
\sqrt{
\frac{\log(4(M+1)/\alpha)}{2n}
},
\]

and

\[
L_\psi C_2(T,y)\delta.
\]

These estimates hold simultaneously for all \(t\le T\), with probability at least \(1-\alpha\). Therefore

\[
\begin{aligned}
\sup_{t\le T}
\left|
\frac1n\sum_i\psi(p_i,z_i^{\mathrm{GD}}(t))
-
\mathbb E[\psi(P,Z(t))]
\right|
\le{}&
L_\psi C_1(T,y)\eta_n
\\
&+2L_\psi C_2(T,y)\delta
\\
&+
\left[L_\psi(e^T-1)\pi+B_\psi\right]
\sqrt{
\frac{\log(4(M+1)/\alpha)}{2n}
}.
\end{aligned}
\]

This proves the headline result as follows.

Given an error tolerance \(\varepsilon>0\) and failure probability \(\alpha>0\):

1. Choose \(\delta\) small enough that the two mesh errors are below \(\varepsilon/3\).
2. Keep \(\delta\) fixed, so \(M=\lceil T/\delta\rceil\) is fixed.
3. Choose \(n\) large enough that \(\eta_n\) and both sampling terms are below the remaining error.

Then

\[
\Pr\left(
\sup_{t\le T}
\left|
\frac1n\sum_i\psi(p_i,z_i^{\mathrm{GD}}(t))
-
\mathbb E[\psi(P,Z(t))]
\right|
>\varepsilon
\right)
\le\alpha.
\]

Since \(\alpha\) is arbitrary, this is uniform compact-time convergence in probability.

“For every bounded Lipschitz \(\psi\)” means that the argument applies to each fixed \(\psi\). A finite collection of observables can be handled simultaneously by another finite union bound.

For the toy model, the step-size requirement is simply

\[
\eta_n\to0.
\]

The full neural-network proof has the raw-GD estimate

\[
C_T\left(
\eta_n+\eta_n\sqrt n+\eta_n^2n
\right),
\]

so its sufficient criterion is

\[
\eta_n\sqrt n\to0.
\]

The audited theorem’s choice \(\eta_n=n^{-2}\) is one convenient instance of this criterion, not the only possible one.

## "# Headline result Let the initial particle pairs \\[ (p_i,z_{i,0}),\\qquad i=1,\\l…": /home/codex-b/.codex/attachments/10208185-2322-4ac9-8bc9-72f4dd83276d/pasted-text.txt

New notes:

You are right about the mismatch, with one correction:

\[
\eta_n\sqrt n\to0
\qquad\Longleftrightarrow\qquad
\eta_n=o(n^{-1/2}),
\]

not \(\eta_n=o(\sqrt n)\).

The earlier headline was sharp for the simplified toy system, whose GD-to-flow error is only \(O(\eta_n)\). But it was not stated in the stronger learning-rate regime certified by the full neural-network proof. Below is the corrected, full-proof-compatible version of the toy argument.

# Headline result

Let

\[
(p_i,z_{i,0}),\qquad i=1,\ldots,n,
\]

be independent copies of \((P,Z_0)\), where \(P\in\{-1,1\}\). The label \(p_i\) remains fixed; only \(z_{i,k}\) is trained.

Define

\[
\phi(z)=\arctan z,
\qquad
f_{n,k}=\frac1n\sum_{i=1}^n p_i\phi(z_{i,k}),
\qquad
r_{n,k}=f_{n,k}-y.
\]

Exact finite-width GD is

\[
z_{i,k+1}
=
z_{i,k}
-
\eta_n p_i r_{n,k}\phi'(z_{i,k}).
\]

Use the physical clock

\[
t=k\eta_n.
\]

Assume

\[
\eta_n+\eta_n\sqrt n+\eta_n^2n\longrightarrow0.
\]

Equivalently, it is enough to assume

\[
\eta_n\sqrt n\longrightarrow0.
\]

The limiting population state is

\[
\mu_t=\operatorname{Law}(P,X(t)),
\]

where

\[
F(z)=z+\frac{z^3}{3},
\qquad
X(0)=F(Z_0),
\qquad
Z(t)=F^{-1}(X(t)).
\]

Its residual and dynamics are

\[
r(t)=\mathbb E[P\phi(Z(t))]-y,
\]

\[
\frac{dX(t)}{dt}=-P\,r(t).
\]

This is autonomous: the current law \(\mu_t\) determines \(r(t)\), and \(r(t)\) determines the next movement.

For every fixed \(T\) and every fixed bounded function \(\psi(p,z)\) that is Lipschitz in \(z\),

\[
\boxed{
\sup_{0\le t\le T}
\left|
\frac1n\sum_{i=1}^n
\psi(p_i,z_i^{\mathrm{GD}}(t))
-
\mathbb E[\psi(P,Z(t))]
\right|
\longrightarrow0
\quad\text{in probability}.
}
\]

Choosing

\[
\psi(p,z)=p\phi(z)
\]

gives convergence of the prediction, residual, and normalized loss.

---

## 1. Raw finite GD versus transformed finite flow

Let

\[
q(z)=\phi'(z)=\frac1{1+z^2},
\qquad
X=F(z)=z+\frac{z^3}{3}.
\]

Because

\[
F'(z)=\frac1{q(z)},
\]

the raw continuous gradient flow

\[
\dot z_i=-p_i r_nq(z_i)
\]

becomes exactly

\[
\dot X_i=-p_i r_n.
\]

The coordinate transformation is exact in continuous time, but not for discrete GD.

Let

\[
v_i=-p_i r_n.
\]

One GD step is

\[
z_i^+=z_i+\eta_nq(z_i)v_i.
\]

Since \(F\) is cubic,

\[
\begin{aligned}
F(z_i^+)
={}&F(z_i)+\eta_n v_i
\\
&+\eta_n^2z_iq(z_i)^2v_i^2
+\frac{\eta_n^3}{3}q(z_i)^3v_i^3.
\end{aligned}
\]

In the toy system, \(|v_i|\) is uniformly bounded, so the accumulated GD-to-flow error is \(O(\eta_n)\).

The full neural-network proof has a backpropagated field whose normalized norm is bounded, but whose largest coordinate may be \(\sqrt n\) times larger. Its one-step coordinate defect is therefore bounded by

\[
C_T\left(\eta_n^2\sqrt n+\eta_n^3n\right).
\]

There is also an ordinary ODE local defect \(O_T(\eta_n^2)\). Over approximately \(T/\eta_n\) steps, stability gives

\[
\sup_{t\le T}
d_n\!\left(
\theta_n^{\mathrm{GD}}(t),
\theta_n^{\mathrm{flow}}(t)
\right)
\le
C_T\left(
\eta_n+\eta_n\sqrt n+\eta_n^2n
\right).
\]

Thus the first approximation vanishes under

\[
\eta_n\sqrt n\to0.
\]

For the toy model this condition is stronger than necessary, but it makes the consolidated statement compatible with the full proof.

---

## 2. Finite flow versus coarse finite Euler

Choose an auxiliary proof mesh \(\delta>0\), independent of \(\eta_n\).

Finite transformed Euler is

\[
X^n_{i,m+1}
=
X^n_{i,m}-\delta p_i r^n_m,
\]

where

\[
r^n_m
=
\frac1n\sum_j
p_j\phi\!\left(F^{-1}(X^n_{j,m})\right)-y.
\]

The transformed vector field is bounded and Lipschitz. Ordinary Euler analysis therefore gives

\[
\sup_{t\le T}
\max_i
|X_i^{n,\mathrm{flow}}(t)-X_i^{n,\mathrm{Euler}}(t)|
\le
C_T\delta,
\]

uniformly in \(n\).

Thus

\[
\text{raw GD}
\approx
\text{finite Euler}
\]

with error at most

\[
C_T\left(
\eta_n+\eta_n\sqrt n+\eta_n^2n+\delta
\right).
\]

The coarse scheme has only

\[
M=\left\lceil\frac T\delta\right\rceil
\]

updates, which is fixed while \(n\to\infty\).

---

## 3. Pop Euler and the finite oracle

Pop Euler is

\[
r_m
=
\mathbb E\!\left[
P\phi(F^{-1}(X_m))
\right]-y,
\]

\[
X_{m+1}=X_m-\delta P r_m.
\]

The finite oracle starts from the same finite initialization as finite Euler but uses the pop residual:

\[
\bar X_{i,m+1}
=
\bar X_{i,m}-\delta p_i r_m.
\]

Its empirical residual is

\[
\bar r^n_m
=
\frac1n\sum_i
p_i\phi(F^{-1}(\bar X_{i,m}))-y,
\]

but the oracle does not use this number in its updates.

Let

\[
E_m=\max_i|X^n_{i,m}-\bar X_{i,m}|.
\]

Subtracting the finite-Euler and oracle updates gives

\[
E_{m+1}
\le
E_m+\delta|r^n_m-r_m|.
\]

Insert \(\bar r^n_m\):

\[
|r^n_m-r_m|
\le
|r^n_m-\bar r^n_m|
+
|\bar r^n_m-r_m|.
\]

Because \(\phi\circ F^{-1}\) is 1-Lipschitz,

\[
|r^n_m-\bar r^n_m|\le E_m.
\]

Therefore

\[
E_{m+1}
\le
(1+\delta)E_m
+
\delta|\bar r^n_m-r_m|.
\]

Since \(E_0=0\),

\[
\max_{m\le M}E_m
\le
(e^T-1)
\max_{m\le M}|\bar r^n_m-r_m|.
\]

Thus finite Euler is close to the oracle whenever the oracle’s residual sampling error is small.

---

## 4. Oracle sampling versus pop Euler

The pop residual sequence

\[
r_0,\ldots,r_{M-1}
\]

is deterministic once the initial population law is fixed.

Each complete oracle trajectory is therefore a deterministic function of its own initialization:

\[
(p_i,\bar X_{i,0})
\longmapsto
(p_i,\bar X_{i,0},\ldots,\bar X_{i,M}).
\]

Hence the complete oracle trajectories are independent copies of the pop Euler trajectory.

At every step \(m\),

\[
p_i\phi(F^{-1}(\bar X_{i,m}))
\]

are independent copies of

\[
P\phi(F^{-1}(X_m)).
\]

Hoeffding’s inequality and a union bound give, with probability at least \(1-\alpha/2\),

\[
\max_{m\le M}|\bar r^n_m-r_m|
\le
\pi
\sqrt{
\frac{\log(4(M+1)/\alpha)}{2n}
}.
\]

Consequently,

\[
\max_{m\le M}E_m
\le
(e^T-1)\pi
\sqrt{
\frac{\log(4(M+1)/\alpha)}{2n}
}.
\]

There is also a separate sampling error for the chosen observable \(\psi\). Let

\[
B_\psi=\sup\psi-\inf\psi.
\]

With probability at least \(1-\alpha/2\),

\[
\max_{m\le M}
\left|
\frac1n\sum_i\psi(p_i,\bar Z_{i,m})
-
\mathbb E[\psi(P,Z_m)]
\right|
\le
B_\psi
\sqrt{
\frac{\log(4(M+1)/\alpha)}{2n}
},
\]

where

\[
\bar Z_{i,m}=F^{-1}(\bar X_{i,m}),
\qquad
Z_m=F^{-1}(X_m).
\]

The first sampling term controls residual feedback. The second controls the empirical \(\psi\)-avg directly.

For complete trajectories, define

\[
\Gamma_i
=
(p_i,\bar Z_{i,0},\ldots,\bar Z_{i,M}),
\]

and

\[
\Gamma=(P,Z_0,\ldots,Z_M).
\]

The \(\Gamma_i\) are independent copies of \(\Gamma\). Therefore, for every integrable trajectory observable \(\Psi\),

\[
\frac1n\sum_i\Psi(\Gamma_i)
\longrightarrow
\mathbb E[\Psi(\Gamma)].
\]

Independence is across complete particle trajectories, not across time within one trajectory.

---

## 5. Pop Euler versus pop flow

The pop flow is

\[
\dot X(t)=-P r(t),
\qquad
r(t)=\mathbb E[P\phi(F^{-1}(X(t)))]-y.
\]

Write

\[
X(t)=X_0-Ps(t).
\]

Then

\[
\frac{ds(t)}{dt}
=
\mathbb E\!\left[
P\phi(F^{-1}(X_0-Ps(t)))
\right]-y.
\]

The right-hand side is bounded and 1-Lipschitz in \(s\), so this scalar ODE has a unique global solution. Therefore the pop flow is well-defined and autonomous.

Pop Euler is ordinary Euler for this same equation. Its error satisfies

\[
\max_{m\delta\le T}|s_m-s(m\delta)|
\le
\frac R2(e^T-1)\delta,
\qquad
R=\frac\pi2+|y|.
\]

Because

\[
X_m=X_0-Ps_m,
\qquad
X(t)=X_0-Ps(t),
\]

the same estimate controls \(X_m-X(m\delta)\). Including the movement between grid points gives

\[
\sup_{t\le T}
|X^{\mathrm{pop\ Euler}}(t)-X^{\mathrm{pop\ flow}}(t)|
\le
C_T\delta.
\]

---

## 6. Add the five errors

For any time \(t\),

\[
\begin{aligned}
&
\left|
\frac1n\sum_i\psi(p_i,z_i^{\mathrm{GD}}(t))
-
\mathbb E[\psi(P,Z(t))]
\right|
\\
&\le
|\text{finite GD avg}-\text{finite flow avg}|
&&\text{(actual step)}
\\
&\quad+
|\text{finite flow avg}-\text{finite Euler avg}|
&&\text{(finite mesh)}
\\
&\quad+
|\text{finite Euler avg}-\text{oracle avg}|
&&\text{(feedback)}
\\
&\quad+
|\text{oracle avg}-\text{pop Euler avg}|
&&\text{(sampling of }\psi\text{)}
\\
&\quad+
|\text{pop Euler avg}-\text{pop flow avg}|
&&\text{(pop mesh)}.
\end{aligned}
\]

If \(\psi\) is \(L_\psi\)-Lipschitz in \(z\), the five terms are bounded by

\[
L_\psi C_T
\left(
\eta_n+\eta_n\sqrt n+\eta_n^2n
\right),
\]

\[
L_\psi C_T\delta,
\]

\[
L_\psi(e^T-1)\pi
\sqrt{
\frac{\log(4(M+1)/\alpha)}{2n}
},
\]

\[
B_\psi
\sqrt{
\frac{\log(4(M+1)/\alpha)}{2n}
},
\]

and

\[
L_\psi C_T\delta.
\]

Therefore, with probability at least \(1-\alpha\),

\[
\begin{aligned}
&
\sup_{t\le T}
\left|
\frac1n\sum_i\psi(p_i,z_i^{\mathrm{GD}}(t))
-
\mathbb E[\psi(P,Z(t))]
\right|
\\
&\le
L_\psi C_T
\left(
\eta_n+\eta_n\sqrt n+\eta_n^2n
\right)
\\
&\quad+
2L_\psi C_T\delta
\\
&\quad+
\left[
L_\psi(e^T-1)\pi+B_\psi
\right]
\sqrt{
\frac{\log(4(M+1)/\alpha)}{2n}
}.
\end{aligned}
\]

Now:

1. Choose \(\delta\) small enough to control both mesh errors.
2. Keep \(\delta\) fixed and take \(n\to\infty\).
3. The learning-rate term vanishes because

   \[
   \eta_n\sqrt n\to0.
   \]

4. The two sampling terms vanish because \(M\) is fixed.
5. Finally let \(\delta\to0\).

This proves uniform compact-time convergence in probability.

The theorem in the audited document fixes

\[
\eta_n=n^{-2},
\]

which satisfies

\[
\eta_n\sqrt n=n^{-3/2}\to0.
\]

More generally, the displayed proof estimate closes for every learning-rate sequence satisfying

\[
\eta_n=o(n^{-1/2}).
\]

\]

not \(\eta_n=o(\sqrt n)\).

\]

becomes exactly

\[

\]

\[
v_i=-p_i r_n.
\]

One GD step is

\[
z_i^+=z_i+\eta_nq(z_i)v_i.
\]

Since \(F\) is cubic,

\[
\begin{aligned}
F(z_i^+)
={}&F(z_i)+\eta_n v_i
\\
&+\eta_n^2z_iq(z_i)^2v_i^2
+\frac{\eta_n^3}{3}q(z_i)^3v_i^3.
\end{aligned}
\]

In the toy system, \(|v_i|\) is uniformly bounded, so the accumulated GD-to-flow error is \(O(\eta_n)\).

The full neural-network proof has a backpropagated field whose normalized norm is bounded, but whose largest coordinate may be \(\sqrt n\) times larger. Its one-step coordinate defect is therefore bounded by

\[
C_T\left(\eta_n^2\sqrt n+\eta_n^3n\right).
\]

There is also an ordinary ODE local defect \(O_T(\eta_n^2)\). Over approximately \(T/\eta_n\) steps, stability gives

\[
\sup_{t\le T}
d_n\!\left(
\theta_n^{\mathrm{GD}}(t),
\theta_n^{\mathrm{flow}}(t)
\right)
\le
C_T\left(
\eta_n+\eta_n\sqrt n+\eta_n^2n
\right).
\]

Thus the first approximation vanishes under

\[
\eta_n\sqrt n\to0.
\]

For the toy model this condition is stronger than necessary, but it makes the consolidated statement compatible with the full proof.

---

## 2. Finite flow versus coarse finite Euler

Choose an auxiliary proof mesh \(\delta>0\), independent of \(\eta_n\).

Finite transformed Euler is

\[
X^n_{i,m+1}
=
X^n_{i,m}-\delta p_i r^n_m,
\]

where

\[
r^n_m
=
\frac1n\sum_j
p_j\phi\!\left(F^{-1}(X^n_{j,m})\right)-y.
\]

The transformed vector field is bounded and Lipschitz. Ordinary Euler analysis therefore gives

\[
\sup_{t\le T}
\max_i
|X_i^{n,\mathrm{flow}}(t)-X_i^{n,\mathrm{Euler}}(t)|
\le
C_T\delta,
\]

uniformly in \(n\).

Thus

\[
\text{raw GD}
\approx
\text{finite Euler}
\]

with error at most

\[
C_T\left(
\eta_n+\eta_n\sqrt n+\eta_n^2n+\delta
\right).
\]

The coarse scheme has only

\[
M=\left\lceil\frac T\delta\right\rceil
\]

updates, which is fixed while \(n\to\infty\).

---

## 3. Pop Euler and the finite oracle

Pop Euler is

\[
r_m
=
\mathbb E\!\left[
P\phi(F^{-1}(X_m))
\right]-y,
\]

\[
X_{m+1}=X_m-\delta P r_m.
\]

The finite oracle starts from the same finite initialization as finite Euler but uses the pop residual:

\[
\bar X_{i,m+1}
=
\bar X_{i,m}-\delta p_i r_m.
\]

Its empirical residual is

\[
\bar r^n_m
=
\frac1n\sum_i
p_i\phi(F^{-1}(\bar X_{i,m}))-y,
\]

but the oracle does not use this number in its updates.

Let

\[
E_m=\max_i|X^n_{i,m}-\bar X_{i,m}|.
\]

Subtracting the finite-Euler and oracle updates gives

\[
E_{m+1}
\le
E_m+\delta|r^n_m-r_m|.
\]

Insert \(\bar r^n_m\):

\[
|r^n_m-r_m|
\le
|r^n_m-\bar r^n_m|
+
|\bar r^n_m-r_m|.
\]

Because \(\phi\circ F^{-1}\) is 1-Lipschitz,

\[
|r^n_m-\bar r^n_m|\le E_m.
\]

Therefore

\[
E_{m+1}
\le
(1+\delta)E_m
+
\delta|\bar r^n_m-r_m|.
\]

Since \(E_0=0\),

\[
\max_{m\le M}E_m
\le
(e^T-1)
\max_{m\le M}|\bar r^n_m-r_m|.
\]

Thus finite Euler is close to the oracle whenever the oracle’s residual sampling error is small.

---

## 4. Oracle sampling versus pop Euler

The pop residual sequence

\[
r_0,\ldots,r_{M-1}
\]

is deterministic once the initial population law is fixed.

Each complete oracle trajectory is therefore a deterministic function of its own initialization:

\[
(p_i,\bar X_{i,0})
\longmapsto
(p_i,\bar X_{i,0},\ldots,\bar X_{i,M}).
\]

Hence the complete oracle trajectories are independent copies of the pop Euler trajectory.

At every step \(m\),

\[
p_i\phi(F^{-1}(\bar X_{i,m}))
\]

are independent copies of

\[
P\phi(F^{-1}(X_m)).
\]

Hoeffding’s inequality and a union bound give, with probability at least \(1-\alpha/2\),

\[
\max_{m\le M}|\bar r^n_m-r_m|
\le
\pi
\sqrt{
\frac{\log(4(M+1)/\alpha)}{2n}
}.
\]

Consequently,

\[
\max_{m\le M}E_m
\le
(e^T-1)\pi
\sqrt{
\frac{\log(4(M+1)/\alpha)}{2n}
}.
\]

There is also a separate sampling error for the chosen observable \(\psi\). Let

\[
B_\psi=\sup\psi-\inf\psi.
\]

With probability at least \(1-\alpha/2\),

\[
\max_{m\le M}
\left|
\frac1n\sum_i\psi(p_i,\bar Z_{i,m})
-
\mathbb E[\psi(P,Z_m)]
\right|
\le
B_\psi
\sqrt{
\frac{\log(4(M+1)/\alpha)}{2n}
},
\]

where

\[
\bar Z_{i,m}=F^{-1}(\bar X_{i,m}),
\qquad
Z_m=F^{-1}(X_m).
\]

The first sampling term controls residual feedback. The second controls the empirical \(\psi\)-avg directly.

For complete trajectories, define

\[
\Gamma_i
=
(p_i,\bar Z_{i,0},\ldots,\bar Z_{i,M}),
\]

and

\[
\Gamma=(P,Z_0,\ldots,Z_M).
\]

The \(\Gamma_i\) are independent copies of \(\Gamma\). Therefore, for every integrable trajectory observable \(\Psi\),

\[
\frac1n\sum_i\Psi(\Gamma_i)
\longrightarrow
\mathbb E[\Psi(\Gamma)].
\]

Independence is across complete particle trajectories, not across time within one trajectory.

---

## 5. Pop Euler versus pop flow

The pop flow is

\[
\dot X(t)=-P r(t),
\qquad
r(t)=\mathbb E[P\phi(F^{-1}(X(t)))]-y.
\]

Write

\[
X(t)=X_0-Ps(t).
\]

Then

\[
\frac{ds(t)}{dt}
=
\mathbb E\!\left[
P\phi(F^{-1}(X_0-Ps(t)))
\right]-y.
\]

The right-hand side is bounded and 1-Lipschitz in \(s\), so this scalar ODE has a unique global solution. Therefore the pop flow is well-defined and autonomous.

Pop Euler is ordinary Euler for this same equation. Its error satisfies

\[
\max_{m\delta\le T}|s_m-s(m\delta)|
\le
\frac R2(e^T-1)\delta,
\qquad
R=\frac\pi2+|y|.
\]

Because

\[
X_m=X_0-Ps_m,
\qquad
X(t)=X_0-Ps(t),
\]

the same estimate controls \(X_m-X(m\delta)\). Including the movement between grid points gives

\[
\sup_{t\le T}
|X^{\mathrm{pop\ Euler}}(t)-X^{\mathrm{pop\ flow}}(t)|
\le
C_T\delta.
\]

---

## 6. Add the five errors

For any time \(t\),

\[
\begin{aligned}
&
\left|
\frac1n\sum_i\psi(p_i,z_i^{\mathrm{GD}}(t))
-
\mathbb E[\psi(P,Z(t))]
\right|
\\
&\le
|\text{finite GD avg}-\text{finite flow avg}|
&&\text{(actual step)}
\\
&\quad+
|\text{finite flow avg}-\text{finite Euler avg}|
&&\text{(finite mesh)}
\\
&\quad+
|\text{finite Euler avg}-\text{oracle avg}|
&&\text{(feedback)}
\\
&\quad+
|\text{oracle avg}-\text{pop Euler avg}|
&&\text{(sampling of }\psi\text{)}
\\
&\quad+
|\text{pop Euler avg}-\text{pop flow avg}|
&&\text{(pop mesh)}.
\end{aligned}
\]

If \(\psi\) is \(L_\psi\)-Lipschitz in \(z\), the five terms are bounded by

\[
L_\psi C_T
\left(
\eta_n+\eta_n\sqrt n+\eta_n^2n
\right),
\]

\[
L_\psi C_T\delta,
\]

\[
L_\psi(e^T-1)\pi
\sqrt{
\frac{\log(4(M+1)/\alpha)}{2n}
},
\]

\[
B_\psi
\sqrt{
\frac{\log(4(M+1)/\alpha)}{2n}
},
\]

and

\[
L_\psi C_T\delta.
\]

Therefore, with probability at least \(1-\alpha\),

\[
\begin{aligned}
&
\sup_{t\le T}
\left|
\frac1n\sum_i\psi(p_i,z_i^{\mathrm{GD}}(t))
-
\mathbb E[\psi(P,Z(t))]
\right|
\\
&\le
L_\psi C_T
\left(
\eta_n+\eta_n\sqrt n+\eta_n^2n
\right)
\\
&\quad+
2L_\psi C_T\delta
\\
&\quad+
\left[
L_\psi(e^T-1)\pi+B_\psi
\right]
\sqrt{
\frac{\log(4(M+1)/\alpha)}{2n}
}.
\end{aligned}
\]

Now:

1. Choose \(\delta\) small enough to control both mesh errors.
2. Keep \(\delta\) fixed and take \(n\to\infty\).
3. The learning-rate term vanishes because

   \[
   \eta_n\sqrt n\to0.
   \]

4. The two sampling terms vanish because \(M\) is fixed.
5. Finally let \(\delta\to0\).

This proves uniform compact-time convergence in probability.

The theorem in the audited document fixes

\[
\eta_n=n^{-2},
\]

which satisfies

\[
\eta_n\sqrt n=n^{-3/2}\to0.
\]

More generally, the displayed proof estimate closes for every learning-rate sequence satisfying

\[
\eta_n=o(n^{-1/2}).
\]

[50 | 2026-08-31T14:49:32.068Z | assistant | None]
Yes—your broad picture is essentially right. The precise replacement for the toy argument is:

\[
\boxed{
\text{Hoeffding for i.i.d. oracle particles}
\quad\rightsquigarrow\quad
\text{a law of large numbers for a finite computation reusing }
W_0^{(2)}
\text{ and }
(W_0^{(2)})^\top.
}
\]

That theorem controls several empirical averages instead of only the residual. Those errors then enter almost exactly the same stability-and-accumulation argument as in the toy model.

The important corrections are:

- Reusing a matrix is harmless if the vectors multiplied by it are independent of that matrix.
- Reusing it on vectors produced using the same matrix destroys literal i.i.d. structure, even without a transpose.
- The transpose creates an additional, stronger effect: deterministic response terms that would be completely missed by pretending each multiplication used fresh Gaussian randomness.
- In the limit, only empirical scalar averages become deterministic. The neuron vectors themselves remain random population fields.

Let us build this carefully.

## Where the easy i.i.d. argument breaks

Let \(W\in\mathbb R^{n\times n}\) have independent entries \(N(0,1/n)\), and first suppose that \(v\in\mathbb R^n\) is independent of \(W\). Then

\[
(Wv)_i=\sum_{j=1}^n W_{ij}v_j.
\]

Conditional on \(v\), different rows of \(W\) are independent, so

\[
(Wv)_1,\ldots,(Wv)_n
\]

are independent Gaussians with common variance

\[
\frac1n\sum_jv_j^2.
\]

Even several uses

\[
Wv^1,\ldots,Wv^k
\]

are easy if all \(v^1,\ldots,v^k\) are independent of \(W\). For each row \(i\), the tuple

\[
\bigl((Wv^1)_i,\ldots,(Wv^k)_i\bigr)
\]

is Gaussian, and these tuples are independent across \(i\). The calls are correlated with one another through

\[
\frac1n(v^a)^\top v^b,
\]

but this is still an ordinary rowwise i.i.d. situation.

The problem begins when the next vector being multiplied already depends on \(W\). For example,

\[
y=Wv,\qquad u=W\phi(y).
\]

Now

\[
u_i = \sum_jW_{ij}\phi\!\left(\sum_kW_{jk}v_k\right).
\]

The coefficient multiplying \(W_{ij}\) is itself a function of the same matrix. Thus we cannot condition on \(\phi(y)\) and continue treating \(W\) as an untouched independent Gaussian matrix.

The linear example makes this visible:

\[
u=W^2v,
\]

so

\[
u_i=\sum_{j,k}W_{ij}W_{jk}v_k.
\]

Different \(u_i\)'s contain overlapping entries of \(W\). They are not independent finite-width samples. Therefore an ordinary statement such as

\[
\frac1n\sum_i\psi(u_i) \longrightarrow \mathbb E[\psi(U)]
\]

cannot be justified by directly saying “the \(u_i\) are i.i.d.” They are not.

Same-direction reuse is nevertheless simpler than transpose reuse. If the history only contains calls

\[
Wv^1,\ldots,Wv^k,
\]

decompose a new input into

\[
v=v_{\parallel}+v_{\perp},
\]

where \(v_{\parallel}\) lies in the span of \(v^1,\ldots,v^k\). Then

\[
Wv=Wv_{\parallel}+Wv_{\perp}.
\]

The first term is a known linear combination of the earlier outputs. After conditioning on the previous calls, the second term is still Gaussian randomness in a previously unused input direction. Thus same-direction reuse produces

\[
\boxed{
\text{known combination of previous outputs}
+
\text{new Gaussian innovation}.
}
\]

It is no longer the elementary toy-model i.i.d. argument, but Gaussian conditioning still has a relatively simple form.

Transpose reuse produces something stronger. Take

\[
y=Wv,\qquad u=W^\top y=W^\top Wv.
\]

Coordinate \(j\) is

\[
\begin{aligned}
u_j
&=
\sum_iW_{ij}\sum_kW_{ik}v_k\\
&=
v_j\sum_iW_{ij}^2
+
\sum_{k\ne j}v_k\sum_iW_{ij}W_{ik}.
\end{aligned}
\]

Because

\[
\sum_iW_{ij}^2\longrightarrow1,
\]

the first term approaches \(v_j\). Thus the backward call contains a systematic response aligned with the original input:

\[
W^\top Wv = v+\text{Gaussian-like fluctuation}.
\]

If we incorrectly replaced \(W^\top\) by a fresh independent matrix, this \(v\) term would disappear. This is why transpose reuse is not merely “some dependence that should wash out.” It changes the limiting formula.

More generally, after observing previous calls in both directions, Gaussian conditioning gives

\[
W\mid\text{previous calls}
\;\overset{d}{=}\;
W_{\mathrm{determined}}
+
\Pi_{\mathrm{unused\ left}}\,
\widetilde W\,
\Pi_{\mathrm{unused\ right}},
\]

where:

- \(W_{\mathrm{determined}}\) is the part forced by all previous forward and backward calls;
- \(\widetilde W\) is an independent Gaussian matrix;
- the projections restrict it to directions that have not yet been observed.

Applying this decomposition to the next vector produces

\[
\boxed{
\text{response determined by previous calls}
+
\text{Gaussian innovation in the remaining directions}.
}
\]

In the population description, that innovation can be represented by fresh Gaussian variables—but only after retaining every response term and the correct joint covariance between innovations. It is not valid to replace every new matrix call by an unrelated fresh Gaussian.

At finite width, the coordinate tuples are therefore dependent. The reused-Gaussian theorem proves the weaker but exactly sufficient conclusion that, for any fixed finite list of computed vectors,

\[
\frac1n\sum_{i=1}^n
\psi\!\left(v_{n,i}^1,\ldots,v_{n,i}^k\right)
\longrightarrow
\mathbb E\,
\psi\!\left(V^1,\ldots,V^k\right).
\]

So the limiting coordinates behave empirically as samples from a joint law, even though the finite coordinates are not literally i.i.d. Within one limiting coordinate, \(V^1,\ldots,V^k\) are generally strongly correlated.

## Why the learned matrix can be reduced to its initialization

At Euler step \(m\), the learned middle matrix has the exact finite-rank form

\[
W_m^{(2)}
=
W_0^{(2)}
-
\frac{2\delta}{n}
\sum_{\ell<m}
r_{n,\ell}
\left[
W_\ell^{(3)}
\odot
\phi'(z_\ell^{(2)})
\right]
\phi(z_\ell^{(1)})^\top.
\]

Thus all genuinely high-dimensional Gaussian randomness remains inside the single initialization matrix \(W_0^{(2)}\). Everything learned afterward is a finite sum of rank-one corrections.

When this matrix acts forward,

\[
\begin{aligned}
W_m^{(2)}\phi(z_m^{(1)})
={}&
W_0^{(2)}\phi(z_m^{(1)})
\\
&-
2\delta
\sum_{\ell<m}
r_{n,\ell}
\left[
W_\ell^{(3)}
\odot
\phi'(z_\ell^{(2)})
\right]
\left\{
\frac1n
\sum_i
\phi(z_{\ell,i}^{(1)})
\phi(z_{m,i}^{(1)})
\right\}.
\end{aligned}
\]

The rank-one corrections enter through scalar empirical overlaps

\[
\frac1n
\sum_i
\phi(z_{\ell,i}^{(1)})
\phi(z_{m,i}^{(1)}).
\]

The transpose action similarly becomes

\[
\begin{aligned}
&
(W_m^{(2)})^\top
\left[
W_m^{(3)}
\odot
\phi'(z_m^{(2)})
\right]
\\
&=
(W_0^{(2)})^\top
\left[
W_m^{(3)}
\odot
\phi'(z_m^{(2)})
\right]
\\
&\quad-
2\delta
\sum_{\ell<m}
r_{n,\ell}\,
\phi(z_\ell^{(1)})
\\
&\qquad\qquad\cdot
\left\{
\frac1n
\sum_j
\left[
W_{\ell,j}^{(3)}
\phi'(z_{\ell,j}^{(2)})
\right]
\left[
W_{m,j}^{(3)}
\phi'(z_{m,j}^{(2)})
\right]
\right\}.
\end{aligned}
\]

So the neural network has three main families of global empirical numbers:

\[
\frac1n\sum_j
W_{m,j}^{(3)}
\phi(z_{m,j}^{(2)})
\]

for the residual,

\[
\frac1n\sum_i
\phi(z_{\ell,i}^{(1)})
\phi(z_{m,i}^{(1)})
\]

for forward reuse of \(W^{(2)}\), and

\[
\frac1n\sum_j
\left[
W_{\ell,j}^{(3)}
\phi'(z_{\ell,j}^{(2)})
\right]
\left[
W_{m,j}^{(3)}
\phi'(z_{m,j}^{(2)})
\right]
\]

for backward reuse of \((W^{(2)})^\top\).

This is the precise generalization of the toy residual. The rank-one vectors do not become deterministic. The scalar empirical averages multiplying those vectors become deterministic population expectations.

## The exact boundary between the finite oracle and population Euler

The population Euler trajectory is defined first. At each step it supplies the deterministic numbers

\[
r_m
=
\mathbb E\!\left[
W_m^{(3)}
\phi(z_m^{(2)})
\right]-1,
\]

\[
\mathbb E\!\left[
\phi(z_\ell^{(1)})
\phi(z_m^{(1)})
\right],
\]

and

\[
\mathbb E\!\left[
W_\ell^{(3)}
\phi'(z_\ell^{(2)})
W_m^{(3)}
\phi'(z_m^{(2)})
\right].
\]

The finite oracle keeps:

- the finite random initialization \(W_0^{(2)}\);
- its actual transpose \((W_0^{(2)})^\top\);
- finite vectors \(\bar z_{n,m}^{(1)},\bar z_{n,m}^{(2)},\bar W_{n,m}^{(3)}\);
- all coordinatewise activations and derivatives.

But it replaces the global empirical feedback numbers above by their population values.

For example, its second-layer preactivation is defined by

\[
\begin{aligned}
\bar z_{n,m}^{(2)}
={}&
W_0^{(2)}
\phi(\bar z_{n,m}^{(1)})
\\
&-
2\delta
\sum_{\ell<m}
r_\ell
\left[
\bar W_{n,\ell}^{(3)}
\odot
\phi'(\bar z_{n,\ell}^{(2)})
\right]
\mathbb E\!\left[
\phi(z_\ell^{(1)})
\phi(z_m^{(1)})
\right].
\end{aligned}
\]

The vector used to update the transformed first-layer state is

\[
\begin{aligned}
&
(W_0^{(2)})^\top
\left[
\bar W_{n,m}^{(3)}
\odot
\phi'(\bar z_{n,m}^{(2)})
\right]
\\
&\quad-
2\delta
\sum_{\ell<m}
r_\ell
\phi(\bar z_{n,\ell}^{(1)})
\\
&\qquad\qquad\cdot
\mathbb E\!\left[
W_\ell^{(3)}
\phi'(z_\ell^{(2)})
W_m^{(3)}
\phi'(z_m^{(2)})
\right].
\end{aligned}
\]

The oracle then uses this finite vector in

\[
\bar x_{n,m+1}
=
\bar x_{n,m}
-
2\delta r_m
\left(
\text{the complete vector displayed above}
\right),
\]

and updates

\[
\bar W_{n,m+1}^{(3)}
=
\bar W_{n,m}^{(3)}
-
2\delta r_m
\phi(\bar z_{n,m}^{(2)}).
\]

So your proposed rule is close, but the exact rule is

\[
\boxed{
\text{The oracle borrows the global scalar quantities that are fed back into later computations.}
}
\]

It does not replace every scalar ever computed. Empirical scalars used only for checking convergence can remain empirical. What must be removed from the oracle recursion are the width-dependent empirical feedback coefficients.

There is one subtle but important consequence. Define the oracle’s finite rank-memory matrix by

\[
\bar W_{n,m}^{(2)}
=
W_0^{(2)}
-
\frac{2\delta}{n}
\sum_{\ell<m}
r_\ell
\left[
\bar W_{n,\ell}^{(3)}
\odot
\phi'(\bar z_{n,\ell}^{(2)})
\right]
\phi(\bar z_{n,\ell}^{(1)})^\top.
\]

Then \(\bar z_{n,m}^{(2)}\) is not exactly equal to

\[
\bar W_{n,m}^{(2)}
\phi(\bar z_{n,m}^{(1)}),
\]

because exact multiplication by the finite rank-memory matrix produces an empirical overlap, while the oracle definition uses the population overlap. Their difference is exactly

\[
\begin{aligned}
&
\bar W_{n,m}^{(2)}
\phi(\bar z_{n,m}^{(1)})
-
\bar z_{n,m}^{(2)}
\\
&=
-2\delta
\sum_{\ell<m}
r_\ell
\left[
\bar W_{n,\ell}^{(3)}
\odot
\phi'(\bar z_{n,\ell}^{(2)})
\right]
\\
&\qquad\cdot
\left[
\frac1n
\sum_i
\phi(\bar z_{n,\ell,i}^{(1)})
\phi(\bar z_{n,m,i}^{(1)})
-
\mathbb E\!\left[
\phi(z_\ell^{(1)})
\phi(z_m^{(1)})
\right]
\right].
\end{aligned}
\]

There is an analogous discrepancy for the transpose action, involving the empirical-minus-population backward overlap.

This is where the sampling errors appear explicitly. Nothing has been silently equated.

## What replaces Hoeffding, and what replaces the toy \(E_m\)

Fix \(\delta>0\) and a time horizon \(T\). Then

\[
M=\left\lceil\frac{T}{\delta}\right\rceil
\]

is fixed while \(n\to\infty\). There are therefore only finitely many oracle vectors and finitely many empirical quantities to control.

Call the largest relevant oracle discrepancy \(\varepsilon_n\). Concretely, it is the maximum over \(\ell,m\le M\) of

\[
\left|
\frac1n\sum_j
\bar W_{n,m,j}^{(3)}
\phi(\bar z_{n,m,j}^{(2)})
-
\mathbb E\!\left[
W_m^{(3)}
\phi(z_m^{(2)})
\right]
\right|,
\]

\[
\left|
\frac1n\sum_i
\phi(\bar z_{n,\ell,i}^{(1)})
\phi(\bar z_{n,m,i}^{(1)})
-
\mathbb E\!\left[
\phi(z_\ell^{(1)})
\phi(z_m^{(1)})
\right]
\right|,
\]

\[
\begin{aligned}
\left|
\frac1n\sum_j
\left[
\bar W_{n,\ell,j}^{(3)}
\phi'(\bar z_{n,\ell,j}^{(2)})
\right]
\left[
\bar W_{n,m,j}^{(3)}
\phi'(\bar z_{n,m,j}^{(2)})
\right]
-
\mathbb E\!\left[
W_\ell^{(3)}
\phi'(z_\ell^{(2)})
W_m^{(3)}
\phi'(z_m^{(2)})
\right]
\right|,
\end{aligned}
\]

together with a finite list of squared-norm discrepancies needed to keep all stability constants bounded.

In the toy model, Hoeffding gave an explicit estimate such as

\[
\varepsilon_n
=
O_{\mathbb P}
\left(
\sqrt{\frac{\log M}{n}}
\right).
\]

Here the coordinates are neither independent nor all bounded, so that argument does not apply. Instead, the fixed-program reused-Gaussian theorem gives

\[
\boxed{
\varepsilon_n\longrightarrow0
\quad\text{almost surely}.
}
\]

This is rigorous, but it is not an explicit finite-\(n\) rate. The audited proof establishes asymptotic convergence, not a Hoeffding-style \(1/\sqrt n\) concentration bound. Because \(M\) is fixed, a rate is not needed.

The analogue of the toy cumulative state error is

\[
\begin{aligned}
E_{n,m}
={}&
\|x_{n,m}-\bar x_{n,m}\|_n
+
\|W_{n,m}^{(3)}-\bar W_{n,m}^{(3)}\|_n
\\
&+
\|W_{n,m}^{(2)}-\bar W_{n,m}^{(2)}\|_{\mathrm{op}}.
\end{aligned}
\]

This compares the actual finite Euler network with the finite oracle rank-memory state. Stability of all the forward and backward operations gives

\[
\begin{aligned}
&
\text{difference of first-layer activations}
+
\text{difference of second-layer preactivations}
\\
&\quad+
\text{difference of second-layer activations}
+
\text{difference of first-layer backpropagated vectors}
\\
&\quad+
|r_{n,m}-r_m|
\le
C\bigl(E_{n,m}+\varepsilon_n\bigr).
\end{aligned}
\]

Subtracting the actual and oracle Euler updates then gives

\[
E_{n,m+1}
\le
(1+C\delta)E_{n,m}
+
C\delta\varepsilon_n.
\]

This is precisely the neural-network version of the toy recurrence. Discrete Gronwall gives

\[
\max_{m\le M}E_{n,m}
\le
C_{T,\delta}
\left(
E_{n,0}+\varepsilon_n
\right)
\longrightarrow0.
\]

The very small random initialization of \(W^{(3)}\), which has population limit zero, is included in \(E_{n,0}\).

Meanwhile, the reused-Gaussian theorem directly identifies the oracle with population Euler:

\[
\frac1n\sum_i
\psi\!\left(
\bar v_{n,i}^{1},\ldots,\bar v_{n,i}^{k}
\right)
\longrightarrow
\mathbb E\,
\psi\!\left(
V^1,\ldots,V^k
\right)
\]

for every fixed finite collection of oracle vectors and every permitted test function \(\psi\).

Thus the entire bridge is

\[
\boxed{
\begin{array}{c}
\text{reused-Gaussian law of large numbers}
\\[-1mm]
\Downarrow
\\[-1mm]
\varepsilon_n\to0
\\[-1mm]
\Downarrow
\\[-1mm]
E_{n,m+1}
\le
(1+C\delta)E_{n,m}
+
C\delta\varepsilon_n
\\[-1mm]
\Downarrow
\\[-1mm]
\text{actual finite Euler}
\longrightarrow
\text{population Euler}.
\end{array}
}
\]

So your induction picture is right after two refinements:

1. The induction makes empirical averages and projection coefficients deterministic; it does not make the population vectors deterministic.
2. “Fresh Gaussian” means the unexplored part of the same initial matrix after conditioning on its past actions. It must be added jointly with all deterministic response terms and with the correct correlations between different calls.

That is the exact neural-network counterpart of “oracle i.i.d. sampling + Hoeffding + discrete Gronwall” in the toy model.

[61 | 2026-08-31T15:30:33.147Z | assistant | None]
Your picture has the right backbone—finite-rank memory plus induction over a fixed computation—but it also mixes together three mechanisms that the proof keeps sharply separate: deterministic population feedback, reused-Gaussian conditioning, and stability of the actual scheme around the oracle. [148 | 2026-08-31T15:35:07.669Z | assistant | None]
Your structural picture is substantially correct. The essential idea really is:

\[
\boxed{
\text{learned matrix}
=
\text{initial Gaussian matrix}
+
\text{finite-rank memory of earlier updates}.
}
\]

But the places where you say “therefore the coordinates are i.i.d.” or “obviously the Gaussian matrix produces i.i.d. rows” are exactly where the hard theorem is needed. After training has begun, those statements are generally false. The correct replacement is convergence of empirical joint distributions, without finite-width independence.

Fix an auxiliary Euler step \(\Delta>0\). Then the number of steps before time \(T\),

\[
N=\left\lceil\frac{T}{\Delta}\right\rceil,
\]

is fixed while \(n\to\infty\). Define

\[
h_k^{(1)}=\phi(z_k^{(1)})
\]

and the second-layer backpropagated vector

\[
\delta_k^{(2)}
=
\overline W_k^{(3)}
\odot\phi'(z_k^{(2)}).
\]

At the population level, the middle-layer weight after \(k\) Euler updates is

\[
W_k^{(2)}
=
W_0^{(2)}
-
2\Delta
\sum_{s<k}
r_s\,
\delta_s^{(2)}
\otimes h_s^{(1)}.
\]

Thus its forward action is

\[
z_k^{(2)}
=
W_0^{(2)}h_k^{(1)}
-
2\Delta
\sum_{s<k}
r_s\delta_s^{(2)}
\mathbb E\!\left[
h_s^{(1)}h_k^{(1)}
\right],
\]

while its backward action is

\[
p_k^{(1)}
=
\bigl(W_0^{(2)}\bigr)^*
\delta_k^{(2)}
-
2\Delta
\sum_{s<k}
r_sh_s^{(1)}
\mathbb E\!\left[
\delta_s^{(2)}\delta_k^{(2)}
\right].
\]

So your “initial matrix times the new vector, plus old vectors multiplied by scalar overlaps” picture is exactly right.

The key question is where those scalar overlaps come from in the finite oracle. The answer is sharper than your tentative picture: every scalar that is fed back into the oracle recursion is supplied as an exact population value. This includes

\[
r_s,
\qquad
\mathbb E[h_s^{(1)}h_k^{(1)}],
\qquad
\mathbb E[\delta_s^{(2)}\delta_k^{(2)}],
\]

including overlaps involving the current step \(k\). None of their finite empirical versions is used to drive the oracle.

The population Euler trajectory is constructed first. Its law is deterministic, so all these expectations are fixed real numbers. The finite oracle then uses those numbers as hard-coded coefficients while retaining the actual finite random initialization

\[
W_{n,0}^{(2)}
\]

and the actual transpose

\[
\bigl(W_{n,0}^{(2)}\bigr)^\top.
\]

For example, its second-layer preactivation is defined as

\[
\begin{aligned}
\bar z_{n,k}^{(2)}
={}&
W_{n,0}^{(2)}\bar h_{n,k}^{(1)}
\\
&-
2\Delta
\sum_{s<k}
r_s\bar\delta_{n,s}^{(2)}
\mathbb E\!\left[
h_s^{(1)}h_k^{(1)}
\right].
\end{aligned}
\]

The oracle’s vectors are still random. Only the global scalar feedback coefficients have been made deterministic.

This gives a clean answer to your “supplied versus proved” question:

- The population residuals and population overlap tables are supplied to the oracle.
- The tensor theorem then proves that the corresponding empirical statistics of the oracle actually converge back to those supplied population values.
- A separate stability argument proves that replacing the actual finite network’s empirical feedback by those population values changes the trajectory by a vanishing amount.

So the population quantities are not assumed to equal the finite empirical quantities. They are used to define a reference process, and their consistency is subsequently verified.

There is even a deliberately exposed mismatch. Define the oracle’s finite-rank memory matrix by

\[
\bar W_{n,k}^{(2)}
=
W_{n,0}^{(2)}
-
2\Delta
\sum_{s<k}
r_s\,
\bar\delta_{n,s}^{(2)}
\otimes_n
\bar h_{n,s}^{(1)},
\]

where

\[
u\otimes_n v=\frac1nuv^\top.
\]

Then the oracle node \(\bar z_{n,k}^{(2)}\) is not exactly equal to

\[
\bar W_{n,k}^{(2)}\bar h_{n,k}^{(1)}.
\]

Their exact difference is

\[
\begin{aligned}
&
\bar W_{n,k}^{(2)}
\bar h_{n,k}^{(1)}
-
\bar z_{n,k}^{(2)}
\\
&=
-2\Delta
\sum_{s<k}
r_s\bar\delta_{n,s}^{(2)}
\left[
\left\langle
\bar h_{n,s}^{(1)},
\bar h_{n,k}^{(1)}
\right\rangle_n
-
\mathbb E[h_s^{(1)}h_k^{(1)}]
\right].
\end{aligned}
\]

Thus the empirical-minus-population overlap error is displayed explicitly. It is not silently set to zero. There is an analogous formula for the backward action.

The main correction to your reasoning concerns i.i.d.-ness. At initialization, if \(v\) is independent of \(W_{n,0}^{(2)}\), then conditional on \(v\), the coordinates of

\[
W_{n,0}^{(2)}v
\]

are indeed independent Gaussians across rows. But after one or more forward and backward calls, a new vector \(v\) generally depends on the same matrix \(W_{n,0}^{(2)}\). Then one cannot condition on \(v\) while continuing to regard the matrix as untouched independent noise.

For example,

\[
\bigl(W_{n,0}^{(2)}\bigr)^\top
W_{n,0}^{(2)}v
\]

contains a systematic component aligned with \(v\). Replacing the transpose by a fresh independent matrix would miss that component.

The correct Gaussian statement is conditional. After recording all earlier forward and backward calls, the remaining unknown part of \(W_{n,0}^{(2)}\) is still Gaussian. A new call therefore has the form

\[
\boxed{
\text{response determined by earlier matrix uses}
+
\text{Gaussian innovation in unexplored directions}.
}
\]

The response part is essential. In the proof’s simplest backward example, the limiting coordinate has the form

\[
P=\kappa H^{(1)}+\sqrt{\nu}\,G,
\]

where \(G\) is a new standard Gaussian independent of the initial first-layer coordinate. The term

\[
\kappa H^{(1)}
\]

is the response caused by reusing the same matrix. A naive fresh-Gaussian argument would retain only \(\sqrt{\nu}G\) and obtain the wrong limit.

Consequently, the finite oracle coordinates are generally not i.i.d. They share the same reused matrix and the same training history. What the tensor theorem proves is the weaker—and sufficient—statement that for every fixed finite list of first-layer oracle vectors,

\[
\frac1n
\sum_{i=1}^n
\psi\!\left(
\bar v_{n,i}^1,\ldots,\bar v_{n,i}^m
\right)
\longrightarrow
\mathbb E\,
\psi(V^1,\ldots,V^m)
\]

almost surely, with a separate analogous statement for second-layer vectors.

This says that their empirical joint distribution converges. It does not say that the finite coordinate tuples are independent.

That distinction also repairs your argument about deterministic linear combinations. Suppose we already know that the empirical joint distribution of

\[
(v_{n,i}^1,\ldots,v_{n,i}^m)
\]

converges to the law of

\[
(V^1,\ldots,V^m).
\]

For fixed deterministic coefficients \(a_1,\ldots,a_m\),

\[
q_{n,i}
=
\sum_{j=1}^m a_jv_{n,i}^j.
\]

Then, for any permitted test function \(\psi\),

\[
\frac1n\sum_i\psi(q_{n,i})
=
\frac1n\sum_i
\psi\!\left(
\sum_j a_jv_{n,i}^j
\right)
\longrightarrow
\mathbb E\,
\psi\!\left(
\sum_j a_jV^j
\right).
\]

No independence across \(i\) was used. We only composed the already-convergent empirical joint law with a deterministic function.

The same applies to a coordinatewise nonlinearity:

\[
\frac1n\sum_i\psi(\phi(v_{n,i}))
\longrightarrow
\mathbb E[\psi(\phi(V))].
\]

Therefore:

- deterministic linear combinations are handled by empirical joint-law convergence;
- coordinatewise nonlinearities are handled by the same principle;
- normalized inner products are handled by convergence of second moments;
- new calls to \(W_{n,0}^{(2)}\) or its transpose require the reused-Gaussian theorem.

That last operation is the genuinely difficult one.

There are also two kinds of deterministic coefficients that should not be conflated. The population residuals and Gram overlaps are manually supplied to the oracle. By contrast, the response coefficients arising from Gaussian matrix reuse are produced internally by the Gaussian-conditioning theorem. They are determined by the limiting covariance structure of all earlier matrix calls; they are not separately inserted by hand.

Your CLT and tail-control intuition is stronger than what the proof actually needs. The theorem used here is not a central limit theorem: it does not estimate fluctuations at scale \(1/\sqrt n\), and it supplies no explicit convergence rate. It is a strong law of large numbers for one fixed finite computation:

\[
\text{empirical moment}
\longrightarrow
\text{population moment}
\quad\text{almost surely}.
\]

The Gaussian part arises from exact conditioning of the Gaussian initialization, not from a CLT applied to each new evolved vector.

Nor does the proof maintain a “every vector is sub-Gaussian” induction. Nonlinear coordinate maps can destroy Gaussianity and sub-Gaussian tails. For example,

\[
F(Z)=Z+\frac{Z^3}{3},
\qquad Z\sim N(0,1),
\]

is not sub-Gaussian. What the fixed-program theorem supplies is enough polynomial-moment control for every fixed finite list of program vectors and every polynomially bounded test function.

For the oracle bridge, define \(\zeta_n\) as the largest empirical-minus-population error among the finitely many residuals, overlaps, squared norms, and other moments required through step \(N\). The theorem gives

\[
\zeta_n\longrightarrow0
\qquad\text{almost surely}.
\]

No rate is needed because \(N\) is fixed. The actual finite Euler network and the oracle satisfy a deterministic stability recurrence

\[
E_{n,k+1}
\le
(1+C\Delta)E_{n,k}
+
C\Delta\zeta_n.
\]

After at most \(N\) steps,

\[
\max_{k\le N}E_{n,k}
\le
C_{T,\Delta}
\bigl(E_{n,0}+\zeta_n\bigr)
\longrightarrow0.
\]

This is the only quantitative accumulation estimate required for this bridge. If the number of steps \(N\) grew with \(n\), then qualitative convergence \(\zeta_n\to0\) would no longer suffice; one would need rates and control uniform in program length. That is precisely why the proof fixes \(\Delta\) before taking \(n\to\infty\).

Operator-norm bounds and the stronger square-tail bounds later needed for kernels and velocity energies are proved separately. They are not part of a hidden sub-Gaussian induction for the oracle.

Could everything be organized as one joint induction without explicitly naming an oracle? In principle, yes. One might try to show simultaneously that

\[
\text{current finite vectors converge}
\Rightarrow
\text{their empirical coefficients converge}
\Rightarrow
\text{the next finite vectors converge}.
\]

But this does not remove the real difficulty. The empirical coefficients are random functions of the same reused Gaussian matrix. The external tensor theorem used in the document only applies to a fixed computation with deterministic coefficients; it does not directly allow random empirical averages to be fed back as parameters.

A direct joint proof would therefore need either:

- a stronger theorem covering adaptive empirical feedback, or
- an internal reference process with frozen population coefficients, followed by a stability comparison.

The second option is exactly the oracle argument, whether or not it is called an oracle. Its purpose is not merely stylistic. It separates two different mathematical tasks:

\[
\boxed{
\begin{aligned}
\text{tensor theorem:}\quad&
\text{identify the joint law of a fixed deterministic-coefficient computation};
\\
\text{stability argument:}\quad&
\text{show that restoring empirical feedback changes the computation negligibly}.
\end{aligned}
}
\]

So the final verdict is:

\[
\boxed{
\begin{array}{l}
\text{Your finite-rank-memory and recursive-construction picture is correct.}\\
\text{Your replacement of dependent coordinates by literal i.i.d. samples is not.}\\
\text{Your intuition that scalar overlaps self-average is correct, but the oracle}\\
\text{hard-codes all feedback overlaps first and verifies them afterward.}\\
\text{The needed probabilistic input is a fixed-program empirical LLN with}\\
\text{Gaussian response terms—not a naive CLT or a sub-Gaussian induction.}
\end{array}}
\]

That is the precise content of the oracle bridge in [Sections 5.1–5.2](/tmp/L2_FULL_AUDITED_PROOF.md:357).
