[159 | 2026-08-31T16:17:30.437Z | assistant | None]
Exactly: the next object is appended to one growing finite collection, and the induction must say enough about that whole collection—not just about the newest vector—to justify every later nonlinear map, inner product, and reuse of \(W_0^{(2)}\) or its transpose. I’ll now unpack that induction itself, including the response-plus-new-noise calculation, rather than treating the tensor-program theorem as a black box.

[244 | 2026-08-31T16:22:24.447Z | assistant | None]
Yes: at each point we append the new vector to the collection of everything constructed earlier. But the induction is over individual computation lines—not merely over Euler time. Within one Euler step we successively construct \(h_k^{(1)}\), \(z_k^{(2)}\), \(\delta_k^{(2)}\), the backward response, and the updated parameters. Each new object becomes available to every later line.

Fix an auxiliary Euler step \(\Delta\), so there are only finitely many computation lines before time \(T\). At some intermediate line, suppose we have already constructed first-layer vectors

\[
v_n^1,\ldots,v_n^r\in\mathbb R^n
\]

and second-layer vectors

\[
u_n^1,\ldots,u_n^s\in\mathbb R^n.
\]

The induction hypothesis is the following joint statement.

For every function \(\psi\) that does not grow faster than some fixed power,

\[
\frac1n\sum_{i=1}^n
\psi\!\left(
v_{n,i}^1,\ldots,v_{n,i}^r
\right)
\longrightarrow
\mathbb E\,
\psi\!\left(
V^1,\ldots,V^r
\right)
\]

with probability one, for some limiting random tuple

\[
(V^1,\ldots,V^r).
\]

Separately, on the second-layer side,

\[
\frac1n\sum_{j=1}^n
\psi\!\left(
u_{n,j}^1,\ldots,u_{n,j}^s
\right)
\longrightarrow
\mathbb E\,
\psi\!\left(
U^1,\ldots,U^s
\right).
\]

The tuples include vectors from all previous Euler times, not just the current time. Thus the induction retains every cross-time correlation needed later.

Choosing \(\psi(x,y)=xy\) gives convergence of normalized dot products:

\[
\frac1n(v_n^a)^\top v_n^b
\longrightarrow
\mathbb E[V^aV^b].
\]

Choosing \(\psi(x)=x^{2q}\) gives the moment bounds needed to prevent rare large coordinates from spoiling later averages.

The induction also records every previous query made to the initial matrix:

\[
W_{n,0}^{(2)}v_n^a=u_n^a
\]

or

\[
\bigl(W_{n,0}^{(2)}\bigr)^\top u_n^b=v_n^b.
\]

These exact matrix relations are the information on which we condition when the same matrix is used again.

At initialization, the property follows from the ordinary law of large numbers because the initial coordinates are genuinely i.i.d. After initialization, i.i.d.-ness is no longer claimed.

Two kinds of new computation lines are easy. Suppose

\[
v_n=\sum_{a=1}^r c_av_n^a
\]

for fixed deterministic numbers \(c_a\). For any test function \(\psi\),

\[
\frac1n\sum_i\psi(v_{n,i})
=
\frac1n\sum_i
\psi\!\left(\sum_a c_av_{n,i}^a\right).
\]

The right side is simply another test of the old joint tuple. Therefore

\[
v_n
\quad\leadsto\quad
V=\sum_a c_aV^a.
\]

No independence is needed.

The same reasoning handles coordinatewise nonlinearities. If

\[
v_{n,i}
=
\phi(v_{n,i}^1,\ldots,v_{n,i}^r),
\]

then the limiting coordinate is

\[
V=\phi(V^1,\ldots,V^r).
\]

Any test of the new tuple is obtained by substituting this formula into a test of the old tuple. This is why the induction must track joint laws rather than separate marginals.

The difficult computation line is multiplication by \(W_{n,0}^{(2)}\) or its transpose. A concrete four-line example contains the whole mechanism. Begin with

\[
h_n=\phi(z_n^{(1)}),
\]

then compute

\[
z_n^{(2)}
=
W_{n,0}^{(2)}h_n,
\]

apply a coordinatewise function

\[
u_n
=
\phi(z_n^{(2)})
\odot
\phi'(z_n^{(2)}),
\]

and finally reuse the matrix backward:

\[
p_n
=
\bigl(W_{n,0}^{(2)}\bigr)^\top u_n.
\]

The initial \(h_n\) is independent of \(W_{n,0}^{(2)}\). Conditional on \(h_n\), different rows of \(W_{n,0}^{(2)}\) are independent, so

\[
z_{n,j}^{(2)}
=
\sum_i
W_{n,0,ji}^{(2)}h_{n,i}
\]

are conditionally independent Gaussians with variance

\[
m_n
=
\frac1n\sum_i h_{n,i}^2.
\]

The induction gives

\[
m_n\longrightarrow
m=\mathbb E[H^2],
\qquad
H=\phi(Z^{(1)}).
\]

Thus one limiting second-layer coordinate is

\[
Z^{(2)}\sim N(0,m).
\]

Applying the coordinatewise map gives the joint limiting pair

\[
\bigl(Z^{(2)},U\bigr)
=
\left(
Z^{(2)},
\phi(Z^{(2)})\phi'(Z^{(2)})
\right).
\]

In particular,

\[
\nu_n
=
\frac1n\sum_j u_{n,j}^2
\longrightarrow
\nu=\mathbb E[U^2],
\]

and

\[
\beta_n
=
\frac1n\sum_j z_{n,j}^{(2)}u_{n,j}
\longrightarrow
\beta=\mathbb E[Z^{(2)}U].
\]

Now consider

\[
p_n
=
\bigl(W_{n,0}^{(2)}\bigr)^\top u_n.
\]

The vector \(u_n\) depends on \(W_{n,0}^{(2)}\), so we cannot treat this as multiplication by a fresh independent Gaussian matrix.

Write \(w_j\) for row \(j\) of \(W_{n,0}^{(2)}\). We already know

\[
w_j^\top h_n=z_{n,j}^{(2)}.
\]

Conditioning a Gaussian row on this one observed dot product gives the exact decomposition

\[
w_j
=
\frac{z_{n,j}^{(2)}}{n m_n}h_n
+
\widetilde w_j,
\]

where \(\widetilde w_j\) is Gaussian in the directions perpendicular to \(h_n\). Therefore

\[
\begin{aligned}
p_n
&=
\sum_j u_{n,j}w_j\\
&=
\frac{\beta_n}{m_n}h_n
+
\sum_j u_{n,j}\widetilde w_j.
\end{aligned}
\]

The remaining sum is conditionally Gaussian. More precisely,

\[
\boxed{
p_n
\overset d=
\frac{\beta_n}{m_n}h_n
+
\sqrt{\nu_n}\,
P_{h_n^\perp}G_n,
}
\]

where \(G_n\) has independent standard Gaussian coordinates, and \(P_{h_n^\perp}\) removes its component parallel to \(h_n\).

This is the exact “old response plus new Gaussian variation” formula:

\[
\underbrace{\frac{\beta_n}{m_n}h_n}_{\text{forced by the earlier forward call}}
+
\underbrace{\sqrt{\nu_n}\,P_{h_n^\perp}G_n}_{\text{unobserved part of the same matrix}}.
\]

Removing one direction from an \(n\)-dimensional Gaussian vector changes it negligibly on the scale of average squared coordinates:

\[
\frac1n
\left\|
P_{h_n^\perp}G_n-G_n
\right\|^2
\longrightarrow0.
\]

Since

\[
\frac{\beta_n}{m_n}\longrightarrow\frac{\beta}{m},
\qquad
\nu_n\longrightarrow\nu,
\]

the enlarged first-layer tuple has limiting law

\[
\boxed{
\bigl(Z^{(1)},H,P\bigr)
=
\left(
Z^{(1)},
H,
\frac{\beta}{m}H+\sqrt{\nu}\,G
\right),
}
\]

where \(G\sim N(0,1)\) is independent of \(Z^{(1)}\).

The response term can be checked without any probability theorem. At finite width,

\[
\begin{aligned}
\langle h_n,p_n\rangle_n
&=
\left\langle
h_n,
(W_{n,0}^{(2)})^\top u_n
\right\rangle_n\\
&=
\left\langle
W_{n,0}^{(2)}h_n,
u_n
\right\rangle_n\\
&=
\langle z_n^{(2)},u_n\rangle_n.
\end{aligned}
\]

Therefore the limit must satisfy

\[
\mathbb E[HP]
=
\mathbb E[Z^{(2)}U]
=
\beta.
\]

Our formula does:

\[
\mathbb E[HP]
=
\frac{\beta}{m}\mathbb E[H^2]
=
\beta.
\]

A fresh independent Gaussian approximation

\[
P_{\mathrm{wrong}}=\sqrt{\nu}\,G
\]

would instead give

\[
\mathbb E[HP_{\mathrm{wrong}}]=0,
\]

contradicting the exact transpose identity.

Having appended \(p_n\), the induction now knows the joint empirical law of

\[
\bigl(z_n^{(1)},h_n,p_n\bigr).
\]

Hence a later coordinatewise backpropagation operation such as

\[
v_n
=
\phi'(z_n^{(1)})\odot p_n
\]

has limiting coordinate

\[
V=\phi'(Z^{(1)})P.
\]

Again, this uses the joint law of \((Z^{(1)},P)\), not their separate marginal laws.

The next forward call

\[
W_{n,0}^{(2)}v_n
\]

is more complicated because \(v_n\) already depends on previous forward and backward uses of \(W_{n,0}^{(2)}\). But the same mechanism repeats.

Collect all previous forward inputs

\[
v_n^1,\ldots,v_n^r
\]

and all previous backward inputs

\[
u_n^1,\ldots,u_n^s.
\]

For a new forward input \(v_n\), first subtract the best linear combination of the old forward inputs:

\[
v_n
=
\sum_{a=1}^r\alpha_{n,a}v_n^a
+
v_{n,\perp}.
\]

The coefficients \(\alpha_{n,a}\) are obtained by solving a finite linear system whose entries are normalized dot products such as

\[
\langle v_n^a,v_n^b\rangle_n,
\qquad
\langle v_n^a,v_n\rangle_n.
\]

The induction says that all these dot products converge, so the coefficients converge to deterministic population coefficients.

The image of the old-span part is already known:

\[
W_{n,0}^{(2)}
\left(
\sum_a\alpha_{n,a}v_n^a
\right)
=
\sum_a\alpha_{n,a}
W_{n,0}^{(2)}v_n^a.
\]

The remaining direction \(v_{n,\perp}\) has never previously been queried forward. But earlier transpose queries impose constraints on its output. Enforcing those constraints produces another finite linear combination of old second-layer vectors. Whatever remains after satisfying all old constraints is Gaussian variation from the still-unobserved part of \(W_{n,0}^{(2)}\).

Thus the general new forward call has the form

\[
\boxed{
W_{n,0}^{(2)}v_n
=
\sum_a\alpha_{n,a}
\bigl(\text{old forward output }a\bigr)
+
\sum_b\gamma_{n,b}
\bigl(\text{old backward input }b\bigr)
+
\sigma_n P_n^\perp G_n.
}
\]

Every coefficient is computed from a finite table of normalized dot products already controlled by the induction. Therefore

\[
\alpha_{n,a}\to\alpha_a,
\qquad
\gamma_{n,b}\to\gamma_b,
\qquad
\sigma_n\to\sigma.
\]

After removing the finite-dimensional projection, the limiting coordinate is

\[
U_{\mathrm{new}}
=
\sum_a\alpha_aU^a
+
\sum_b\gamma_bU^b
+
\sigma G.
\]

A new transpose call is identical with first- and second-layer roles exchanged.

There are two cases. If \(\sigma>0\), a genuinely new Gaussian direction has been discovered. If \(\sigma=0\), the new query contains no unexplored direction and its result is completely determined by old vectors. The technical proof also ensures that near-zero directions do not cause unstable linear-system coefficients; this is the extra rank bookkeeping accompanying the empirical-law induction.

For a bounded Lipschitz test \(\psi\), the quantitative step is elementary once the conditional decomposition has been obtained. If \(\widetilde u_n\) denotes the response-plus-unprojected-Gaussian approximation, then

\[
\begin{aligned}
&
\left|
\frac1n\sum_i\psi(\text{old coordinates}_i,u_{n,i})
-
\frac1n\sum_i\psi(\text{old coordinates}_i,\widetilde u_{n,i})
\right|
\\
&\le
\operatorname{Lip}(\psi)
\|u_n-\widetilde u_n\|_n
\longrightarrow0.
\end{aligned}
\]

The empirical average involving \(\widetilde u_n\) then converges by the Gaussian law of large numbers conditional on the old computation. For tests that grow polynomially, one first controls sufficiently high moments and truncates rare large coordinates. No CLT rate is required.

At fixed \(\Delta\), the population oracle contains only finitely many such lines. Its scalar feedback coefficients—population residuals and population dot products—are deterministic and supplied in advance. Therefore every line is one of only four operations:

\[
\text{fixed linear combination},
\qquad
\text{coordinatewise map},
\qquad
W_{n,0}^{(2)}\text{ call},
\qquad
(W_{n,0}^{(2)})^\top\text{ call}.
\]

Starting from the initial Gaussian law and repeatedly applying the preceding four induction steps proves the desired empirical joint-law property for every oracle vector through time \(T\).

That is the actual proof engine packaged by the tensor-program theorem. Afterward, one takes the finitely many resulting empirical errors, calls their maximum \(\zeta_n\), and uses the separate stability recurrence

\[
E_{n,k+1}
\le
(1+C\Delta)E_{n,k}
+
C\Delta\zeta_n
\]

to return from the oracle to the actual empirical-feedback Euler network.

[178 | 2026-09-01T08:37:35.341Z | assistant | None]
Yes. Your revised picture is essentially right:

> The induction starts with a collection of already-understood vectors. It constructs one new vector, proves that its coordinates have the correct joint empirical behavior with every old vector, and then adds it to the understood collection.

One correction is important: the “response” is not separately assumed or added. Whenever \(W_0^{(2)}\) or \((W_0^{(2)})^\top\) is reused, the proof derives

\[
\boxed{\text{new vector}
=
\text{response forced by old matrix uses}
+
\text{fresh Gaussian part}.}
\]

The whole right-hand side becomes the new population variable. The proof then establishes the empirical-average property for this enlarged collection.

Let us watch this happen in the first genuinely nontrivial sequence of matrix calls.

## The first forward call

Use

\[
\langle a,b\rangle_n=\frac1n a^\top b,
\qquad
\|a\|_n^2=\langle a,a\rangle_n.
\]

Let

\[
v:=h_0^{(1)}=\phi(z_0^{(1)})
\]

be the initial first-layer activation, and let

\[
y:=z_0^{(2)}=W_0^{(2)}v.
\]

The entries of \(W_0^{(2)}\) are independent \(N(0,1/n)\), and \(v\) is independent of \(W_0^{(2)}\).

Conditional on \(v\),

\[
y_j=\sum_i W_{0,ji}^{(2)}v_i
\]

is Gaussian with variance

\[
\operatorname{Var}(y_j\mid v)
=
\frac1n\sum_i v_i^2
=
\|v\|_n^2.
\]

Different \(j\)'s use different rows of \(W_0^{(2)}\), so they are conditionally independent. If

\[
\|v\|_n^2\longrightarrow m,
\]

then the empirical distribution of the \(y_j\)'s approaches \(N(0,m)\).

After this, coordinatewise quantities such as

\[
h_0^{(2)}=\phi(y)
\]

are easy. For example,

\[
\frac1n\sum_j
\psi\bigl(y_j,\phi(y_j)\bigr)
\]

is just an empirical average of a function of \(y_j\). No new matrix randomness is involved.

So far this really does look like ordinary iid sampling. Reuse becomes visible at the transpose call.

## The first transpose call

In the oracle, the initial rescaled output weight is zero. After the first Euler update,

\[
\overline W_1^{(3)}
=
2\Delta\,\phi(y).
\]

Therefore the next second-layer backpropagation signal is

\[
\delta_1^{(2)}
=
2\Delta\,\phi(y)\odot\phi'(y).
\]

Ignore the harmless deterministic factor \(2\Delta\), and define

\[
u:=\phi(y)\odot\phi'(y),
\qquad
p:=(W_0^{(2)})^\top u.
\]

The crucial fact is that \(u\) depends on

\[
y=W_0^{(2)}v.
\]

Therefore \(p\) cannot be treated as a fresh independent Gaussian vector.

To see exactly what happens, write \(w_j^\top\) for row \(j\) of \(W_0^{(2)}\). We already know

\[
w_j^\top v=y_j.
\]

Conditioning a Gaussian vector \(w_j\) on this one scalar equation gives

\[
w_j
=
\frac{y_j}{n\|v\|_n^2}v+\widetilde w_j,
\]

where the remaining Gaussian part satisfies

\[
\widetilde w_j
\sim
N\left(
0,\frac1n\operatorname{proj}_{v^\perp}
\right).
\]

Now multiply by \(u_j\) and sum over \(j\):

\[
\begin{aligned}
p
&=
\sum_j u_jw_j\\
&=
\frac{\langle y,u\rangle_n}{\|v\|_n^2}v
+
\sum_j u_j\widetilde w_j.
\end{aligned}
\]

The second term is conditionally Gaussian, so

\[
\boxed{
p
\overset d=
\kappa_n v
+
\sqrt{\nu_n}\,
\operatorname{proj}_{v^\perp}\gamma,
}
\]

where

\[
\kappa_n
=
\frac{\langle y,u\rangle_n}{\|v\|_n^2},
\qquad
\nu_n=\|u\|_n^2,
\]

and \(\gamma\) has independent \(N(0,1)\) coordinates.

This is the first complete example of

\[
\text{response}+\text{new Gaussian part}.
\]

The response is

\[
\kappa_n v.
\]

It is forced by the earlier forward call. Indeed, the exact transpose identity says

\[
\langle v,p\rangle_n
=
\langle W_0^{(2)}v,u\rangle_n
=
\langle y,u\rangle_n.
\]

The Gaussian remainder is orthogonal to \(v\), while

\[
\langle v,\kappa_n v\rangle_n
=
\langle y,u\rangle_n.
\]

If we incorrectly replaced \(p\) by an independent Gaussian vector, its overlap with \(v\) would approach zero, contradicting this exact identity whenever

\[
\mathbb E[YU]\ne0.
\]

The induction already knows that

\[
\|v\|_n^2\to \mathbb E[V^2],
\qquad
\langle y,u\rangle_n\to\mathbb E[YU],
\qquad
\|u\|_n^2\to\mathbb E[U^2].
\]

Therefore

\[
\kappa_n\to
\kappa
=
\frac{\mathbb E[YU]}{\mathbb E[V^2]},
\qquad
\nu_n\to\nu=\mathbb E[U^2].
\]

Consequently, the new right-side coordinate behaves empirically like

\[
\boxed{
\kappa V+\sqrt{\nu}\,\Gamma,
}
\]

where \(\Gamma\sim N(0,1)\) is new and independent of the old right-side population coordinates.

Notice the precise claim: the finite coordinates of \(p\) are not iid. The claim is that joint empirical averages involving \(p\) and the old right-side vectors converge to the corresponding population expectations.

## The next forward call

Now the first-layer state is updated using \(p\). In the transformed coordinate \(x=F(z^{(1)})\),

\[
x_2^{(1)}
=
x_1^{(1)}
-
2\Delta r_1p_1^{(1)},
\]

and then

\[
h_2^{(1)}
=
\phi\left(F^{-1}(x_2^{(1)})\right).
\]

Let

\[
a:=h_2^{(1)}.
\]

Coordinate \(a_i\) is a deterministic scalar function of previously understood coordinates such as \(x_{1,i}^{(1)}\) and \(p_i\). Therefore the induction immediately extends to \(a\).

But \(a\) now depends on \((W_0^{(2)})^\top u\). Hence the next forward multiplication

\[
W_0^{(2)}a
\]

is not an independent Gaussian multiplication either.

At this point the matrix history contains both constraints

\[
W_0^{(2)}v=y,
\qquad
(W_0^{(2)})^\top u=p.
\]

Define

\[
m_n=\|v\|_n^2,
\qquad
\nu_n=\|u\|_n^2,
\qquad
\kappa_n=\frac{\langle y,u\rangle_n}{m_n},
\]

and remove from \(p\) the part already forced by the first constraint:

\[
p_\perp=p-\kappa_n v.
\]

Conditioned simultaneously on both previous matrix calls, the remaining uncertainty in \(W_0^{(2)}\) has the exact Gaussian representation

\[
\boxed{
W_0^{(2)}
\overset d=
\frac{yv^\top}{nm_n}
+
\frac{up_\perp^\top}{n\nu_n}
+
\operatorname{proj}_{u^\perp}
\widetilde W_0^{(2)}
\operatorname{proj}_{v^\perp},
}
\]

where \(\widetilde W_0^{(2)}\) is a fresh independent Gaussian matrix with entries \(N(0,1/n)\).

Every term has a concrete purpose:

- The first term guarantees \(W_0^{(2)}v=y\).
- The second guarantees \((W_0^{(2)})^\top u=p\).
- The last term is invisible to both old constraints.

Now define

\[
\alpha_n=\frac{\langle v,a\rangle_n}{m_n},
\qquad
a_\perp=a-\alpha_n v,
\]

\[
\beta_n
=
\frac{\langle p_\perp,a\rangle_n}{\nu_n},
\qquad
\sigma_n^2=\|a_\perp\|_n^2.
\]

Multiplying the conditional matrix formula by \(a\) gives

\[
\boxed{
W_0^{(2)}a
\overset d=
\alpha_n y
+
\beta_nu
+
\sigma_n\operatorname{proj}_{u^\perp}\xi,
}
\]

where \(\xi\) has independent \(N(0,1)\) coordinates.

Again:

\[
\underbrace{\alpha_ny+\beta_nu}_{\text{response forced by old calls}}
+
\underbrace{\sigma_n\operatorname{proj}_{u^\perp}\xi}_{\text{unexplored Gaussian part}}.
\]

The meaning of the two response terms is especially tangible:

- The component of \(a\) parallel to \(v\) must be sent to the corresponding component of \(y=W_0^{(2)}v\). This creates \(\alpha_ny\).
- The new result must obey the exact transpose constraint

  \[
  \left\langle u,W_0^{(2)}a\right\rangle_n
  =
  \langle p,a\rangle_n.
  \]

  The term \(\beta_nu\) supplies precisely the remaining overlap required by this identity.
- The fresh Gaussian term is orthogonal to \(u\), so it does not disturb that constraint.

All the coefficients are empirical inner products of previously understood vectors. The induction therefore gives

\[
\alpha_n\to\alpha,
\qquad
\beta_n\to\beta,
\qquad
\sigma_n\to\sigma.
\]

Thus the newly added left-side population coordinate is

\[
\boxed{
Z_{\mathrm{new}}^{(2)}
=
\alpha Y+\beta U+\sigma\Xi,
}
\]

where \(\Xi\sim N(0,1)\) is fresh and independent of the previous left-side population coordinates.

This two-forward-one-transpose calculation is the core mechanism of the entire reused-matrix theorem. With more previous calls, there are simply more old directions. Scalar divisions become finite Gram-matrix systems, and the response becomes a finite linear combination of previous forward outputs and previous transpose inputs.

## The exact induction hypothesis

After some number of program instructions, the proof maintains two properties.

The first is the empirical-average property. For example, suppose the right-side collection currently contains

\[
z_0^{(1)},\ h_0^{(1)},\ p_1^{(1)},\
x_2^{(1)},\ h_2^{(1)},\ldots
\]

For every fixed function \(\psi\) that grows at most polynomially,

\[
\boxed{
\frac1n\sum_{i=1}^n
\psi\left(
z_{0,i}^{(1)},
h_{0,i}^{(1)},
p_{1,i}^{(1)},
x_{2,i}^{(1)},
h_{2,i}^{(1)},\ldots
\right)
\longrightarrow
\mathbb E\,
\psi\left(
Z_0^{(1)},
H_0^{(1)},
P_1^{(1)},
X_2^{(1)},
H_2^{(1)},\ldots
\right).
}
\]

There is a separate statement for the second-layer collection

\[
z_0^{(2)},\ h_0^{(2)},\
\delta_1^{(2)},\ z_2^{(2)},\ldots
\]

because first-layer index \(i\) and second-layer index \(j\) label different neuron populations.

“Polynomially bounded” means simply that, for some fixed \(C\) and \(q\),

\[
|\psi(x)|\le C(1+\|x\|^q).
\]

Before writing this abstractly, notice what it contains:

- Taking \(\psi(x)=x^2\) gives squared-norm convergence.
- Taking \(\psi(x,y)=xy\) gives inner-product convergence.
- Taking higher powers gives control of large coordinates.
- Taking a bounded function of several coordinates gives their joint empirical distribution.
- Taking \(\psi\) after a coordinatewise activation allows the next activation vector to be added.

This is considerably stronger than saying that each vector has the correct marginal distribution.

The second induction property is stable linear dependence.

Gaussian conditioning requires solving small linear systems made from Gram matrices of previously queried vectors. Merely knowing

\[
G_n\to G
\]

is insufficient if \(G\) is singular: \(G_n^{-1}\) could explode because of tiny finite-width eigenvalues.

The proof therefore tracks a minimal core of genuinely independent directions and proves:

- If a limiting direction is genuinely new, its finite-width distance from the old span stays positive.
- If its limiting distance is zero, it is eventually an exact combination of the old core directions; it is not treated as a tiny fresh direction.
- Consequently, the relevant Gram ranks stabilize and their pseudoinverses converge.

The simplest example already occurs above. Since

\[
h_1^{(1)}=h_0^{(1)},
\]

the repeated call

\[
W_0^{(2)}h_1^{(1)}
\]

must equal the old result \(z_0^{(2)}\) exactly. Its innovation variance is zero. The proof must recognize this as an exact repeated direction, rather than invert a duplicated Gram matrix and accidentally invent new noise.

So the full induction hypothesis is

\[
\boxed{
\begin{array}{c}
\text{all joint empirical averages and moments are correct}\\[2mm]
+\quad
\text{all relevant linear spans and zero directions are stable}.
\end{array}}
\]

## How one induction step is closed

There are only two kinds of steps.

For a coordinatewise operation,

\[
a_i^{\mathrm{new}}
=
\rho(a_i^1,\ldots,a_i^m),
\]

nothing probabilistic must be proved. For any new measurement \(\psi\),

\[
\psi(a_i^1,\ldots,a_i^m,a_i^{\mathrm{new}})
=
\widetilde\psi(a_i^1,\ldots,a_i^m),
\]

where

\[
\widetilde\psi(x_1,\ldots,x_m)
=
\psi(x_1,\ldots,x_m,\rho(x_1,\ldots,x_m)).
\]

The old induction hypothesis applies directly to \(\widetilde\psi\).

The genuinely difficult step is a new call to \(W_0^{(2)}\) or \((W_0^{(2)})^\top\). After conditioning on every preceding call, it has the form

\[
\text{new vector}
=
\omega_n
+
\sigma_n\operatorname{proj}_{\text{old directions}^\perp}\xi,
\]

where \(\omega_n\) is a finite linear combination of old vectors.

To prove the new \(\psi\)-average, the error is divided into three concrete pieces:

\[
\begin{aligned}
&\text{fresh Gaussian empirical average}
-\text{its conditional mean}\\
+{}&
\text{conditional mean with finite-width coefficients}
-\text{conditional mean with limiting coefficients}\\
+{}&
\text{average of the resulting function of old coordinates}
-\text{its population expectation}.
\end{aligned}
\]

The first term vanishes by a high-moment law for the fresh Gaussian vector. The coordinates are not exactly independent because of the projection, but only finitely many directions are removed. For example,

\[
\left\|
\operatorname{proj}_{u^\perp}\xi-\xi
\right\|_n^2
=
\frac1n\left\|
\operatorname{proj}_{u}\xi
\right\|^2
\longrightarrow0.
\]

The second term vanishes because the empirical Gram matrices, response coefficients, and innovation variances converge. The high empirical moments keep unusually large coordinates from magnifying small coefficient errors.

For the third term, average over a fresh scalar Gaussian \(\Xi\) and define

\[
\Psi(\text{old coordinates})
=
\mathbb E_\Xi\!
\left[
\psi\bigl(
\text{old coordinates},
\text{limiting response}+\sigma\Xi
\bigr)
\right].
\]

This is now just a function of the old coordinates, so the previous induction hypothesis applies to \(\Psi\).

That proves the enlarged empirical-average property. The proof then checks whether \(\sigma=0\) or \(\sigma>0\) and updates the stable core accordingly.

No central limit theorem is being used here. The proof does not study the \(\sqrt n\)-scaled fluctuation of an empirical average. It proves that the unscaled error tends to zero. What it needs beyond a basic weak law is:

- convergence of every required polynomial moment;
- sufficiently high moment bounds for the conditional Gaussian sampling step;
- stable ranks when limiting Gram matrices are singular.

Sub-Gaussian tails are not maintained as an induction invariant. They would be unnecessarily strong. Initial Gaussian variables have every polynomial moment; bounded activations and polynomially bounded operations preserve the moments needed by this fixed finite calculation.

Finally, this induction is applied only to the oracle at a fixed mesh \(\Delta\). Then

\[
N=\left\lceil\frac{T}{\Delta}\right\rceil
\]

is fixed while \(n\to\infty\), so there are only finitely many vector additions and finitely many matrix directions to project away.

The population residuals and population Gram values appearing in the oracle instructions are supplied deterministically. What the induction proves is that the oracle’s empirical vectors, norms, outputs, and Grams converge to those supplied population quantities. The actual finite Euler network is compared with the oracle afterward through a deterministic stability recurrence.

Thus one could combine both into a single larger induction, but it would still contain the same two logical components:

1. the reused-Gaussian induction above;
2. a perturbation argument showing that empirical feedback coefficients stay close to their population values.

The oracle merely separates them cleanly.

This is the direct proof mechanism underneath Sections 3 and 5.1 of the [audited proof](/tmp/L2_FULL_AUDITED_PROOF.md:181).

\# Notation and exposition specification

Use these rules when rewriting or teaching the two-hidden-layer mean-field/gradient-flow proof.

\## Canonical network notation

Let \\(k\\) denote time/Euler step, \\(\ell\\) denote layer, and \\(i,j\\) denote neuron coordinates.

Use

\\[
z\_k^{(\ell)}
\quad\text{for preactivation},
\qquad
h\_k^{(\ell)}=\phi\\!\left(z\_k^{(\ell)}\right)
\quad\text{for activation},
\\]

and

\\[
W\_k^{(\ell)}
\quad\text{for the weights entering layer \\(\ell\\)}.
\\]

Always retain the layer superscript. Write

\\[
W\_0^{(2)},\qquad
\bigl(W\_0^{(2)}\bigr)^\top,
\\]

never a bare \\(W\\).

The forward pass is

\\[
h\_k^{(1)}=\phi\\!\left(z\_k^{(1)}\right),
\qquad
z\_k^{(2)}=W\_k^{(2)}h\_k^{(1)},
\qquad
h\_k^{(2)}=\phi\\!\left(z\_k^{(2)}\right).
\\]

Write \\(\phi'(z)\\) explicitly. Do not replace it with an unnecessary function such as \\(q(z)\\).

\## Output-weight normalization

Define \\(W^{(3)}\\) from the beginning to mean the rescaled output weight used throughout the proof. Never introduce a raw output weight or barred notation.

Use

\\[
f\_{n,k}
\=
\frac1n
\bigl(W\_k^{(3)}\bigr)^\top h\_k^{(2)},
\qquad
r\_{n,k}=f\_{n,k}-1,
\qquad
L\_{n,k}=r\_{n,k}^2.
\\]

The finite initialization is

\\[
W\_{0,j}^{(3)}
\sim N\left(0,\frac1{n^2}\right),
\\]

while the population oracle and limiting system use

\\[
W\_0^{(3)}=0.
\\]

This same \\(W^{(3)}\\) must be used consistently in the finite network, Euler scheme, oracle, empirical limits, and autonomous population flow.

\## Backpropagation notation

Use the canonical preactivation derivative

\\[
\delta\_k^{(\ell)}
:=
n\frac{\partial f\_{n,k}}
&#x20;       {\partial z\_k^{(\ell)}}.
\\]

Thus

\\[
\delta\_k^{(2)}
\=
W\_k^{(3)}
\odot
\phi'\\!\left(z\_k^{(2)}\right),
\\]

and

\\[
\delta\_k^{(1)}
\=
\phi'\\!\left(z\_k^{(1)}\right)
\odot
\bigl(W\_k^{(2)}\bigr)^\top\delta\_k^{(2)}.
\\]

The loss residual remains outside \\(\delta\\):

\\[
\frac{\partial L\_{n,k}}
&#x20;    {\partial z\_k^{(\ell)}}
\=
\frac{2r\_{n,k}}{n}\delta\_k^{(\ell)}.
\\]

When useful, express derivatives directly with partial notation:

\\[
\delta\_1^{(2)}
\=
2\Delta\\,
\frac{\partial}{\partial z\_0^{(2)}}
\frac12
\left\\|\phi\\!\left(z\_0^{(2)}\right)\right\\|\_2^2.
\\]

Do not introduce an auxiliary scalar name merely to differentiate it.

\## Avoid redundant aliases

Do not rename existing network objects with generic symbols such as

\\[
v:=h\_0^{(1)},\qquad
y:=z\_0^{(2)},\qquad
u:=\frac{\delta\_1^{(2)}}{2\Delta},
\qquad
p:=\bigl(W\_0^{(2)}\bigr)^\top u,
\qquad
a:=h\_2^{(1)}.
\\]

Write the canonical objects directly:

\\[
z\_0^{(2)}
\=
W\_0^{(2)}h\_0^{(1)},
\\]

\\[
\bigl(W\_0^{(2)}\bigr)^\top\delta\_1^{(2)},
\\]

\\[
W\_0^{(2)}h\_2^{(1)}.
\\]

Introduce shorthand only when it recurs enough to provide genuine compression and has a clear mathematical meaning.

\## Ordinary linear-algebra notation

Do not introduce special normalized notation such as

\\[
\langle b,c\rangle\_n
\quad\text{or}\quad
\\|b\\|\_n.
\\]

Write the normalization explicitly:

\\[
\frac{b^\top c}{n},
\qquad
\frac{\\|b\\|\_2^2}{n}.
\\]

Use \\(\\|\cdot\\|\_2\\) only for the ordinary Euclidean norm.

Orthogonal projection may be used because it genuinely compresses the conditioning formulas, but define it:

\\[
P\_{b^\perp}
\=
I-\frac{bb^\top}{b^\top b}.
\\]

Use \\((W^{(2)})^\top\\) for the finite matrix transpose and \\((W^{(2)})^\*\\) for the corresponding population adjoint action.

\## Exact finite dynamics

With

\\[
F(s)=s+\frac{s^3}{3},
\qquad
x\_k^{(1)}=F\\!\left(z\_k^{(1)}\right),
\\]

write the Euler updates as

\\[
x\_{k+1}^{(1)}
\=
x\_k^{(1)}
\-
2\Delta r\_{n,k}
\bigl(W\_k^{(2)}\bigr)^\top\delta\_k^{(2)},
\\]

\\[
W\_{k+1}^{(2)}
\=
W\_k^{(2)}
\-
\frac{2\Delta r\_{n,k}}{n}
\delta\_k^{(2)}
\bigl(h\_k^{(1)}\bigr)^\top,
\\]

\\[
W\_{k+1}^{(3)}
\=
W\_k^{(3)}
\-
2\Delta r\_{n,k}h\_k^{(2)}.
\\]

Every factor of \\(n\\), \\(2\\), \\(\Delta\\), and \\(r\_{n,k}\\) should remain visible.

\## Population notation

Use lowercase arrays for finite-width vectors and capitalized versions for typical population coordinates:

\\[
z\_k^{(\ell)}
\longleftrightarrow
Z\_k^{(\ell)},
\qquad
h\_k^{(\ell)}
\longleftrightarrow
H\_k^{(\ell)}.
\\]

The population output is

\\[
f\_k
\=
\mathbb E\left[
W\_k^{(3)}H\_k^{(2)}
\right].
\\]

The limiting state uses the same symbols

\\[
X^{(1)},\qquad W^{(2)},\qquad W^{(3)}
\\]

as the finite theory, with \\(W^{(2)}\\) now an action between the two population coordinate spaces.

\## Exposition order

Make every explanation self-contained. Introduce, in order:

1\. the network objects and their dimensions;
2\. the forward pass, output, residual, and loss;
3\. initialization and scaling;
4\. backpropagation derivatives;
5\. exact Euler updates;
6\. the oracle and what it is supplied;
7\. the first forward call;
8\. the first reused transpose call;
9\. the next reused forward call;
10\. only then the abstract induction hypothesis.

Build the abstract argument from concrete network operations rather than beginning with a generic tensor-program theorem.

\## Meaning before abstraction

Explain the reused-matrix mechanism concretely as

\\[
\text{new matrix result}
\=
\text{response forced by all previous matrix uses}
\+
\text{Gaussian randomness in unexplored directions}.
\\]

Explain why each response term is forced using exact identities such as

\\[
\bigl(h\_0^{(1)}\bigr)^\top
\bigl(W\_0^{(2)}\bigr)^\top\delta\_1^{(2)}
\=
\bigl(z\_0^{(2)}\bigr)^\top\delta\_1^{(2)}.
\\]

Only after concrete examples introduce the general empirical statement

\\[
\frac1n\sum\_i
\psi(\text{previous coordinates})
\longrightarrow
\mathbb E\\,
\psi(\text{population coordinates}).
\\]

Explain \\(\psi\\) as an arbitrary measurement function and give tangible choices:

\\[
\psi(s)=s^2
\\]

for moments and

\\[
\psi(s,t)=st
\\]

for empirical contractions.

\## Conceptual distinctions to preserve

Do not describe the finite coordinates as iid after matrix reuse. Say that their joint empirical averages converge to the population expectations.

Keep first-layer and second-layer neuron populations separate. The indices \\(i\\) and \\(j\\) do not create a natural coordinatewise pairing across layers.

Distinguish:

\- deterministic population residuals and contractions supplied to the oracle;
\- empirical convergence proved for the oracle vectors;
\- the later stability argument transferring the result to the actual empirical-feedback system.

Do not call the argument a CLT. It proves unscaled empirical errors vanish, not their \\(\sqrt n\\)-scaled fluctuations.

Do not impose unnecessary sub-Gaussian assumptions. State the actual requirement: sufficiently high polynomial moments and stable Gram-matrix ranks.

\## Writing style

Use a cohesive narrative with few headings. Avoid fragmenting one argument into many apparently unrelated sections.

Use plain mathematical language and explain unfamiliar concepts through elementary linear algebra and probability.

Derive central equations rather than paraphrasing them. State what each term does and why it is present.

Avoid unnecessary jargon, undefined abstractions, redundant notation, and phrases such as “clearly” that conceal an argument.

Do not use boxed equations. Use display equations only when they materially improve readability.

Keep the explanation mathematically deep but compact: concrete first, abstract second, and no extra symbols unless they genuinely reduce the reader’s work.

[276 | 2026-09-01T09:25:59.189Z | assistant | None]
Yes. We should define \(W^{(3)}\) from the beginning to mean the rescaled output weight used by the proof. There is then no raw output-weight symbol and no bar notation anywhere.

The factor \(1/n\) in the output and the initialization and update of \(W^{(3)}\) are adjusted accordingly.

## Complete finite-width setup

For width \(n\), the evolving parameters are

\[
z_k^{(1)}\in\mathbb R^n,
\qquad
W_k^{(2)}\in\mathbb R^{n\times n},
\qquad
W_k^{(3)}\in\mathbb R^n.
\]

Here \(W_k^{(3)}\) always denotes the rescaled output weight. It is the only output-weight variable used anywhere below.

The forward pass is

\[
h_k^{(1)}
=
\phi\!\left(z_k^{(1)}\right),
\]

\[
z_k^{(2)}
=
W_k^{(2)}h_k^{(1)},
\]

\[
h_k^{(2)}
=
\phi\!\left(z_k^{(2)}\right),
\]

\[
f_{n,k}
=
\frac1n
\bigl(W_k^{(3)}\bigr)^\top h_k^{(2)},
\]

where

\[
\phi(s)=\arctan s
\]

acts coordinatewise.

The target is \(1\), so

\[
r_{n,k}=f_{n,k}-1,
\qquad
L_{n,k}=r_{n,k}^2.
\]

The initialization is

\[
z_{0,i}^{(1)}
\overset{\mathrm{iid}}{\sim}N(0,1),
\]

\[
W_{0,ji}^{(2)}
\overset{\mathrm{iid}}{\sim}
N\left(0,\frac1n\right),
\]

\[
W_{0,j}^{(3)}
\overset{\mathrm{iid}}{\sim}
N\left(0,\frac1{n^2}\right),
\]

with all initial quantities independent.

Thus \(W_0^{(3)}\) is already written in its rescaled coordinates. Its coordinates vanish as \(n\to\infty\), so the population oracle sets

\[
W_0^{(3)}=0.
\]

The difference between the small random finite initialization and zero is handled later by the stability comparison.

Define the residual-free backpropagation derivatives by

\[
\delta_k^{(\ell)}
:=
n\frac{\partial f_{n,k}}
        {\partial z_k^{(\ell)}}.
\]

For the second hidden layer,

\[
\delta_k^{(2)}
=
W_k^{(3)}
\odot
\phi'\!\left(z_k^{(2)}\right).
\]

Backpropagating through \(W_k^{(2)}\) and then through the first activation gives

\[
\delta_k^{(1)}
=
\phi'\!\left(z_k^{(1)}\right)
\odot
\bigl(W_k^{(2)}\bigr)^\top\delta_k^{(2)}.
\]

The corresponding loss derivatives are

\[
\frac{\partial L_{n,k}}
     {\partial z_k^{(\ell)}}
=
\frac{2r_{n,k}}{n}\delta_k^{(\ell)}.
\]

For \(\phi(s)=\arctan s\), define

\[
F(s)=s+\frac{s^3}{3},
\qquad
x_k^{(1)}
=
F\!\left(z_k^{(1)}\right).
\]

Since

\[
F'(s)=1+s^2=\frac1{\phi'(s)},
\]

the first-layer activation derivative cancels in the evolution of \(x_k^{(1)}\).

With Euler step \(\Delta\), the exact induced updates in the rescaled coordinates are

\[
x_{k+1}^{(1)}
=
x_k^{(1)}
-
2\Delta r_{n,k}
\bigl(W_k^{(2)}\bigr)^\top
\delta_k^{(2)},
\]

\[
W_{k+1}^{(2)}
=
W_k^{(2)}
-
\frac{2\Delta r_{n,k}}{n}
\delta_k^{(2)}
\bigl(h_k^{(1)}\bigr)^\top,
\]

\[
W_{k+1}^{(3)}
=
W_k^{(3)}
-
2\Delta r_{n,k}h_k^{(2)}.
\]

The last equation is already the update for the rescaled output weights \(W_k^{(3)}\). No conversion to or from another output-weight variable is needed.

The population oracle is analyzed for fixed \(\Delta\). For a fixed time horizon \(T\),

\[
N=\left\lceil\frac T\Delta\right\rceil
\]

is fixed while \(n\to\infty\). Therefore only finitely many forward and transpose calls must be controlled.

## The first forward call

At initialization,

\[
h_0^{(1)}
=
\phi\!\left(z_0^{(1)}\right),
\]

and

\[
z_0^{(2)}
=
W_0^{(2)}h_0^{(1)}.
\]

Conditional on \(h_0^{(1)}\),

\[
z_{0,j}^{(2)}
=
\sum_{i=1}^n
W_{0,ji}^{(2)}h_{0,i}^{(1)}
\]

is Gaussian with variance

\[
\operatorname{Var}
\left(
z_{0,j}^{(2)}
\mid h_0^{(1)}
\right)
=
\frac1n
\sum_{i=1}^n
\left(h_{0,i}^{(1)}\right)^2
=
\frac{\|h_0^{(1)}\|_2^2}{n}.
\]

Different second-layer coordinates \(j\) use different rows of \(W_0^{(2)}\), so they are conditionally independent.

The initial empirical law gives

\[
\frac{\|h_0^{(1)}\|_2^2}{n}
\longrightarrow
\mathbb E\left[
\left(H_0^{(1)}\right)^2
\right].
\]

Therefore the coordinates of \(z_0^{(2)}\) behave empirically like

\[
Z_0^{(2)}
\sim
N\left(
0,
\mathbb E\left[
\left(H_0^{(1)}\right)^2
\right]
\right).
\]

The second-layer activation is

\[
h_0^{(2)}
=
\phi\!\left(z_0^{(2)}\right).
\]

This is a coordinatewise function and introduces no new matrix randomness.

In the oracle,

\[
W_0^{(3)}=0.
\]

Consequently,

\[
f_0=0,
\qquad
r_0=-1,
\qquad
\delta_0^{(2)}=0.
\]

The first update gives

\[
x_1^{(1)}=x_0^{(1)},
\]

\[
W_1^{(2)}=W_0^{(2)},
\]

and

\[
W_1^{(3)}
=
2\Delta h_0^{(2)}
=
2\Delta\phi\!\left(z_0^{(2)}\right).
\]

It follows that

\[
h_1^{(1)}=h_0^{(1)},
\qquad
z_1^{(2)}=z_0^{(2)},
\qquad
h_1^{(2)}=h_0^{(2)}.
\]

The next second-layer backpropagation derivative is

\[
\begin{aligned}
\delta_1^{(2)}
&=
W_1^{(3)}
\odot
\phi'\!\left(z_1^{(2)}\right)\\
&=
2\Delta\,
\phi\!\left(z_0^{(2)}\right)
\odot
\phi'\!\left(z_0^{(2)}\right).
\end{aligned}
\]

Equivalently,

\[
\delta_1^{(2)}
=
2\Delta\,
\frac{\partial}{\partial z_0^{(2)}}
\frac12
\left\|
\phi\!\left(z_0^{(2)}\right)
\right\|_2^2.
\]

The population residual used by the oracle at this step is

\[
r_1
=
2\Delta\,
\mathbb E\left[
\phi\!\left(Z_0^{(2)}\right)^2
\right]
-1.
\]

## The first transpose call

The first nontrivial transpose operation is

\[
\bigl(W_0^{(2)}\bigr)^\top
\delta_1^{(2)}.
\]

This is not multiplication by an independent Gaussian matrix because

\[
\delta_1^{(2)}
\]

depends on

\[
z_0^{(2)}
=
W_0^{(2)}h_0^{(1)}.
\]

For any nonzero vector \(b\), define the ordinary orthogonal projection

\[
P_{b^\perp}
=
I-\frac{bb^\top}{b^\top b}.
\]

For row \(j\) of \(W_0^{(2)}\), the previous forward call supplies the constraint

\[
W_{0,j:}^{(2)}h_0^{(1)}
=
z_{0,j}^{(2)}.
\]

Conditioning the Gaussian row on this equation gives

\[
\left(W_{0,j:}^{(2)}\right)^\top
\overset d=
\frac{
z_{0,j}^{(2)}
}{
\bigl(h_0^{(1)}\bigr)^\top h_0^{(1)}
}
h_0^{(1)}
+
\widetilde W_{0,j:}^{(2)\top},
\]

where, conditionally,

\[
\widetilde W_{0,j:}^{(2)\top}
\sim
N\left(
0,
\frac1nP_{(h_0^{(1)})^\perp}
\right).
\]

Multiplying row \(j\) by \(\delta_{1,j}^{(2)}\) and summing gives

\[
\begin{aligned}
\bigl(W_0^{(2)}\bigr)^\top\delta_1^{(2)}
\overset d={}&
\frac{
\bigl(z_0^{(2)}\bigr)^\top\delta_1^{(2)}
}{
\bigl(h_0^{(1)}\bigr)^\top h_0^{(1)}
}
h_0^{(1)}
\\
&+
\frac{\|\delta_1^{(2)}\|_2}{\sqrt n}
P_{(h_0^{(1)})^\perp}
\gamma^{(1)},
\end{aligned}
\]

where

\[
\gamma^{(1)}\sim N(0,I_n)
\]

is independent of the previously constructed vectors.

The first term is the response forced by the old forward call:

\[
\frac{
\bigl(z_0^{(2)}\bigr)^\top\delta_1^{(2)}
}{
\bigl(h_0^{(1)}\bigr)^\top h_0^{(1)}
}
h_0^{(1)}.
\]

The second term is the genuinely new Gaussian part:

\[
\frac{\|\delta_1^{(2)}\|_2}{\sqrt n}
P_{(h_0^{(1)})^\perp}
\gamma^{(1)}.
\]

The response is necessary because of the exact transpose identity

\[
\bigl(h_0^{(1)}\bigr)^\top
\bigl(W_0^{(2)}\bigr)^\top
\delta_1^{(2)}
=
\bigl(z_0^{(2)}\bigr)^\top
\delta_1^{(2)}.
\]

The new Gaussian term is perpendicular to \(h_0^{(1)}\), so it contributes zero to the left-hand side. The response term supplies the required overlap exactly.

The induction already knows

\[
\frac{
\bigl(h_0^{(1)}\bigr)^\top h_0^{(1)}
}{n}
\longrightarrow
\mathbb E\left[
\left(H_0^{(1)}\right)^2
\right],
\]

\[
\frac{
\bigl(z_0^{(2)}\bigr)^\top\delta_1^{(2)}
}{n}
\longrightarrow
\mathbb E\left[
Z_0^{(2)}\delta_1^{(2)}
\right],
\]

and

\[
\frac{
\bigl(\delta_1^{(2)}\bigr)^\top\delta_1^{(2)}
}{n}
\longrightarrow
\mathbb E\left[
\left(\delta_1^{(2)}\right)^2
\right].
\]

Therefore the corresponding population adjoint action is

\[
\begin{aligned}
\bigl(W_0^{(2)}\bigr)^*
\delta_1^{(2)}
={}&
\frac{
\mathbb E\left[
Z_0^{(2)}\delta_1^{(2)}
\right]
}{
\mathbb E\left[
\left(H_0^{(1)}\right)^2
\right]
}
H_0^{(1)}
\\
&+
\sqrt{
\mathbb E\left[
\left(\delta_1^{(2)}\right)^2
\right]
}
\,\Gamma^{(1)},
\end{aligned}
\]

where

\[
\Gamma^{(1)}\sim N(0,1)
\]

is independent of the previous first-layer population coordinates.

The finite coordinates of

\[
\bigl(W_0^{(2)}\bigr)^\top\delta_1^{(2)}
\]

are not claimed to be iid. The conclusion is that their joint empirical averages with all previous first-layer coordinates approach the corresponding population expectations.

## The next forward call

The first-layer transformed coordinate now moves according to

\[
x_2^{(1)}
=
x_1^{(1)}
-
2\Delta r_1
\bigl(W_0^{(2)}\bigr)^\top
\delta_1^{(2)}.
\]

Then

\[
z_2^{(1)}
=
F^{-1}\!\left(x_2^{(1)}\right),
\]

and

\[
h_2^{(1)}
=
\phi\!\left(z_2^{(1)}\right).
\]

Each coordinate of \(h_2^{(1)}\) is a deterministic scalar function of previously controlled first-layer coordinates. Therefore its joint empirical behavior follows from the existing induction hypothesis.

The next difficult operation is

\[
W_0^{(2)}h_2^{(1)}.
\]

It is not an independent Gaussian multiplication because \(h_2^{(1)}\) depends on

\[
\bigl(W_0^{(2)}\bigr)^\top\delta_1^{(2)}.
\]

At this point the matrix history contains both

\[
W_0^{(2)}h_0^{(1)}
=
z_0^{(2)}
\]

and

\[
\bigl(W_0^{(2)}\bigr)^\top\delta_1^{(2)}.
\]

Conditioning \(W_0^{(2)}\) simultaneously on both observations gives

\[
\begin{aligned}
W_0^{(2)}
\overset d={}&
\frac{
z_0^{(2)}
\bigl(h_0^{(1)}\bigr)^\top
}{
\bigl(h_0^{(1)}\bigr)^\top h_0^{(1)}
}
\\
&+
\frac{
\delta_1^{(2)}
\left[
P_{(h_0^{(1)})^\perp}
\bigl(W_0^{(2)}\bigr)^\top
\delta_1^{(2)}
\right]^\top
}{
\bigl(\delta_1^{(2)}\bigr)^\top
\delta_1^{(2)}
}
\\
&+
P_{(\delta_1^{(2)})^\perp}
\widetilde W_0^{(2)}
P_{(h_0^{(1)})^\perp},
\end{aligned}
\]

where \(\widetilde W_0^{(2)}\) is a fresh independent Gaussian matrix with entries \(N(0,1/n)\).

The first term retains

\[
W_0^{(2)}h_0^{(1)}
=
z_0^{(2)}.
\]

The second retains

\[
\bigl(W_0^{(2)}\bigr)^\top
\delta_1^{(2)}.
\]

The final term is invisible to both previously inspected matrix directions.

Multiplying by \(h_2^{(1)}\) gives

\[
\begin{aligned}
W_0^{(2)}h_2^{(1)}
\overset d={}&
\frac{
\bigl(h_0^{(1)}\bigr)^\top h_2^{(1)}
}{
\bigl(h_0^{(1)}\bigr)^\top h_0^{(1)}
}
z_0^{(2)}
\\
&+
\frac{
\left[
P_{(h_0^{(1)})^\perp}
\bigl(W_0^{(2)}\bigr)^\top
\delta_1^{(2)}
\right]^\top
h_2^{(1)}
}{
\bigl(\delta_1^{(2)}\bigr)^\top
\delta_1^{(2)}
}
\delta_1^{(2)}
\\
&+
\frac{
\left\|
P_{(h_0^{(1)})^\perp}
h_2^{(1)}
\right\|_2
}{
\sqrt n
}
P_{(\delta_1^{(2)})^\perp}
\gamma^{(2)},
\end{aligned}
\]

where

\[
\gamma^{(2)}\sim N(0,I_n)
\]

is fresh and independent of the previous history.

The first response term comes from the component of \(h_2^{(1)}\) parallel to \(h_0^{(1)}\). Since

\[
W_0^{(2)}h_0^{(1)}
=
z_0^{(2)},
\]

that component must be mapped to the corresponding multiple of \(z_0^{(2)}\).

The second response term is forced by

\[
\bigl(\delta_1^{(2)}\bigr)^\top
W_0^{(2)}h_2^{(1)}
=
\left[
\bigl(W_0^{(2)}\bigr)^\top
\delta_1^{(2)}
\right]^\top
h_2^{(1)}.
\]

The first response term supplies part of the required overlap. The second response term supplies the remainder.

The fresh Gaussian term is perpendicular to \(\delta_1^{(2)}\), so it does not disturb this exact constraint.

Every coefficient in this formula is an empirical contraction of previously controlled vectors. For example,

\[
\frac{
\bigl(h_0^{(1)}\bigr)^\top h_2^{(1)}
}{n}
\longrightarrow
\mathbb E\left[
H_0^{(1)}H_2^{(1)}
\right],
\]

and

\[
\frac{
\bigl(\delta_1^{(2)}\bigr)^\top\delta_1^{(2)}
}{n}
\longrightarrow
\mathbb E\left[
\left(\delta_1^{(2)}\right)^2
\right].
\]

Therefore all response coefficients and Gaussian variances converge to their population values.

## The exact induction hypothesis

The first-layer collection now contains quantities such as

\[
z_0^{(1)},
\qquad
h_0^{(1)},
\qquad
\bigl(W_0^{(2)}\bigr)^\top\delta_1^{(2)},
\qquad
x_2^{(1)},
\qquad
h_2^{(1)}.
\]

For every fixed polynomially bounded function \(\psi\), the induction asserts

\[
\begin{aligned}
\frac1n\sum_{i=1}^n
\psi\Bigl(
&z_{0,i}^{(1)},
h_{0,i}^{(1)},
\left[
\bigl(W_0^{(2)}\bigr)^\top\delta_1^{(2)}
\right]_i,
x_{2,i}^{(1)},
h_{2,i}^{(1)},
\ldots
\Bigr)
\\
\longrightarrow\;
\mathbb E\,\psi\Bigl(
&Z_0^{(1)},
H_0^{(1)},
\bigl(W_0^{(2)}\bigr)^*\delta_1^{(2)},
X_2^{(1)},
H_2^{(1)},
\ldots
\Bigr).
\end{aligned}
\]

The second-layer collection contains quantities such as

\[
z_0^{(2)},
\qquad
h_0^{(2)},
\qquad
W_1^{(3)},
\qquad
\delta_1^{(2)},
\qquad
W_0^{(2)}h_2^{(1)}.
\]

Its empirical statement is

\[
\begin{aligned}
\frac1n\sum_{j=1}^n
\psi\Bigl(
&z_{0,j}^{(2)},
h_{0,j}^{(2)},
W_{1,j}^{(3)},
\delta_{1,j}^{(2)},
\left[
W_0^{(2)}h_2^{(1)}
\right]_j,
\ldots
\Bigr)
\\
\longrightarrow\;
\mathbb E\,\psi\Bigl(
&Z_0^{(2)},
H_0^{(2)},
W_1^{(3)},
\delta_1^{(2)},
W_0^{(2)}H_2^{(1)},
\ldots
\Bigr).
\end{aligned}
\]

The first-layer index \(i\) and second-layer index \(j\) label different neuron populations. There is no artificial coordinatewise pairing between them.

Polynomially bounded means that, for some fixed constants \(C\) and \(q\),

\[
|\psi(s)|
\le
C\left(1+\|s\|_2^q\right).
\]

Taking

\[
\psi(s)=s^2
\]

gives empirical second-moment convergence.

Taking

\[
\psi(s,t)=st
\]

gives contractions such as

\[
\frac{
\bigl(h_r^{(1)}\bigr)^\top h_k^{(1)}
}{n}
\longrightarrow
\mathbb E\left[
H_r^{(1)}H_k^{(1)}
\right],
\]

\[
\frac{
\bigl(\delta_r^{(2)}\bigr)^\top
\delta_k^{(2)}
}{n}
\longrightarrow
\mathbb E\left[
\delta_r^{(2)}\delta_k^{(2)}
\right],
\]

and

\[
\frac{
\bigl(W_k^{(3)}\bigr)^\top h_k^{(2)}
}{n}
\longrightarrow
\mathbb E\left[
W_k^{(3)}H_k^{(2)}
\right].
\]

The last convergence is exactly the convergence of the oracle prediction.

Higher powers provide the moment control needed to prevent a small number of unusually large coordinates from dominating these averages.

The induction also maintains stable linear relations among the vectors previously supplied to \(W_0^{(2)}\) and \(\bigl(W_0^{(2)}\bigr)^\top\).

This is necessary because simultaneous Gaussian conditioning produces finite Gram matrices with entries such as

\[
\frac{
\bigl(h_r^{(1)}\bigr)^\top h_s^{(1)}
}{n}
\]

and

\[
\frac{
\bigl(\delta_r^{(2)}\bigr)^\top
\delta_s^{(2)}
}{n}.
\]

If a limiting Gram matrix is singular, entrywise convergence alone does not guarantee convergence of its inverse or pseudoinverse. The proof must distinguish a genuinely new direction from a direction that is already represented by previous ones.

For example,

\[
h_1^{(1)}=h_0^{(1)},
\]

so

\[
W_0^{(2)}h_1^{(1)}
=
W_0^{(2)}h_0^{(1)}
=
z_0^{(2)}
\]

exactly. This repeated source direction creates no fresh Gaussian component.

More generally, the induction proves:

- a genuinely new source direction has a positive limiting distance from the old source span and therefore a positive Gaussian innovation variance;
- a direction with zero limiting distance is eventually an exact combination of the retained core directions;
- the relevant Gram ranks and pseudoinverses therefore stabilize.

Thus the induction maintains both joint empirical-moment convergence and stable linear relations.

Coordinatewise operations are easy to add. For example,

\[
h_k^{(\ell)}
=
\phi\!\left(z_k^{(\ell)}\right),
\]

\[
W_{k+1}^{(3)}
=
W_k^{(3)}
-
2\Delta r_kh_k^{(2)},
\]

and

\[
\delta_k^{(2)}
=
W_k^{(3)}
\odot
\phi'\!\left(z_k^{(2)}\right)
\]

are coordinatewise functions of already controlled second-layer vectors, with deterministic oracle coefficient \(r_k\). They introduce no new matrix randomness.

The difficult operation is a new call to

\[
W_0^{(2)}
\quad\text{or}\quad
\bigl(W_0^{(2)}\bigr)^\top.
\]

Conditioning on every previous forward and transpose call gives a finite response made from old network vectors plus Gaussian randomness projected away from all previously inspected directions.

To prove the enlarged empirical-average property, the new error is divided into:

\[
\text{fresh Gaussian empirical average}
-
\text{its conditional expectation},
\]

\[
\text{conditional expectation using finite-width contractions}
-
\text{the same expression using population contractions},
\]

and

\[
\text{empirical average of the resulting function of old coordinates}
-
\text{its population expectation}.
\]

The first difference vanishes by a sufficiently high-moment law for the fresh Gaussian vector.

The projected Gaussian coordinates are dependent, but only finitely many directions are removed. For example,

\[
\frac1n
\left\|
\left(
I-P_{(\delta_1^{(2)})^\perp}
\right)\gamma^{(2)}
\right\|_2^2
\longrightarrow0.
\]

Thus the projection is negligible at the level of empirical averages while remaining essential for preserving the exact previous matrix constraints.

The second difference vanishes because every finite-width contraction, response coefficient, and Gaussian variance converges.

The third difference vanishes by the previous induction hypothesis because it involves only previously controlled coordinates.

No central limit theorem is used. The proof establishes that the unscaled empirical errors tend to zero; it does not identify their \(\sqrt n\)-scaled fluctuations.

What is needed is:

- convergence of all required polynomial moments;
- sufficiently high moments for the conditional Gaussian sampling argument;
- stable ranks when a limiting Gram matrix is singular.

It is not necessary for every constructed vector to remain sub-Gaussian.

## The oracle and the trained matrix

Iterating the second-layer matrix update gives

\[
W_k^{(2)}
=
W_0^{(2)}
-
\frac{2\Delta}{n}
\sum_{r<k}
r_r\,
\delta_r^{(2)}
\bigl(h_r^{(1)}\bigr)^\top.
\]

Therefore

\[
W_k^{(2)}h_k^{(1)}
=
W_0^{(2)}h_k^{(1)}
-
2\Delta
\sum_{r<k}
r_r\,
\delta_r^{(2)}
\frac{
\bigl(h_r^{(1)}\bigr)^\top h_k^{(1)}
}{n},
\]

and

\[
\bigl(W_k^{(2)}\bigr)^\top
\delta_k^{(2)}
=
\bigl(W_0^{(2)}\bigr)^\top
\delta_k^{(2)}
-
2\Delta
\sum_{r<k}
r_r\,
h_r^{(1)}
\frac{
\bigl(\delta_r^{(2)}\bigr)^\top
\delta_k^{(2)}
}{n}.
\]

The rescaled output weights satisfy

\[
W_k^{(3)}
=
W_0^{(3)}
-
2\Delta
\sum_{r<k}
r_rh_r^{(2)}.
\]

In the oracle,

\[
W_0^{(3)}=0,
\]

so

\[
W_k^{(3)}
=
-2\Delta
\sum_{r<k}
r_rh_r^{(2)}.
\]

The population oracle supplies the deterministic values

\[
\mathbb E\left[
H_r^{(1)}H_k^{(1)}
\right],
\]

\[
\mathbb E\left[
\delta_r^{(2)}\delta_k^{(2)}
\right],
\]

and

\[
r_k
=
\mathbb E\left[
W_k^{(3)}H_k^{(2)}
\right]-1.
\]

The finite oracle uses those deterministic population values in its instructions. Its only matrix-random operations are finitely many calls to

\[
W_0^{(2)}
\quad\text{and}\quad
\bigl(W_0^{(2)}\bigr)^\top.
\]

The reused-Gaussian induction proves that

\[
\frac{
\bigl(h_r^{(1)}\bigr)^\top h_k^{(1)}
}{n}
-
\mathbb E\left[
H_r^{(1)}H_k^{(1)}
\right]
\longrightarrow0,
\]

\[
\frac{
\bigl(\delta_r^{(2)}\bigr)^\top
\delta_k^{(2)}
}{n}
-
\mathbb E\left[
\delta_r^{(2)}\delta_k^{(2)}
\right]
\longrightarrow0,
\]

and

\[
\frac{
\bigl(W_k^{(3)}\bigr)^\top h_k^{(2)}
}{n}
-
\mathbb E\left[
W_k^{(3)}H_k^{(2)}
\right]
\longrightarrow0.
\]

The actual finite Euler network uses its own empirical residuals and contractions. It is compared with the oracle afterward by a deterministic stability estimate.

Thus the proof separates the reused-Gaussian empirical-law argument from the stability argument for empirical feedback.

## The population evolution in the same notation

The same rescaled \(W^{(3)}\) is used in the limiting autonomous system. There is no change of output-weight variables when passing to the population limit.

The population state consists of

\[
X^{(1)}(t),
\qquad
W^{(2)}(t),
\qquad
W^{(3)}(t).
\]

The derived population quantities are

\[
Z^{(1)}
=
F^{-1}\!\left(X^{(1)}\right),
\]

\[
H^{(1)}
=
\phi\!\left(Z^{(1)}\right),
\]

\[
Z^{(2)}
=
W^{(2)}H^{(1)},
\]

\[
H^{(2)}
=
\phi\!\left(Z^{(2)}\right),
\]

\[
f
=
\mathbb E\left[
W^{(3)}H^{(2)}
\right],
\]

\[
r=f-1,
\]

and

\[
\delta^{(2)}
=
W^{(3)}
\phi'\!\left(Z^{(2)}\right).
\]

Here \(W^{(2)}\) is the limiting action from first-layer population coordinates to second-layer population coordinates, and \(\bigl(W^{(2)}\bigr)^*\) is its adjoint action in the reverse direction.

The autonomous population gradient flow is

\[
\frac{dX^{(1)}}{dt}
=
-2r\,
\bigl(W^{(2)}\bigr)^*
\delta^{(2)},
\]

\[
\frac{dW^{(2)}}{dt}
=
-2r\,
\delta^{(2)}\otimes H^{(1)},
\]

\[
\frac{dW^{(3)}}{dt}
=
-2rH^{(2)}.
\]

The rank-one operator is defined explicitly by

\[
\left(
\delta^{(2)}\otimes H^{(1)}
\right)a
=
\delta^{(2)}
\,
\mathbb E\left[
H^{(1)}a
\right].
\]

The initial population state is

\[
Z^{(1)}(0)\sim N(0,1),
\]

\[
W^{(2)}(0)=W_0^{(2)},
\]

\[
W^{(3)}(0)=0.
\]

Thus \(W^{(3)}\) means the rescaled output weight at every level:

- in the finite network;
- in the Euler scheme;
- in the oracle;
- in the empirical convergence statements;
- and in the limiting autonomous population flow.

No raw output-weight variable and no barred notation are needed.
