[390 | 2026-09-04T15:26:36.437Z | assistant | None]
The central structural issue is now isolated: the one-hidden-layer proof can move directly from fixed-mesh Gaussian conditioning to population Euler, whereas the two-hidden-layer proof must insert a deterministic-coefficient oracle because residuals and two families of contractions feed back through training. I’m keeping that extra bridge explicit while preserving the attached proof’s linear, “obligation-by-obligation” flow.

[434 | 2026-09-04T15:33:35.406Z | assistant | None]
The one-hidden-layer proof has the right spine. The two-hidden-layer proof needs two additional bridges:

1. the residual and empirical contractions feed back into later steps, so we introduce a deterministic-coefficient oracle;
2. the limit must retain one operator \(W^{(2)}\) acting forward and its adjoint \(\bigl(W^{(2)}\bigr)^*\) acting backward.

Everything else follows the same pattern: expand the trained matrix around \(W_0^{(2)}\), analyze finitely many reused Gaussian calls, construct the population operator, prove Euler convergence, and finally send the learning rate to zero.

## Finite-width system

At width \(n\), the trainable quantities are

\[
z_k^{(1)}\in\mathbb R^n,
\qquad
W_k^{(2)}\in\mathbb R^{n\times n},
\qquad
W_k^{(3)}\in\mathbb R^n.
\]

Here \(W_k^{(3)}\) is already the rescaled output weight. The forward pass is

\[
h_k^{(1)}
=
\phi\!\left(z_k^{(1)}\right),
\]

\[
z_k^{(2)}
=
W_k^{(2)}h_k^{(1)},
\]

\[
h_k^{(2)}
=
\phi\!\left(z_k^{(2)}\right),
\]

with

\[
\phi(s)=\arctan s.
\]

The output, residual, and loss are

\[
f_{n,k}
=
\frac1n
\bigl(W_k^{(3)}\bigr)^\top h_k^{(2)},
\qquad
r_{n,k}=f_{n,k}-1,
\qquad
L_{n,k}=r_{n,k}^2.
\]

Initialization is independent across all parameters:

\[
z_{0,i}^{(1)}
\overset{\mathrm{iid}}{\sim}N(0,1),
\]

\[
W_{0,ji}^{(2)}
\overset{\mathrm{iid}}{\sim}
N\!\left(0,\frac1n\right),
\]

\[
W_{0,j}^{(3)}
\overset{\mathrm{iid}}{\sim}
N\!\left(0,\frac1{n^2}\right).
\]

Define the residual-free backpropagation derivatives by

\[
\delta_k^{(\ell)}
=
n\frac{\partial f_{n,k}}
        {\partial z_k^{(\ell)}}.
\]

Direct differentiation gives

\[
\delta_k^{(2)}
=
W_k^{(3)}
\odot
\phi'\!\left(z_k^{(2)}\right),
\]

and

\[
\delta_k^{(1)}
=
\phi'\!\left(z_k^{(1)}\right)
\odot
\bigl(W_k^{(2)}\bigr)^\top
\delta_k^{(2)}.
\]

Since \(L_{n,k}=r_{n,k}^2\),

\[
\frac{\partial L_{n,k}}
     {\partial z_k^{(\ell)}}
=
\frac{2r_{n,k}}{n}\delta_k^{(\ell)}.
\]

For base learning rate \(\eta_n\), with the layer scalings used in the theorem, exact GD is

\[
z_{k+1}^{(1)}
=
z_k^{(1)}
-
2\eta_n r_{n,k}\delta_k^{(1)},
\]

\[
W_{k+1}^{(2)}
=
W_k^{(2)}
-
\frac{2\eta_n r_{n,k}}{n}
\delta_k^{(2)}
\bigl(h_k^{(1)}\bigr)^\top,
\]

\[
W_{k+1}^{(3)}
=
W_k^{(3)}
-
2\eta_n r_{n,k}h_k^{(2)}.
\]

The theorem takes

\[
\eta_n=n^{-2}
\]

and uses physical time

\[
t=k\eta_n.
\]

The corresponding finite-width gradient flow is

\[
\frac{dz^{(1)}}{dt}
=
-2r\,
\phi'\!\left(z^{(1)}\right)
\odot
\bigl(W^{(2)}\bigr)^\top\delta^{(2)},
\]

\[
\frac{dW^{(2)}}{dt}
=
-\frac{2r}{n}
\delta^{(2)}
\bigl(h^{(1)}\bigr)^\top,
\]

\[
\frac{dW^{(3)}}{dt}
=
-2r h^{(2)}.
\]

The three scaled tangent-kernel blocks are

\[
K_n^{(1)}
=
\frac{\|\delta^{(1)}\|_2^2}{n},
\]

\[
K_n^{(2)}
=
\frac{\|h^{(1)}\|_2^2}{n}
\frac{\|\delta^{(2)}\|_2^2}{n},
\]

\[
K_n^{(3)}
=
\frac{\|h^{(2)}\|_2^2}{n}.
\]

Let

\[
K_n=K_n^{(1)}+K_n^{(2)}+K_n^{(3)}.
\]

Differentiating the output along the flow gives three contributions:

\[
\frac{df_n}{dt}
=
-2rK_n.
\]

Therefore

\[
\frac{dL_n}{dt}
=
2r\frac{dr}{dt}
=
-4r^2K_n
\le 0.
\]

In particular,

\[
|r(t)|\le |r(0)|.
\]

This supplies the main stability bound.

The first-layer equation contains the factor \(\phi'(z^{(1)})\). Introduce

\[
F(s)=s+\frac{s^3}{3},
\qquad
x^{(1)}=F\!\left(z^{(1)}\right).
\]

Because

\[
F'(s)=1+s^2=\frac1{\phi'(s)},
\]

the chain rule gives the exact continuous-time identity

\[
\frac{dx^{(1)}}{dt}
=
-2r
\bigl(W^{(2)}\bigr)^\top\delta^{(2)}.
\]

Thus the transformed finite-width flow is

\[
z^{(1)}=F^{-1}\!\left(x^{(1)}\right),
\qquad
h^{(1)}=\phi\!\left(z^{(1)}\right),
\]

\[
z^{(2)}=W^{(2)}h^{(1)},
\qquad
h^{(2)}=\phi\!\left(z^{(2)}\right),
\]

\[
\delta^{(2)}
=
W^{(3)}
\odot
\phi'\!\left(z^{(2)}\right),
\]

\[
r
=
\frac1n
\bigl(W^{(3)}\bigr)^\top h^{(2)}-1,
\]

followed by

\[
\frac{dx^{(1)}}{dt}
=
-2r
\bigl(W^{(2)}\bigr)^\top\delta^{(2)},
\]

\[
\frac{dW^{(2)}}{dt}
=
-\frac{2r}{n}
\delta^{(2)}
\bigl(h^{(1)}\bigr)^\top,
\]

\[
\frac{dW^{(3)}}{dt}
=
-2r h^{(2)}.
\]

This transformation is exact for the continuous flow. It is not exact for a discrete GD step; that discrepancy will be handled later.

Both maps

\[
x\longmapsto F^{-1}(x)
\]

and

\[
x\longmapsto
\phi\!\left(F^{-1}(x)\right)
\]

are globally Lipschitz, because

\[
\frac{d}{dx}F^{-1}(x)
=
\phi'\!\left(F^{-1}(x)\right),
\]

and

\[
\frac{d}{dx}
\phi\!\left(F^{-1}(x)\right)
=
\phi'\!\left(F^{-1}(x)\right)^2.
\]

For two finite-width states, define

\[
\begin{aligned}
d_n(\theta,\widetilde\theta)
={}&
\frac{
\|x^{(1)}-\widetilde x^{(1)}\|_2
}{\sqrt n}
+
\left\|
W^{(2)}-\widetilde W^{(2)}
\right\|_{\mathrm{op}}
\\
&+
\frac{
\|W^{(3)}-\widetilde W^{(3)}\|_2
}{\sqrt n}.
\end{aligned}
\]

On a set where

\[
\|W^{(2)}\|_{\mathrm{op}}
\quad\text{and}\quad
\|W^{(3)}\|_\infty
\]

are bounded, the transformed vector field is Lipschitz in \(d_n\), with a constant independent of \(n\).

For example,

\[
\frac{
\|h^{(1)}-\widetilde h^{(1)}\|_2
}{\sqrt n}
\le
\frac{
\|x^{(1)}-\widetilde x^{(1)}\|_2
}{\sqrt n},
\]

and

\[
\begin{aligned}
\frac{
\|z^{(2)}-\widetilde z^{(2)}\|_2
}{\sqrt n}
\le{}&
\frac{\pi}{2}
\left\|
W^{(2)}-\widetilde W^{(2)}
\right\|_{\mathrm{op}}
\\
&+
\|\widetilde W^{(2)}\|_{\mathrm{op}}
\frac{
\|h^{(1)}-\widetilde h^{(1)}\|_2
}{\sqrt n}.
\end{aligned}
\]

Similarly,

\[
\begin{aligned}
\frac{
\left\|
\bigl(W^{(2)}\bigr)^\top\delta^{(2)}
-
\bigl(\widetilde W^{(2)}\bigr)^\top
\widetilde\delta^{(2)}
\right\|_2
}{\sqrt n}
\le{}&
\left\|
W^{(2)}-\widetilde W^{(2)}
\right\|_{\mathrm{op}}
\frac{\|\delta^{(2)}\|_2}{\sqrt n}
\\
&+
\|\widetilde W^{(2)}\|_{\mathrm{op}}
\frac{
\|\delta^{(2)}-\widetilde\delta^{(2)}\|_2
}{\sqrt n}.
\end{aligned}
\]

For the matrix update,

\[
\left\|
\frac1n
\delta^{(2)}
\bigl(h^{(1)}\bigr)^\top
\right\|_{\mathrm{op}}
=
\frac{\|\delta^{(2)}\|_2}{\sqrt n}
\frac{\|h^{(1)}\|_2}{\sqrt n}.
\]

The required bounds hold uniformly on every finite time interval. Since \(|r(t)|\le |r(0)|\) and \(|\phi|\le\pi/2\),

\[
\|W^{(3)}(t)\|_\infty
\le
\|W^{(3)}(0)\|_\infty
+
\pi |r(0)|t.
\]

Also,

\[
\left\|
\frac{dW^{(2)}}{dt}
\right\|_{\mathrm{op}}
\le
2|r(0)|
\frac{\|\delta^{(2)}\|_2}{\sqrt n}
\frac{\|h^{(1)}\|_2}{\sqrt n},
\]

and

\[
\frac1{\sqrt n}
\left\|
\frac{dx^{(1)}}{dt}
\right\|_2
\le
2|r(0)|
\|W^{(2)}\|_{\mathrm{op}}
\frac{\|\delta^{(2)}\|_2}{\sqrt n}.
\]

At initialization,

\[
\frac{\|W_0^{(3)}\|_2}{\sqrt n}
=
O_{\mathbb P}\!\left(\frac1n\right),
\qquad
\|W_0^{(3)}\|_\infty=o_{\mathbb P}(1),
\]

and

\[
\frac{\|x_0^{(1)}\|_2}{\sqrt n}
=
O_{\mathbb P}(1).
\]

A \(1/4\)-net argument gives constants \(M_0,c>0\), independent of \(n\), such that

\[
\mathbb P\!\left(
\|W_0^{(2)}\|_{\mathrm{op}}>M_0
\right)
\le 2e^{-cn}.
\]

Indeed, the unit sphere has a \(1/4\)-net with at most \(9^n\) elements,

\[
\|W_0^{(2)}\|_{\mathrm{op}}
\le
2\max_{a,b}
\left|
b^\top W_0^{(2)}a
\right|,
\]

and each fixed bilinear form is \(N(0,1/n)\). A Gaussian tail bound and a union bound give the claim.

Consequently, the finite-width flow exists globally. Explicit Euler with auxiliary mesh \(\Delta\) satisfies

\[
x_{k+1}^{(1)}
=
x_k^{(1)}
-
2\Delta r_{n,k}
\bigl(W_k^{(2)}\bigr)^\top
\delta_k^{(2)},
\]

\[
W_{k+1}^{(2)}
=
W_k^{(2)}
-
\frac{2\Delta r_{n,k}}{n}
\delta_k^{(2)}
\bigl(h_k^{(1)}\bigr)^\top,
\]

\[
W_{k+1}^{(3)}
=
W_k^{(3)}
-
2\Delta r_{n,k}h_k^{(2)}.
\]

One exact flow step differs from one Euler step by \(O_T(\Delta^2)\). If \(e_k\) is their state error at time \(k\Delta\), then

\[
e_{k+1}
\le
(1+L_T\Delta)e_k+C_T\Delta^2.
\]

Hence

\[
\sup_{0\le t\le T}
d_n\!\left(
\theta_n^\Delta(t),\theta_n(t)
\right)
\le C_T\Delta,
\]

with \(C_T\) independent of \(n\).

## Fixed-mesh width limit

Fix \(\Delta>0\). Then

\[
N=\left\lceil\frac T\Delta\right\rceil
\]

is fixed while \(n\to\infty\).

Iterating the finite matrix update gives

\[
W_k^{(2)}
=
W_0^{(2)}
-
\frac{2\Delta}{n}
\sum_{s<k}
r_{n,s}\,
\delta_s^{(2)}
\bigl(h_s^{(1)}\bigr)^\top.
\]

Therefore

\[
\begin{aligned}
W_k^{(2)}h_k^{(1)}
={}&
W_0^{(2)}h_k^{(1)}
\\
&-
2\Delta
\sum_{s<k}
r_{n,s}\,
\delta_s^{(2)}
\frac{
\bigl(h_s^{(1)}\bigr)^\top h_k^{(1)}
}{n},
\end{aligned}
\]

and

\[
\begin{aligned}
\bigl(W_k^{(2)}\bigr)^\top\delta_k^{(2)}
={}&
\bigl(W_0^{(2)}\bigr)^\top\delta_k^{(2)}
\\
&-
2\Delta
\sum_{s<k}
r_{n,s}\,
h_s^{(1)}
\frac{
\bigl(\delta_s^{(2)}\bigr)^\top\delta_k^{(2)}
}{n}.
\end{aligned}
\]

Thus training adds only finite sums of old vectors multiplied by scalar empirical contractions. The only genuinely random matrix requiring repeated analysis is \(W_0^{(2)}\).

The population Euler recursion is formally

\[
Z_k^{(1)}
=
F^{-1}\!\left(X_k^{(1)}\right),
\qquad
H_k^{(1)}
=
\phi\!\left(Z_k^{(1)}\right),
\]

\[
Z_k^{(2)}
=
W_k^{(2)}H_k^{(1)},
\qquad
H_k^{(2)}
=
\phi\!\left(Z_k^{(2)}\right),
\]

\[
\delta_k^{(2)}
=
W_k^{(3)}
\phi'\!\left(Z_k^{(2)}\right),
\]

\[
r_k
=
\mathbb E\left[
W_k^{(3)}H_k^{(2)}
\right]-1,
\]

with updates

\[
X_{k+1}^{(1)}
=
X_k^{(1)}
-
2\Delta r_k
\bigl(W_k^{(2)}\bigr)^*
\delta_k^{(2)},
\]

\[
W_{k+1}^{(2)}
=
W_k^{(2)}
-
2\Delta r_k
\delta_k^{(2)}\otimes H_k^{(1)},
\]

\[
W_{k+1}^{(3)}
=
W_k^{(3)}
-
2\Delta r_kH_k^{(2)}.
\]

The population rank-one operator means

\[
\left(
\delta_k^{(2)}\otimes H_k^{(1)}
\right)V
=
\delta_k^{(2)}
\mathbb E\left[
H_k^{(1)}V
\right],
\]

where the expectation is over the first-layer population coordinate.

Iterating the population matrix update gives

\[
W_k^{(2)}
=
W_0^{(2)}
-
2\Delta
\sum_{s<k}
r_s\,
\delta_s^{(2)}\otimes H_s^{(1)}.
\]

Consequently,

\[
\begin{aligned}
W_k^{(2)}H_k^{(1)}
={}&
W_0^{(2)}H_k^{(1)}
\\
&-
2\Delta
\sum_{s<k}
r_s\,
\delta_s^{(2)}
\mathbb E\left[
H_s^{(1)}H_k^{(1)}
\right],
\end{aligned}
\]

and

\[
\begin{aligned}
\bigl(W_k^{(2)}\bigr)^*\delta_k^{(2)}
={}&
\bigl(W_0^{(2)}\bigr)^*\delta_k^{(2)}
\\
&-
2\Delta
\sum_{s<k}
r_s\,
H_s^{(1)}
\mathbb E\left[
\delta_s^{(2)}\delta_k^{(2)}
\right].
\end{aligned}
\]

The population oracle supplies the deterministic numbers

\[
r_s,
\qquad
\mathbb E\left[
H_s^{(1)}H_k^{(1)}
\right],
\qquad
\mathbb E\left[
\delta_s^{(2)}\delta_k^{(2)}
\right].
\]

Its random vectors are still constructed from the finite initialization and repeated applications of

\[
W_0^{(2)}
\quad\text{and}\quad
\bigl(W_0^{(2)}\bigr)^\top.
\]

Use the suffix \(\mathrm{or}\) only to distinguish oracle arrays from the actual empirical-feedback Euler arrays. Initialize

\[
x_{n,0,\mathrm{or}}^{(1)}
=
F\!\left(z_{n,0}^{(1)}\right),
\qquad
W_{n,0,\mathrm{or}}^{(3)}=0.
\]

At step \(k\), define

\[
z_{n,k,\mathrm{or}}^{(1)}
=
F^{-1}\!\left(x_{n,k,\mathrm{or}}^{(1)}\right),
\qquad
h_{n,k,\mathrm{or}}^{(1)}
=
\phi\!\left(z_{n,k,\mathrm{or}}^{(1)}\right),
\]

\[
\begin{aligned}
z_{n,k,\mathrm{or}}^{(2)}
={}&
W_0^{(2)}h_{n,k,\mathrm{or}}^{(1)}
\\
&-
2\Delta
\sum_{s<k}
r_s
\delta_{n,s,\mathrm{or}}^{(2)}
\mathbb E\left[
H_s^{(1)}H_k^{(1)}
\right],
\end{aligned}
\]

\[
h_{n,k,\mathrm{or}}^{(2)}
=
\phi\!\left(z_{n,k,\mathrm{or}}^{(2)}\right),
\]

\[
\delta_{n,k,\mathrm{or}}^{(2)}
=
W_{n,k,\mathrm{or}}^{(3)}
\odot
\phi'\!\left(z_{n,k,\mathrm{or}}^{(2)}\right),
\]

\[
\begin{aligned}
x_{n,k+1,\mathrm{or}}^{(1)}
={}&
x_{n,k,\mathrm{or}}^{(1)}
\\
&-
2\Delta r_k
\Bigg[
\bigl(W_0^{(2)}\bigr)^\top
\delta_{n,k,\mathrm{or}}^{(2)}
\\
&\hspace{27mm}-
2\Delta
\sum_{s<k}
r_s h_{n,s,\mathrm{or}}^{(1)}
\mathbb E\left[
\delta_s^{(2)}\delta_k^{(2)}
\right]
\Bigg],
\end{aligned}
\]

and

\[
W_{n,k+1,\mathrm{or}}^{(3)}
=
W_{n,k,\mathrm{or}}^{(3)}
-
2\Delta r_kh_{n,k,\mathrm{or}}^{(2)}.
\]

Every scalar coefficient in this recursion is deterministic. Because \(N\) is fixed, it uses \(W_0^{(2)}\) and \(\bigl(W_0^{(2)}\bigr)^\top\) only finitely many times.

The first forward call is

\[
z_{n,0,\mathrm{or}}^{(2)}
=
W_0^{(2)}h_{n,0,\mathrm{or}}^{(1)}.
\]

Conditional on \(h_{n,0,\mathrm{or}}^{(1)}\), the coordinates are independent Gaussian variables with variance

\[
\frac{
\|h_{n,0,\mathrm{or}}^{(1)}\|_2^2
}{n}.
\]

Because

\[
W_{n,0,\mathrm{or}}^{(3)}=0,
\]

we have

\[
r_0=-1,
\qquad
\delta_{n,0,\mathrm{or}}^{(2)}=0,
\]

and

\[
W_{n,1,\mathrm{or}}^{(3)}
=
2\Delta h_{n,0,\mathrm{or}}^{(2)}.
\]

It follows that

\[
\delta_{n,1,\mathrm{or}}^{(2)}
=
2\Delta\,
\phi\!\left(z_{n,0,\mathrm{or}}^{(2)}\right)
\odot
\phi'\!\left(z_{n,0,\mathrm{or}}^{(2)}\right).
\]

The first nontrivial transpose call is

\[
\bigl(W_0^{(2)}\bigr)^\top
\delta_{n,1,\mathrm{or}}^{(2)}.
\]

For every nonzero vector \(b\), define

\[
P_{b^\perp}
=
I-\frac{bb^\top}{b^\top b}.
\]

Conditioning on the previous forward observation gives

\[
\begin{aligned}
\bigl(W_0^{(2)}\bigr)^\top
\delta_{n,1,\mathrm{or}}^{(2)}
\overset d={}&
\frac{
\bigl(z_{n,0,\mathrm{or}}^{(2)}\bigr)^\top
\delta_{n,1,\mathrm{or}}^{(2)}
}{
\bigl(h_{n,0,\mathrm{or}}^{(1)}\bigr)^\top
h_{n,0,\mathrm{or}}^{(1)}
}
h_{n,0,\mathrm{or}}^{(1)}
\\
&+
\frac{
\|\delta_{n,1,\mathrm{or}}^{(2)}\|_2
}{\sqrt n}
P_{\left(h_{n,0,\mathrm{or}}^{(1)}\right)^\perp}
\gamma_n^{(1)},
\end{aligned}
\]

where

\[
\gamma_n^{(1)}\sim N(0,I_n)
\]

is fresh Gaussian randomness.

The response term is forced by

\[
\begin{aligned}
&
\bigl(h_{n,0,\mathrm{or}}^{(1)}\bigr)^\top
\bigl(W_0^{(2)}\bigr)^\top
\delta_{n,1,\mathrm{or}}^{(2)}
\\
&\qquad=
\bigl(z_{n,0,\mathrm{or}}^{(2)}\bigr)^\top
\delta_{n,1,\mathrm{or}}^{(2)}.
\end{aligned}
\]

The Gaussian term is perpendicular to \(h_{n,0,\mathrm{or}}^{(1)}\), so it does not disturb this identity.

After this transpose call changes \(x_{n,2,\mathrm{or}}^{(1)}\), the next first-layer activation \(h_{n,2,\mathrm{or}}^{(1)}\) depends on the transpose result. Therefore

\[
W_0^{(2)}h_{n,2,\mathrm{or}}^{(1)}
\]

is another reused call.

Conditioning simultaneously on the old forward and transpose observations gives

\[
\begin{aligned}
W_0^{(2)}h_{n,2,\mathrm{or}}^{(1)}
\overset d={}&
\frac{
\bigl(h_{n,0,\mathrm{or}}^{(1)}\bigr)^\top
h_{n,2,\mathrm{or}}^{(1)}
}{
\bigl(h_{n,0,\mathrm{or}}^{(1)}\bigr)^\top
h_{n,0,\mathrm{or}}^{(1)}
}
z_{n,0,\mathrm{or}}^{(2)}
\\
&+
\frac{
\left[
P_{\left(h_{n,0,\mathrm{or}}^{(1)}\right)^\perp}
\bigl(W_0^{(2)}\bigr)^\top
\delta_{n,1,\mathrm{or}}^{(2)}
\right]^\top
h_{n,2,\mathrm{or}}^{(1)}
}{
\bigl(\delta_{n,1,\mathrm{or}}^{(2)}\bigr)^\top
\delta_{n,1,\mathrm{or}}^{(2)}
}
\delta_{n,1,\mathrm{or}}^{(2)}
\\
&+
\frac{
\left\|
P_{\left(h_{n,0,\mathrm{or}}^{(1)}\right)^\perp}
h_{n,2,\mathrm{or}}^{(1)}
\right\|_2
}{
\sqrt n
}
P_{\left(\delta_{n,1,\mathrm{or}}^{(2)}\right)^\perp}
\gamma_n^{(2)}.
\end{aligned}
\]

The first response term remembers

\[
W_0^{(2)}h_{n,0,\mathrm{or}}^{(1)}
=
z_{n,0,\mathrm{or}}^{(2)}.
\]

The second is forced by

\[
\begin{aligned}
&
\bigl(\delta_{n,1,\mathrm{or}}^{(2)}\bigr)^\top
W_0^{(2)}h_{n,2,\mathrm{or}}^{(1)}
\\
&\qquad=
\left[
\bigl(W_0^{(2)}\bigr)^\top
\delta_{n,1,\mathrm{or}}^{(2)}
\right]^\top
h_{n,2,\mathrm{or}}^{(1)}.
\end{aligned}
\]

The last term is Gaussian randomness in directions unexplored by both previous calls.

The general induction repeats this calculation with finitely many old source directions. For every new use of \(W_0^{(2)}\) or \(\bigl(W_0^{(2)}\bigr)^\top\),

\[
\text{new matrix result}
=
\text{response forced by all previous matrix uses}
+
\text{Gaussian randomness in unexplored directions}.
\]

The induction asserts separately for the two neuron populations that, for every fixed polynomially bounded measurement function \(\psi\),

\[
\frac1n
\sum_{i=1}^n
\psi\!\left(
\text{previous first-layer coordinates at }i
\right)
\longrightarrow
\mathbb E\,
\psi\!\left(
\text{corresponding population coordinates}
\right),
\]

and

\[
\frac1n
\sum_{j=1}^n
\psi\!\left(
\text{previous second-layer coordinates at }j
\right)
\longrightarrow
\mathbb E\,
\psi\!\left(
\text{corresponding population coordinates}
\right).
\]

For example,

\[
\psi(s)=s^2
\]

gives normalized second moments, and

\[
\psi(s,t)=st
\]

gives every scalar contraction appearing above.

Coordinatewise operations extend the induction by composition. A new reused matrix call is handled by Gaussian conditioning. Its empirical-average error splits into:

\[
\text{fresh Gaussian sampling error},
\]

\[
\text{finite-width response coefficients}
-
\text{population response coefficients},
\]

and

\[
\text{an empirical average involving only old coordinates}
-
\text{its population expectation}.
\]

The first vanishes by a sufficiently high Gaussian-moment estimate, the second by convergence of the relevant contractions, and the third by the preceding induction hypothesis.

The induction also tracks stable linear dependence. A genuinely new source direction has positive limiting distance from the old source span and hence positive Gaussian innovation variance. A source direction with zero limiting distance is eventually an exact linear combination of the retained core directions. This prevents pseudoinverses of nearly singular Gram matrices from becoming unstable.

This is an empirical law of large numbers, not a central limit theorem. It uses sufficiently high polynomial moments and stable Gram ranks; it does not require every constructed vector to remain sub-Gaussian.

Now define the oracle’s rank-memory matrix

\[
W_{n,k,\mathrm{mem}}^{(2)}
=
W_0^{(2)}
-
\frac{2\Delta}{n}
\sum_{s<k}
r_s
\delta_{n,s,\mathrm{or}}^{(2)}
\bigl(h_{n,s,\mathrm{or}}^{(1)}\bigr)^\top.
\]

The oracle node \(z_{n,k,\mathrm{or}}^{(2)}\) is not exactly the action of this matrix. Their exact difference is

\[
\begin{aligned}
&
W_{n,k,\mathrm{mem}}^{(2)}
h_{n,k,\mathrm{or}}^{(1)}
-
z_{n,k,\mathrm{or}}^{(2)}
\\
&=
-2\Delta
\sum_{s<k}
r_s\delta_{n,s,\mathrm{or}}^{(2)}
\left[
\frac{
\bigl(h_{n,s,\mathrm{or}}^{(1)}\bigr)^\top
h_{n,k,\mathrm{or}}^{(1)}
}{n}
-
\mathbb E\left[
H_s^{(1)}H_k^{(1)}
\right]
\right].
\end{aligned}
\]

Likewise, the rank-memory transpose differs from the backward expression used in the oracle by

\[
\begin{aligned}
-2\Delta
\sum_{s<k}
r_s h_{n,s,\mathrm{or}}^{(1)}
\left[
\frac{
\bigl(\delta_{n,s,\mathrm{or}}^{(2)}\bigr)^\top
\delta_{n,k,\mathrm{or}}^{(2)}
}{n}
-
\mathbb E\left[
\delta_s^{(2)}\delta_k^{(2)}
\right]
\right].
\end{aligned}
\]

Let \(\varepsilon_n^{\mathrm{or}}\) be the largest error among the finitely many oracle output averages, contractions, and squared norms required up to step \(N\). The reused-Gaussian induction gives

\[
\varepsilon_n^{\mathrm{or}}
\longrightarrow0
\qquad\text{almost surely}.
\]

The two rank-memory residuals are therefore at most

\[
C_{N,\Delta}\varepsilon_n^{\mathrm{or}}
\]

after dividing their Euclidean norms by \(\sqrt n\).

We now compare the actual empirical-feedback Euler network with the oracle. Use the same \(z_0^{(1)}\) and \(W_0^{(2)}\), and define

\[
\begin{aligned}
E_{n,k}
={}&
\frac{
\|x_{n,k}^{(1)}-x_{n,k,\mathrm{or}}^{(1)}\|_2
}{\sqrt n}
\\
&+
\left\|
W_{n,k}^{(2)}
-
W_{n,k,\mathrm{mem}}^{(2)}
\right\|_{\mathrm{op}}
+
\frac{
\|W_{n,k}^{(3)}-W_{n,k,\mathrm{or}}^{(3)}\|_2
}{\sqrt n}.
\end{aligned}
\]

The actual network begins with its small random \(W_0^{(3)}\), whereas the oracle begins at zero, so

\[
E_{n,0}
=
O_{\mathbb P}\!\left(\frac1n\right).
\]

The width-independent Lipschitz estimates and the oracle residual formulas imply

\[
\begin{aligned}
&
\frac{
\|h_{n,k}^{(1)}-h_{n,k,\mathrm{or}}^{(1)}\|_2
}{\sqrt n}
+
\frac{
\|z_{n,k}^{(2)}-z_{n,k,\mathrm{or}}^{(2)}\|_2
}{\sqrt n}
\\
&+
\frac{
\|h_{n,k}^{(2)}-h_{n,k,\mathrm{or}}^{(2)}\|_2
}{\sqrt n}
+
\frac{
\|\delta_{n,k}^{(2)}-\delta_{n,k,\mathrm{or}}^{(2)}\|_2
}{\sqrt n}
+
|r_{n,k}-r_k|
\\
&\le
C_{N,\Delta}
\left(
E_{n,k}+\varepsilon_n^{\mathrm{or}}
\right).
\end{aligned}
\]

Subtracting the three Euler updates then gives

\[
E_{n,k+1}
\le
\left(1+C_{N,\Delta}\Delta\right)E_{n,k}
+
C_{N,\Delta}\Delta
\varepsilon_n^{\mathrm{or}}.
\]

Since \(N\) is fixed, discrete Gronwall yields

\[
\max_{0\le k\le N}E_{n,k}
\longrightarrow0
\qquad\text{in probability}.
\]

This proves the fixed-mesh width limit. The Gaussian argument handles only the deterministic-coefficient oracle. The stability recurrence transfers its conclusion to the actual empirical-feedback Euler network.

## One population operator and autonomous flow

The preceding induction produces consistent joint limits for every fixed finite collection of forward and transpose calls. We now prove that these limiting calls are actions of one actual operator.

There are two separate neuron populations. Begin with a countable family of first-layer and second-layer probes generated by:

- the initial population coordinates;
- rational linear combinations;
- the coordinatewise functions used by the network;
- integer clipping operations;
- repeated applications of \(W_0^{(2)}\) and \(\bigl(W_0^{(2)}\bigr)^\top\);
- a countable dense collection of bounded Lipschitz measurement functions.

Every finite subset is one fixed reused-Gaussian program. Since the full family is countable, all its empirical limits hold simultaneously on one probability-one event.

For two first-layer probe sequences, define

\[
v_n\sim\widetilde v_n
\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad
\frac{\|v_n-\widetilde v_n\|_2^2}{n}
\longrightarrow0.
\]

The same relation is used separately on second-layer probe sequences. Completing the resulting equivalence classes under mean-square distance gives two spaces,

\[
\mathcal H^{(1)}
\quad\text{and}\quad
\mathcal H^{(2)}.
\]

A Hilbert space here means only that limits of mean-square Cauchy sequences have been added. Its squared norm is the corresponding population second moment:

\[
\|V\|_{L^2}^2=\mathbb E[V^2].
\]

There is no coordinatewise pairing between \(\mathcal H^{(1)}\) and \(\mathcal H^{(2)}\).

Define

\[
W_0^{(2)}[v_n]
=
[W_0^{(2)}v_n].
\]

If \(v_n\sim\widetilde v_n\), the uniform operator bound gives

\[
\frac{
\|W_0^{(2)}v_n-W_0^{(2)}\widetilde v_n\|_2^2
}{n}
\le
M_0^2
\frac{\|v_n-\widetilde v_n\|_2^2}{n}
\longrightarrow0.
\]

Thus this action is independent of the representative, linear, and bounded:

\[
W_0^{(2)}
:
\mathcal H^{(1)}
\longrightarrow
\mathcal H^{(2)}.
\]

Define the reverse action through finite transposes. At finite width,

\[
\frac{
u_n^\top W_0^{(2)}v_n
}{n}
=
\frac{
\left[
\bigl(W_0^{(2)}\bigr)^\top u_n
\right]^\top v_n
}{n}
\]

exactly. Passing to the joint limit gives

\[
\mathbb E\left[
U\,W_0^{(2)}V
\right]
=
\mathbb E\left[
\bigl(W_0^{(2)}\bigr)^*U\,V
\right].
\]

Therefore the limiting transpose action is the adjoint of the same \(W_0^{(2)}\), not an independent Gaussian operator.

The population state is

\[
X^{(1)}(t)\in\mathcal H^{(1)},
\]

\[
W^{(2)}(t):
\mathcal H^{(1)}
\longrightarrow
\mathcal H^{(2)},
\]

\[
W^{(3)}(t)\in\mathcal H^{(2)}.
\]

From the current state, define

\[
Z^{(1)}
=
F^{-1}\!\left(X^{(1)}\right),
\qquad
H^{(1)}
=
\phi\!\left(Z^{(1)}\right),
\]

\[
Z^{(2)}
=
W^{(2)}H^{(1)},
\qquad
H^{(2)}
=
\phi\!\left(Z^{(2)}\right),
\]

\[
f
=
\mathbb E\left[
W^{(3)}H^{(2)}
\right],
\qquad
r=f-1,
\]

\[
\delta^{(2)}
=
W^{(3)}
\phi'\!\left(Z^{(2)}\right).
\]

The population gradient flow is

\[
\frac{dX^{(1)}}{dt}
=
-2r
\bigl(W^{(2)}\bigr)^*
\delta^{(2)},
\]

\[
\frac{dW^{(2)}}{dt}
=
-2r
\delta^{(2)}\otimes H^{(1)},
\]

\[
\frac{dW^{(3)}}{dt}
=
-2rH^{(2)}.
\]

Its initial state is

\[
Z_0^{(1)}\sim N(0,1),
\qquad
X_0^{(1)}=F\!\left(Z_0^{(1)}\right),
\]

\[
W^{(2)}(0)=W_0^{(2)},
\qquad
W^{(3)}(0)=0.
\]

The same Lipschitz estimates used at finite width hold on these two mean-square spaces. On a set where

\[
\|W^{(2)}\|_{\mathrm{op}}
\quad\text{and}\quad
\|W^{(3)}\|_\infty
\]

are bounded, the right-hand side is Lipschitz in

\[
\|X^{(1)}-\widetilde X^{(1)}\|_{L^2}
+
\|W^{(2)}-\widetilde W^{(2)}\|_{\mathrm{op}}
+
\|W^{(3)}-\widetilde W^{(3)}\|_{L^2}.
\]

The integral equation is therefore a contraction on a sufficiently short time interval, proving local existence and uniqueness.

The same loss identity holds at population level. Define

\[
\delta^{(1)}
=
\phi'\!\left(Z^{(1)}\right)
\bigl(W^{(2)}\bigr)^*
\delta^{(2)},
\]

\[
K^{(1)}
=
\mathbb E\left[
\left(\delta^{(1)}\right)^2
\right],
\]

\[
K^{(2)}
=
\mathbb E\left[
\left(H^{(1)}\right)^2
\right]
\mathbb E\left[
\left(\delta^{(2)}\right)^2
\right],
\]

\[
K^{(3)}
=
\mathbb E\left[
\left(H^{(2)}\right)^2
\right].
\]

Then

\[
\frac{dr}{dt}
=
-2r
\left(
K^{(1)}+K^{(2)}+K^{(3)}
\right),
\]

so

\[
|r(t)|\le |r(0)|.
\]

This gives

\[
\|W^{(3)}(t)\|_\infty
\le
\pi |r(0)|t
\]

and

\[
\left\|
\frac{dW^{(2)}}{dt}
\right\|_{\mathrm{op}}
\le
\pi |r(0)|
\|W^{(3)}\|_{L^2},
\]

\[
\left\|
\frac{dX^{(1)}}{dt}
\right\|_{L^2}
\le
2|r(0)|
\|W^{(2)}\|_{\mathrm{op}}
\|W^{(3)}\|_{L^2}.
\]

These bounds prevent finite-time blow-up, so the population flow exists uniquely for all finite times.

This system is autonomous: its right-hand side depends only on the current state

\[
X^{(1)},\qquad W^{(2)},\qquad W^{(3)}.
\]

At the level of laws, the current state must retain the complete action law: the joint laws and same-layer expected products of every finite collection obtainable by applying the current \(W^{(2)}\), its adjoint, and the coordinatewise functions used by the dynamics.

If two representations have the same complete action law, matching identically constructed probes preserves mean-square distances. The matching therefore extends to a norm-preserving bijection between the closures of all future-relevant coordinates. It commutes with \(W^{(2)}\), \(\bigl(W^{(2)}\bigr)^*\), and all coordinatewise maps. Hence the two representations satisfy the same integral equation. Uniqueness gives the same future action law.

Restarting at time \(s\) therefore gives

\[
\Phi_t\!\left(\Phi_s(\Theta_0)\right)
=
\Phi_{t+s}(\Theta_0).
\]

No initialization array, absolute-time label, or previous residual must be retained.

For each fixed finite collection of current-state probes, the width-limit proof is now a three-part sandwich:

\[
\text{finite flow}
\approx
\text{finite Euler}_{\Delta},
\]

\[
\text{finite Euler}_{\Delta}
\longrightarrow
\text{population Euler}_{\Delta},
\]

\[
\text{population Euler}_{\Delta}
\approx
\text{population flow}.
\]

The first and third errors are bounded by \(C_T\Delta\). The middle convergence holds as \(n\to\infty\) for fixed \(\Delta\). Therefore one first sends \(n\to\infty\) at fixed \(\Delta\), and then sends \(\Delta\to0\).

This proves uniform compact-time convergence of every finite action record. Enumerating the countable records and weighting the \(m\)-th record by \(2^{-m}\) combines these individual statements into convergence of the complete action law.

## Exact GD versus the flow

It remains to compare exact GD with the finite-width gradient flow.

At a GD node, define the transformed first-layer velocity

\[
V_k^{(1)}
=
-2r_{n,k}
\bigl(W_k^{(2)}\bigr)^\top
\delta_k^{(2)}.
\]

The exact raw update can be written

\[
z_{k+1}^{(1)}
=
z_k^{(1)}
+
\eta_n
\phi'\!\left(z_k^{(1)}\right)
\odot V_k^{(1)}.
\]

Since \(F\) is cubic, coordinatewise,

\[
\begin{aligned}
F\!\left(
z+\eta_n\phi'(z)V
\right)
={}&
F(z)+\eta_nV
\\
&+
\eta_n^2z\phi'(z)^2V^2
+
\frac{\eta_n^3}{3}\phi'(z)^3V^3.
\end{aligned}
\]

Euler for the transformed flow retains only

\[
F(z)+\eta_nV.
\]

On every bounded time interval,

\[
\frac{\|V_k^{(1)}\|_2}{\sqrt n}
\le C_T,
\qquad
\|V_k^{(1)}\|_\infty
\le C_T\sqrt n.
\]

Because

\[
\sup_z |z|\phi'(z)^2<\infty,
\qquad
0<\phi'(z)\le1,
\]

the extra one-step defect is bounded in the state distance by

\[
C_T\left(
\eta_n^2\sqrt n+\eta_n^3n
\right).
\]

The ordinary local Euler defect is \(O_T(\eta_n^2)\). Width-independent stability therefore gives

\[
\sup_{0\le t\le T}
d_n\!\left(
\theta_n^{\mathrm{GD}}(t),
\theta_n^{\mathrm{flow}}(t)
\right)
\le
C_T\left(
\eta_n+\eta_n\sqrt n+\eta_n^2n
\right).
\]

The right-hand side vanishes whenever

\[
\eta_n\sqrt n\longrightarrow0.
\]

The theorem’s choice satisfies this because

\[
\eta_n=n^{-2},
\qquad
\eta_n\sqrt n=n^{-3/2}\longrightarrow0.
\]

Thus exact GD and the finite-width flow have the same population limit.

## Paths, kernels, and hidden-layer velocities

Action-law convergence at each time must still be upgraded to convergence of complete neuron trajectories.

For either hidden layer and any interval \(I\),

\[
\frac1n
\sum_i
\sup_{s,t\in I}
\left|
z_i^{(\ell)}(t)-z_i^{(\ell)}(s)
\right|^2
\le
|I|
\int_I
\frac{
\|\partial_tz^{(\ell)}(u)\|_2^2
}{n}\,du.
\]

This is the scalar Cauchy–Schwarz inequality applied coordinatewise and then averaged.

The bounded-state estimates give a uniform bound on the right-hand side. Hence each complete path is close, in average squared supremum distance, to its interpolation from a sufficiently fine fixed time grid.

At a fixed grid, action-law convergence gives convergence of the joint empirical laws of all grid values and their second moments. Taking \(n\to\infty\) at fixed grid and then sending the grid mesh to zero proves that the empirical path laws converge in

\[
W_2\!\left(C([0,T])\right)
\]

to the laws of

\[
Z^{(1)}(\cdot)
\quad\text{and}\quad
Z^{(2)}(\cdot).
\]

This is convergence of complete neuron trajectories, not merely of their distributions at each separate time.

The second and third kernel blocks pass directly through action-law convergence because \(\phi\) is bounded and

\[
\delta^{(2)}
=
W^{(3)}\phi'\!\left(Z^{(2)}\right)
\]

has controlled second moment.

The first block requires an additional tail argument because

\[
\bigl(W^{(2)}\bigr)^*\delta^{(2)}
\]

need not be uniformly bounded coordinatewise.

Action-law convergence gives uniform \(W_2\) convergence of the joint empirical law of

\[
\left(
z^{(1)},
\bigl(W^{(2)}\bigr)^\top\delta^{(2)}
\right)
\]

to the population law of

\[
\left(
Z^{(1)},
\bigl(W^{(2)}\bigr)^*\delta^{(2)}
\right).
\]

This implies uniform control of the squared tails of the second coordinate. For fixed \(M\), clip that coordinate to \([-M,M]\). Then

\[
(a,b)
\longmapsto
\phi'(a)\operatorname{clip}_M(b)
\]

and

\[
(a,b)
\longmapsto
\phi'(a)^2\operatorname{clip}_M(b)
\]

are bounded Lipschitz functions, so action-law convergence applies directly. The difference between the clipped and unclipped quantities is bounded by the squared tail. Taking \(n\to\infty\) first and then \(M\to\infty\) gives uniform convergence of \(K_n^{(1)}\).

The same argument handles the hidden velocities. The population first-layer velocity is

\[
\frac{dZ^{(1)}}{dt}
=
-2r\delta^{(1)}.
\]

For the second layer,

\[
\begin{aligned}
\frac{dZ^{(2)}}{dt}
&=
\frac{dW^{(2)}}{dt}H^{(1)}
+
W^{(2)}
\frac{dH^{(1)}}{dt}
\\
&=
-2r
\Bigg\{
\mathbb E\left[
\left(H^{(1)}\right)^2
\right]\delta^{(2)}
\\
&\hspace{18mm}+
W^{(2)}
\left[
\phi'\!\left(Z^{(1)}\right)^2
\bigl(W^{(2)}\bigr)^*
\delta^{(2)}
\right]
\Bigg\}.
\end{aligned}
\]

The finite-width identity is

\[
\begin{aligned}
\frac{dz^{(2)}}{dt}
=
-2r
\Bigg\{
&
\frac{\|h^{(1)}\|_2^2}{n}
\delta^{(2)}
\\
&+
W^{(2)}
\left[
\phi'\!\left(z^{(1)}\right)^2
\odot
\bigl(W^{(2)}\bigr)^\top
\delta^{(2)}
\right]
\Bigg\}.
\end{aligned}
\]

The potentially unbounded part is exactly the second clipped field above. The uniform operator bound ensures that its clipping error remains small after applying \(W^{(2)}\). Therefore

\[
\int_0^T
\frac{
\|\partial_tz_n^{(1)}(t)\|_2^2
}{n}\,dt
\longrightarrow
\int_0^T
\mathbb E\left[
\left|
\partial_tZ^{(1)}(t)
\right|^2
\right]dt,
\]

and

\[
\int_0^T
\frac{
\|\partial_tz_n^{(2)}(t)\|_2^2
}{n}\,dt
\longrightarrow
\int_0^T
\mathbb E\left[
\left|
\partial_tZ^{(2)}(t)
\right|^2
\right]dt.
\]

For linearly interpolated exact GD, differentiate

\[
z_n^{(2)}(t)
=
W_n^{(2)}(t)
\phi\!\left(z_n^{(1)}(t)\right)
\]

inside each interpolation interval. The resulting matrix-update and activation-update terms differ from the corresponding flow velocity by the already controlled GD-to-flow error, a vanishing within-step error, and the same clipped tail. Thus the two velocity-energy limits hold for exact GD as well.

The output

\[
f_n(t)
=
\frac1n
\bigl(W_n^{(3)}(t)\bigr)^\top h_n^{(2)}(t)
\]

is itself an action-law measurement. Hence the output and loss converge uniformly. Together with the clipping argument, this proves uniform convergence of all three kernel blocks and the total kernel.

## Strict feature learning at small times

It remains to prove that the limiting system is not merely well-defined but actually moves both hidden layers and remains nonlinear.

Initially,

\[
Z_0^{(1)}\sim N(0,1),
\qquad
H_0^{(1)}
=
\phi\!\left(Z_0^{(1)}\right).
\]

Define

\[
m
=
\mathbb E\left[
\left(H_0^{(1)}\right)^2
\right]
>0.
\]

Then

\[
Z_0^{(2)}
=
W_0^{(2)}H_0^{(1)}
\sim N(0,m),
\]

and

\[
H_0^{(2)}
=
\phi\!\left(Z_0^{(2)}\right).
\]

The first nonzero second-layer backpropagation direction is

\[
H_0^{(2)}
\phi'\!\left(Z_0^{(2)}\right).
\]

Define

\[
\nu
=
\mathbb E\left[
\left(
H_0^{(2)}
\phi'\!\left(Z_0^{(2)}\right)
\right)^2
\right]
>0,
\]

and

\[
\kappa
=
\frac{
\mathbb E\left[
Z_0^{(2)}
H_0^{(2)}
\phi'\!\left(Z_0^{(2)}\right)
\right]
}{m}.
\]

Gaussian integration by parts also gives

\[
\kappa
=
\mathbb E\left[
\frac{d}{dz}
\left(
\phi(z)\phi'(z)
\right)
\bigg|_{z=Z_0^{(2)}}
\right].
\]

The reused-adjoint calculation gives

\[
\begin{aligned}
\bigl(W_0^{(2)}\bigr)^*
\left[
H_0^{(2)}
\phi'\!\left(Z_0^{(2)}\right)
\right]
=
\sqrt{\nu}\,\Gamma^{(1)}
+
\kappa H_0^{(1)},
\end{aligned}
\]

where

\[
\Gamma^{(1)}\sim N(0,1)
\]

is independent of \(Z_0^{(1)}\).

The first term is new Gaussian randomness. The second is the response forced by the previous forward call

\[
Z_0^{(2)}
=
W_0^{(2)}H_0^{(1)}.
\]

Define

\[
\begin{aligned}
d
=
\mathbb E\Bigg[
\phi'\!\left(Z_0^{(1)}\right)^2
\left(
\bigl(W_0^{(2)}\bigr)^*
\left[
H_0^{(2)}
\phi'\!\left(Z_0^{(2)}\right)
\right]
\right)^2
\Bigg].
\end{aligned}
\]

Using the response law,

\[
\begin{aligned}
d
={}&
\nu\,
\mathbb E\left[
\phi'\!\left(Z_0^{(1)}\right)^2
\right]
\\
&+
\kappa^2
\mathbb E\left[
\phi'\!\left(Z_0^{(1)}\right)^2
\left(H_0^{(1)}\right)^2
\right]
>0.
\end{aligned}
\]

Define the second-layer response operator by

\[
\begin{aligned}
\mathcal R_0U
={}&
mU
\\
&+
W_0^{(2)}
\left[
\phi'\!\left(Z_0^{(1)}\right)^2
\bigl(W_0^{(2)}\bigr)^*U
\right].
\end{aligned}
\]

The adjoint identity gives

\[
\begin{aligned}
\mathbb E\left[
U\,\mathcal R_0U
\right]
={}&
m\mathbb E[U^2]
\\
&+
\mathbb E\left[
\phi'\!\left(Z_0^{(1)}\right)^2
\left(
\bigl(W_0^{(2)}\bigr)^*U
\right)^2
\right].
\end{aligned}
\]

For

\[
U=
H_0^{(2)}
\phi'\!\left(Z_0^{(2)}\right),
\]

this becomes

\[
\mathbb E\left[
U\,\mathcal R_0U
\right]
=
m\nu+d>0.
\]

Therefore

\[
\mathcal R_0
\left[
H_0^{(2)}
\phi'\!\left(Z_0^{(2)}\right)
\right]
\ne0.
\]

At \(t=0\),

\[
W^{(3)}(0)=0,
\qquad
r(0)=-1,
\qquad
\delta^{(2)}(0)=0.
\]

The output-weight equation gives

\[
W^{(3)}(t)
=
2tH_0^{(2)}
+
O_{L^2}(t^2).
\]

Hence

\[
\delta^{(2)}(t)
=
2tH_0^{(2)}
\phi'\!\left(Z_0^{(2)}\right)
+
O_{L^2}(t^2).
\]

Substituting this into the first-layer equation gives

\[
\begin{aligned}
Z^{(1)}(t)-Z_0^{(1)}
=
2t^2
\phi'\!\left(Z_0^{(1)}\right)
\bigl(W_0^{(2)}\bigr)^*
\left[
H_0^{(2)}
\phi'\!\left(Z_0^{(2)}\right)
\right]
+
o_{L^2}(t^2).
\end{aligned}
\]

The matrix equation gives

\[
\begin{aligned}
W^{(2)}(t)-W_0^{(2)}
=
2t^2
\left[
H_0^{(2)}
\phi'\!\left(Z_0^{(2)}\right)
\right]
\otimes H_0^{(1)}
+
o_{\mathrm{op}}(t^2).
\end{aligned}
\]

Since

\[
Z^{(2)}(t)
=
W^{(2)}(t)H^{(1)}(t),
\]

the change in \(W^{(2)}\) contributes

\[
2t^2m
H_0^{(2)}
\phi'\!\left(Z_0^{(2)}\right),
\]

while the change in \(H^{(1)}\) contributes

\[
\begin{aligned}
2t^2W_0^{(2)}
\Bigg[
\phi'\!\left(Z_0^{(1)}\right)^2
\bigl(W_0^{(2)}\bigr)^*
\left[
H_0^{(2)}
\phi'\!\left(Z_0^{(2)}\right)
\right]
\Bigg].
\end{aligned}
\]

Therefore

\[
\begin{aligned}
Z^{(2)}(t)-Z_0^{(2)}
=
2t^2
\mathcal R_0
\left[
H_0^{(2)}
\phi'\!\left(Z_0^{(2)}\right)
\right]
+
o_{L^2}(t^2).
\end{aligned}
\]

It follows that

\[
\mathbb E\left[
\left|
\partial_tZ^{(1)}(t)
\right|^2
\right]
=
16dt^2+o(t^2),
\]

and

\[
\begin{aligned}
\mathbb E\left[
\left|
\partial_tZ^{(2)}(t)
\right|^2
\right]
={}&
16t^2
\mathbb E\Bigg[
\left(
\mathcal R_0
\left[
H_0^{(2)}
\phi'\!\left(Z_0^{(2)}\right)
\right]
\right)^2
\Bigg]
\\
&+
o(t^2).
\end{aligned}
\]

Both leading coefficients are positive. The mean-square displacements are similarly

\[
\mathbb E\left[
\left|
Z^{(1)}(t)-Z_0^{(1)}
\right|^2
\right]
=
4dt^4+o(t^4),
\]

and

\[
\begin{aligned}
\mathbb E\left[
\left|
Z^{(2)}(t)-Z_0^{(2)}
\right|^2
\right]
={}&
4t^4
\mathbb E\Bigg[
\left(
\mathcal R_0
\left[
H_0^{(2)}
\phi'\!\left(Z_0^{(2)}\right)
\right]
\right)^2
\Bigg]
\\
&+
o(t^4).
\end{aligned}
\]

Thus both hidden layers genuinely move.

Let

\[
k_3
=
\mathbb E\left[
\left(H_0^{(2)}\right)^2
\right]
>0.
\]

The same expansions give

\[
K^{(1)}(t)
=
4dt^2+o(t^2),
\]

\[
K^{(2)}(t)
=
4m\nu t^2+o(t^2),
\]

and

\[
K^{(3)}(t)
=
k_3+4(m\nu+d)t^2+o(t^2).
\]

Therefore

\[
K(t)
=
k_3+8(m\nu+d)t^2+o(t^2).
\]

Every parameter block has positive integrated kernel on every sufficiently short positive interval, and the total kernel is nonconstant.

Furthermore,

\[
f(t)=2k_3t+o(t),
\]

so

\[
L(t)
=
\bigl(f(t)-1\bigr)^2
=
1-4k_3t+o(t).
\]

Hence \(L(T)<L(0)=1\) for all sufficiently small \(T>0\).

Finally, for either hidden layer \(\ell\), define the squared error of the best affine approximation to the activation by

\[
\mathcal N^{(\ell)}(t)
=
\inf_{\alpha,\beta\in\mathbb R}
\mathbb E\left[
\left(
\phi\!\left(Z^{(\ell)}(t)\right)
-
\alpha Z^{(\ell)}(t)-\beta
\right)^2
\right].
\]

Whenever \(\operatorname{Var}(Z^{(\ell)}(t))>0\),

\[
\begin{aligned}
\mathcal N^{(\ell)}(t)
={}&
\operatorname{Var}\left(
\phi\!\left(Z^{(\ell)}(t)\right)
\right)
\\
&-
\frac{
\operatorname{Cov}\left(
Z^{(\ell)}(t),
\phi\!\left(Z^{(\ell)}(t)\right)
\right)^2
}{
\operatorname{Var}\left(
Z^{(\ell)}(t)
\right)
}.
\end{aligned}
\]

At \(t=0\),

\[
Z_0^{(1)}\sim N(0,1),
\qquad
Z_0^{(2)}\sim N(0,m).
\]

Both laws have positive variance and full support. If either \(\mathcal N^{(\ell)}(0)\) were zero, then \(\arctan z\) would agree almost everywhere under a full-support Gaussian law with an affine function. Continuity would make that equality hold for every \(z\in\mathbb R\), which is false. Hence

\[
\mathcal N^{(1)}(0)>0,
\qquad
\mathcal N^{(2)}(0)>0.
\]

The hidden preactivation laws vary continuously in \(W_2\), and the moments in the regression formula are continuous under this convergence. For the only unbounded mixed moment, any coupling of square-integrable \(X,Y\) satisfies

\[
\begin{aligned}
\left|
\mathbb E[X\phi(X)]
-
\mathbb E[Y\phi(Y)]
\right|
\le{}&
\frac{\pi}{2}
\left(
\mathbb E|X-Y|^2
\right)^{1/2}
\\
&+
\left(
\mathbb E[Y^2]
\right)^{1/2}
\left(
\mathbb E|X-Y|^2
\right)^{1/2}.
\end{aligned}
\]

Therefore both hidden variances and both nonlinear regression residuals remain positive for all sufficiently small times.

This proves the full result: exact finite-width GD converges to one unique autonomous and restartable population gradient flow; the complete hidden-unit path laws, outputs, losses, all three kernel blocks, and both hidden velocity energies converge; and the limiting dynamics genuinely train both hidden layers while retaining nonlinear features.

I found the named conjecture, and there is an important source-level distinction: the later “MFP–Borel” conjecture is about a scalar output/kernel reconstruction, while an older top-level monograph contains a broader “all observables” hierarchy criterion. I’m checking both because your description appears to combine them, and their implications for the proved case are not the same.

[581 | 2026-09-04T19:58:42.621Z | assistant | None]
I found it in [AUTONOMOUS_DEEP_FEATURE_LEARNING_THEOREM_AND_PROGRAM_2026-08-21.md](/home/amir/Codes/PDE/AUTONOMOUS_DEEP_FEATURE_LEARNING_THEOREM_AND_PROGRAM_2026-08-21.md:830), with the formal conjecture beginning around [line 1018](/home/amir/Codes/PDE/AUTONOMOUS_DEEP_FEATURE_LEARNING_THEOREM_AND_PROGRAM_2026-08-21.md:1018).

There is one important correction to your recollection: the Borel conjecture concerns uniform approximation for all times of the loss along one canonical trajectory. It does not currently claim uniform approximation of the complete population state or of all observables.

For the proved two-hidden-layer flow, the exact population dynamics give

\[
\frac{df}{d\tau}=(1-f)K,
\qquad
L=(1-f)^2,
\]

where \(K\) is the population tangent kernel along the trajectory.

If \(K>0\), then \(f\) moves monotonically toward \(1\). Consequently, along this particular trajectory, time can be eliminated: whenever the output has value \(f\), the kernel has some corresponding value

\[
K=\kappa(f).
\]

Thus the exact output trajectory obeys the scalar equation

\[
\frac{df}{d\tau}=(1-f)\kappa(f).
\]

This reduction is exact, but only on the trajectory generated by the prescribed initialization. It does not say that every population state having the same output \(f\) must have the same kernel.

The proposed series begins at initialization:

\[
\kappa(f_0+s)
\sim
\sum_{m=0}^{\infty}c_m s^m.
\]

The coefficients \(c_m\) are supposed to be computable entirely from derivatives of the dynamics at initialization. The conjecture does not expect this ordinary Taylor series necessarily to converge. Instead, it proposes a Borel–Leroy reconstruction: modify the coefficients to obtain a better-behaved series, continue that transformed function, and integrate it back.

Using the first \(N\) initialization coefficients and an \(M\)-point numerical integration gives a finite approximation

\[
\kappa_{N,M}(f),
\]

and therefore a finite scalar autonomous ODE

\[
\frac{df_{N,M}}{d\tau}
=
(1-f_{N,M})\kappa_{N,M}(f_{N,M}),
\qquad
L_{N,M}=(1-f_{N,M})^2.
\]

The formal conjecture is that, for every \(\varepsilon>0\), suitable finite \(N\) and \(M\) can be chosen so that

\[
\sup_{\tau\ge 0}
\left|
L_{N,M}(\tau)-L(\tau)
\right|
\le \varepsilon.
\]

So the intended consequence for the proven case is quite striking:

> The already-proved joint population flow might admit an arbitrarily accurate, width-independent, all-time compression into a single scalar ODE whose coefficients are computed solely from initialization.

But three distinctions matter.

First, “uniform” means uniform over all physical times, not over all observables. The formal statement controls the loss, equivalently the scalar output in this one-sample setting. It does not reconstruct \(Z^{(1)}\), \(Z^{(2)}\), \(W^{(2)}\), the forward and adjoint actions of \(W^{(2)}\), or arbitrary empirical measurements of those objects.

Second, it does not claim that increasing \(M\) by one, or increasing \(N\) by one, must monotonically improve the approximation. Here \(N\) controls the number of initialization coefficients/rational approximation order, while \(M\) controls the numerical integration. The claim is only that sufficiently good finite pairs \((N,M)\) exist. If

\[
\delta_{N,M}
=
\sup_{f\in[f_0,1]}
\left|
\kappa_{N,M}(f)-\kappa(f)
\right|
\]

decreases, then the proved stability estimate makes the all-time loss bound decrease. But monotonic decrease of \(\delta_{N,M}\) itself is not established.

Third, this remains open even though the joint mean-field gradient-flow limit is proved. The proved theorem supplies:

- the exact autonomous population flow;
- compact-time convergence of the finite network to that flow;
- the exact output–kernel identity;
- local positivity and genuine evolution of the kernel.

It does not yet supply the all-order coefficient construction, controlled growth of those coefficients, Borel summability, uniform reconstruction of \(\kappa\), or a positive lower bound

\[
\kappa(f)\ge a>0
\]

over the entire journey from \(f_0\) to \(1\). Those open obligations are listed explicitly [here](/home/amir/Codes/PDE/AUTONOMOUS_DEEP_FEATURE_LEARNING_THEOREM_AND_PROGRAM_2026-08-21.md:1150).

Your “all observables” recollection probably also draws from a different, older hierarchy in [FINITE_CAUSAL_NEURAL_PDE_MASTER_MONOGRAPH_v2.2_2026-07-31.md](/home/amir/Codes/PDE/FINITE_CAUSAL_NEURAL_PDE_MASTER_MONOGRAPH_v2.2_2026-07-31.md:6793). That document conjectures finite systems with decreasing worst-case error for a declared collection of observables, with the strongest version uniform over all time. It is broader, separate from the Borel scalar-output conjecture, and is likewise not proved by the present two-hidden-layer theorem.

In short: the proven joint limit gives the rigorous full population dynamics. The Borel conjecture asks whether the one canonical loss trajectory generated by those dynamics can be compressed, from initialization alone, into arbitrarily accurate finite scalar ODEs. It does not yet give an all-observable approximation hierarchy.

[219 | 2026-09-04T20:08:06.756Z | assistant | None]
I found it in [Section 10, “an MFP-compiled output-kernel Borel hierarchy”](/home/amir/Codes/PDE/AUTONOMOUS_DEEP_FEATURE_LEARNING_THEOREM_AND_PROGRAM_2026-08-21.md:830).

The central idea you remembered is right: after proving the joint mean-field/gradient-flow limit, try to compress its canonical training trajectory into increasingly accurate finite scalar ODEs whose coefficients are calculated entirely at initialization.

But the written conjecture is narrower than your description in three important ways:

- It reconstructs the canonical predictor and loss trajectory, not the full joint population state or all its observables.
- It does not say ordinary series truncations converge. It uses a Borel reconstruction because the raw series may diverge.
- It does not promise that every successive truncation has a smaller error. It says that for every desired error, some sufficiently large finite approximation attains it.

## The object being reconstructed

Let \(f(\tau)\) be the limiting predictor, \(y\) the target, and

\[
\mathcal L(\tau)=(y-f(\tau))^2.
\]

After absorbing the fixed learning-rate factor into the clock \(\tau\), one-sample gradient flow satisfies

\[
\frac{df}{d\tau}=(y-f)K,
\]

where \(K(\tau)\geq 0\) is the current tangent kernel.

The complete autonomous IDE determines both \(f(\tau)\) and \(K(\tau)\) from its current population state. The proposed compression begins with the observation that, as long as

\[
K(\tau)>0
\]

and \(f(\tau)\) has not crossed \(y\), the predictor moves monotonically. Consequently, time can be replaced by the current output as the coordinate along this particular trajectory.

Thus there is a function \(\kappa\), defined along the canonical orbit, such that

\[
K(\tau)=\kappa(f(\tau)).
\]

The predictor then obeys the exact scalar equation

\[
\frac{df}{d\tau}=(y-f)\kappa(f).
\]

This is already a major compression:

\[
\text{full population IDE}
\quad\longrightarrow\quad
\text{one scalar output }f
\text{ driven by one scalar function }\kappa.
\]

But it is only an orbit reduction. Two different population states can have the same \(f\) and different \(K\). Therefore, \(\kappa(f)\) is not asserted to be a valid state law off the canonical trajectory, and the scalar equation cannot generally be restarted from an arbitrary IDE state. The document is explicit about this distinction [here](/home/amir/Codes/PDE/AUTONOMOUS_DEEP_FEATURE_LEARNING_THEOREM_AND_PROGRAM_2026-08-21.md:883).

The conjecture asks whether the entire function \(\kappa\) along that orbit can be reconstructed from initialization information alone.

## How the initialization coefficients are obtained

Write

\[
f_0=f(0),
\qquad
e_0=y-f_0,
\qquad
s=f-f_0.
\]

Here \(s\) is output displacement; I am avoiding \(z\), since \(z^{(\ell)}\) is reserved for network preactivations.

Formally expand the output-coordinate kernel as

\[
\kappa(f_0+s)
\sim
\sum_{m=0}^{\infty}c_m s^m.
\]

The coefficients are not fitted from observed values of \(K(\tau)\). Instead, define the initialization derivatives

\[
d_j
=
\left.
\frac{d^j f}{d\tau^j}
\right|_{\tau=0}.
\]

Because

\[
\frac{ds}{d\tau}
=
(e_0-s)
\sum_{m=0}^{\infty}c_m s^m,
\]

the \(d_j\) determine the \(c_m\) recursively.

For example, at \(\tau=0\),

\[
d_1=e_0c_0,
\]

and therefore

\[
c_0=\frac{d_1}{e_0}=K(0).
\]

Differentiate once:

\[
d_2=(e_0c_1-c_0)d_1,
\]

so

\[
c_1
=
\frac{e_0d_2+d_1^2}{e_0^2d_1}.
\]

Differentiating again gives

\[
c_2
=
\frac{d_3}{2e_0d_1^2}
-
\frac{d_2^2}{2e_0d_1^3}
+
\frac{d_2}{e_0^2d_1}
+
\frac{d_1}{e_0^3}.
\]

These formulas appear in the conjecture [here](/home/amir/Codes/PDE/AUTONOMOUS_DEEP_FEATURE_LEARNING_THEOREM_AND_PROGRAM_2026-08-21.md:889).

Conceptually,

\[
c_0=K(0),
\]

\[
c_1
=
\left.
\frac{dK}{df}
\right|_{f=f_0}
=
\frac{K'(0)}{f'(0)},
\]

and

\[
c_2
=
\frac12
\left.
\frac{d^2K}{df^2}
\right|_{f=f_0}.
\]

Thus the \(c_m\) describe how the tangent kernel changes with output displacement, not directly with time. The conversion from \(d_j\) to \(c_m\) removes the nonuniform speed at which the trajectory moves through output space.

At every finite order the recursion is triangular: \(d_{m+1}\) contains the new coefficient \(c_m\), multiplied by a nonzero quantity determined by \(e_0\) and \(d_1\), plus terms involving only \(c_0,\ldots,c_{m-1}\). Hence \(c_m\) is uniquely determined whenever

\[
e_0\neq0,
\qquad
d_1\neq0.
\]

These conditions mean that initialization is off target and \(K(0)>0\).

### Where MFP enters

To calculate \(d_j\), one does not simulate the network to positive time. Instead:

1. Start from the exact gradient-flow equations for
   \[
   W^{(\ell)},\quad z^{(\ell)},\quad h^{(\ell)}=\phi(z^{(\ell)}).
   \]

2. Differentiate \(f(\tau)\) \(j\) times.

3. Whenever a time derivative such as
   \[
   \frac{dW^{(\ell)}}{d\tau},
   \qquad
   \frac{dz^{(\ell)}}{d\tau},
   \qquad
   \frac{dh^{(\ell)}}{d\tau}
   \]
   appears, replace it by the right-hand side of the gradient-flow equations.

4. After doing this finitely many times, set \(\tau=0\).

For every fixed \(j\), the result is a finite expression made only from initialization objects:

\[
W_0^{(\ell)},
\quad
\bigl(W_0^{(\ell)}\bigr)^\top,
\quad
z_0^{(\ell)},
\quad
\phi(z_0^{(\ell)}),
\quad
\phi'(z_0^{(\ell)}),
\]

together with normalized sums and contractions.

MFP then evaluates that finite expression layer by layer. It conditions on the Gaussian initialization matrices and applies exact Gaussian integration identities. Crucially, it preserves reuse of the same matrix in forward and transpose directions. A call involving \(W_0^{(2)}\) after earlier calls involving \(\bigl(W_0^{(2)}\bigr)^\top\) produces

\[
\text{response forced by the earlier uses}
+
\text{fresh Gaussian randomness in unexplored directions}.
\]

Those response terms are part of the coefficients; treating \(W_0^{(2)}\) and \(\bigl(W_0^{(2)}\bigr)^\top\) as independent would give the wrong derivatives. The precise fixed-program mechanism is summarized in [MFP Reconstruction](/home/amir/Codes/PDE/studies/causal_flow_peeling_calculus/MFP_RECONSTRUCTION.md:7).

After all Gaussian layers have been peeled, \(d_j\) becomes a finite collection of deterministic Gaussian expectations. Therefore

\[
d_1,\ldots,d_{m+1}
\quad\Longrightarrow\quad
c_m
\]

uses only:

- the architecture and its scaling;
- the activation \(\phi\);
- the label \(y\);
- the declared population initialization law.

“Initialization-only” means the population initialization law, not a fitted coefficient obtained from one random finite-width run.

For the proved two-hidden-layer case, \(f_0=0\), \(y=1\), and the audited proof shows

\[
K(0)
=
\mathbb E\!\left[\arctan(Z)^2\right]
>0
\]

for an explicitly determined initial Gaussian \(Z\). It also proves

\[
K'(0)=0.
\]

Consequently, its first output-coordinate coefficients satisfy

\[
c_0=K(0),
\qquad
c_1=0.
\]

The first nonconstant coefficient \(c_2\) comes from the reused-adjoint response at initialization and is strictly positive. The relevant initialization calculation and small-time expansion are given in the [audited proof](/tmp/L2_FULL_AUDITED_PROOF.md:838) and [its kernel expansion](/tmp/L2_FULL_AUDITED_PROOF.md:939). This is a concrete example of a genuinely feature-learning coefficient being computed without seeing any positive-time trajectory.

## Why Borel reconstruction is needed

The raw coefficients may grow too quickly for

\[
\sum_{m=0}^{\infty}c_m s^m
\]

to converge. The conjecture therefore proposes finding a finite number \(\sigma>0\) such that the factorially rescaled series

\[
\mathcal B_\sigma\kappa(\xi)
=
\sum_{m=0}^{\infty}
\frac{c_m}{\Gamma(1+\sigma m)}\xi^m
\]

does converge near \(\xi=0\).

If this transformed function can be continued along the positive direction with suitable growth control, the original kernel is reconstructed by

\[
\kappa(f_0+s)
=
\int_0^\infty
e^{-u}
\mathcal B_\sigma\kappa(su^\sigma)\,du.
\]

The normalization is chosen because, formally,

\[
\int_0^\infty
e^{-u}
\frac{(su^\sigma)^m}{\Gamma(1+\sigma m)}
\,du
=
s^m.
\]

So the integral reverses the factorial rescaling term by term. The difficult part is justifying the continuation, the integral, the exchange of limits, and—most importantly—proving that the reconstructed function is the actual \(\kappa\) generated by the IDE.

For a finite approximation:

- \(N\) controls how many initialization coefficients are used to build a rational approximation \(B_N\) of the Borel transform.
- \(M\) is the number of quadrature points used to approximate the integral.

Thus

\[
\kappa_{N,M}(f_0+s)
=
\sum_{j=1}^{M}
w_jB_N(su_j^\sigma),
\]

and the resulting finite autonomous scalar ODE is

\[
\frac{df_{N,M}}{d\tau}
=
(y-f_{N,M})
\kappa_{N,M}(f_{N,M}),
\qquad
f_{N,M}(0)=f_0.
\]

Every model-dependent number in this ODE comes from finitely many initialization derivatives. The quadrature rule is universal.

The exact conjecture is:

\[
\text{for every }\varepsilon>0,
\quad
\text{there exist finite }N,M
\]

such that

\[
\sup_{\tau\geq0}
\left|
\mathcal L_{N,M}(\tau)-\mathcal L(\tau)
\right|
\leq\varepsilon.
\]

See the exact quantifiers [here](/home/amir/Codes/PDE/AUTONOMOUS_DEEP_FEATURE_LEARNING_THEOREM_AND_PROGRAM_2026-08-21.md:1018).

This does not imply

\[
\text{error at }N+1
\leq
\text{error at }N.
\]

Rational approximations can temporarily worsen, acquire bad poles, and then improve again. What is conjectured is convergence along a declared approximation scheme—not monotone improvement after every increment.

## What must actually be proved

The proof obligations form one continuous chain.

1. **Every fixed coefficient must be correct.**

   For each fixed \(j\), one must show that the MFP calculation equals the actual derivative

   \[
   \left.
   \frac{d^jf}{d\tau^j}
   \right|_{\tau=0}
   \]

   of the limiting IDE.

   Fixed-order MFP is legitimate, but compact-time convergence of \(f_n\) to \(f\) does not by itself imply convergence of their derivatives. Moreover, proving every separately fixed order does not give estimates uniform as \(j\to\infty\).

2. **The entire coefficient sequence must have controlled growth.**

   One needs constants \(C,A<\infty\) and a finite \(\sigma\) such that

   \[
   |c_m|
   \leq
   CA^m\Gamma(1+\sigma m)
   \]

   for every \(m\).

   Bounds on the time derivatives \(d_j\) must also survive division by \(y-f\) and the inversion that converts time into output displacement. Finite algebraic invertibility does not automatically yield a uniform bound on the resulting \(c_m\).

3. **The Borel transform must reconstruct the actual kernel.**

   Local convergence of \(\mathcal B_\sigma\kappa\) is insufficient. It must have an unambiguous continuation along the required direction, obey suitable growth bounds, and its Laplace integral must equal the IDE-generated \(\kappa\).

   This identification is indispensable because a nonzero function such as

   \[
   e^{-1/s^{1/\sigma}},
   \qquad s>0,
   \]

   has every derivative equal to zero at \(s=0\). Thus even the complete initialization jet can fail to identify a positive-\(s\) function unless one proves an additional reconstruction principle.

4. **The finite rational and quadrature approximations must converge uniformly.**

   The required quantity is

   \[
   \delta_{N,M}
   =
   \sup_{f_0\leq u\leq y}
   \left|
   \kappa_{N,M}(u)-\kappa(u)
   \right|.
   \]

   One must prove

   \[
   \delta_{N,M}\longrightarrow0
   \]

   along a specified choice of \(N,M\), preferably with a computable error bound. This includes controlling spurious rational-approximation poles, the infinite integration tail, and quadrature error uniformly over the entire output interval.

5. **Kernel error must imply trajectory error for all time.**

   Suppose

   \[
   0<a\leq\kappa(u)\leq K_{\max}
   \qquad
   \text{for }f_0\leq u\leq y
   \]

   and \(\delta_{N,M}<a\). Then the approximation remains positive and the document proves

   \[
   \sup_{\tau\geq0}
   |\mathcal L_{N,M}(\tau)-\mathcal L(\tau)|
   \leq
   \frac{K_{\max}e_0^2}{a\,\mathrm e}
   \left[
   -\log\left(1-\frac{\delta_{N,M}}{a}\right)
   \right].
   \]

   Hence uniform approximation of \(\kappa\) on the finite output interval gives uniform approximation of the loss over the entire infinite time axis. This transfer theorem is already available; producing \(\delta_{N,M}\to0\) and proving the global lower bound \(a>0\) are the hard parts.

These are also the five obligations recorded by the document [here](/home/amir/Codes/PDE/AUTONOMOUS_DEEP_FEATURE_LEARNING_THEOREM_AND_PROGRAM_2026-08-21.md:1150).

## What this adds to the proved two-hidden-layer result

The current theorem already proves:

\[
\text{finite-width GD}
\longrightarrow
\text{unique autonomous population IDE}
\]

uniformly on every fixed time interval, including convergence of \(f_n\), \(K_n\), residual, and loss. That result is summarized [here](/home/amir/Codes/PDE/AUTONOMOUS_DEEP_FEATURE_LEARNING_THEOREM_AND_PROGRAM_2026-08-21.md:295).

The Borel conjecture would add a second approximation:

\[
\text{autonomous population IDE}
\longrightarrow
\text{finite initialization-compiled scalar ODE}.
\]

Thus, on a fixed time interval, the complete comparison would be

\[
\begin{aligned}
|\mathcal L_{n,\eta}(t)-\mathcal L_{N,M}(t)|
\leq{}&
|\mathcal L_{n,\eta}(t)-\mathcal L(t)|
\\
&+
|\mathcal L(t)-\mathcal L_{N,M}(t)|.
\end{aligned}
\]

The first term is controlled by the proved joint mean-field/gradient-flow theorem. The second is the conjectural Borel compression.

This would be remarkable because, after the coefficients were compiled, one could reproduce the canonical limiting loss trajectory without evolving the population fields, \(W^{(2)}\), its adjoint action, or any stored history. But it would not replace the IDE as the complete state law.

For the proven case, the outstanding points are still:

- the all-order coefficient bounds;
- reconstruction of the actual \(\kappa\) from its jet;
- uniform rational-plus-quadrature convergence;
- a global positive lower bound for \(K\) up to the target.

The existing proof establishes \(K(0)>0\), short-time positivity, nonconstant kernel evolution, and global well-posedness. It does not establish a uniform positive lower bound for all output values between \(f_0\) and \(y\), nor does it prove that the low-order jet ODEs approximate positive time. The status ledger states this explicitly [here](/home/amir/Codes/PDE/AUTONOMOUS_DEEP_FEATURE_LEARNING_THEOREM_AND_PROGRAM_2026-08-21.md:1483).

A later audit does not disprove this conjecture. It disproves the shortcut

\[
\text{fixed-order MFP}
+
\text{Gevrey growth}
\quad\Longrightarrow\quad
\text{automatic reconstruction of the real flow}.
\]

It shows that continuation, growth, identification, and a genuine truncation modulus are independent obligations [GEVREY/Borel audit](/home/amir/Codes/PDE/studies/causal_flow_peeling_calculus/GEVREY_BOREL_COMPLETION_AUDIT.md:473). So the conjecture remains open, while one proposed automatic proof of it has been ruled out.

## The stronger “all observables” version

What you described would be a strictly stronger conjecture.

A statement uniform over literally every observable is impossible without normalizing the observables: if an observable has error \(e\), multiplying it by \(10^{100}\) multiplies the error by \(10^{100}\).

One meaningful version would choose a normalized class \(\mathcal A\), for example bounded observables that are \(1\)-Lipschitz in the joint action-law state, and require

\[
\sup_{\Psi\in\mathcal A}
\sup_{0\leq t\leq T}
\left|
\Psi(\Theta_M(t))
-
\Psi(\Theta(t))
\right|
\longrightarrow0,
\]

where \(\Theta(t)\) denotes the full limiting state containing the population fields and the two-sided action of \(W^{(2)}\).

To prove that, one would need either:

- a Borel reconstruction of the entire state \(\Theta(t)\) in a metric strong enough to control every \(\Psi\in\mathcal A\); or
- a separate compatible Borel hierarchy for every observable.

It would also require compatibility between all reconstructed observables so that they truly come from one common state, plus autonomy and restartability of that reconstructed state. None of these properties follows from reconstructing the scalar function \(\kappa(f)\).

So the clean conclusion is:

\[
\boxed{\text{The written conjecture is a predictor/loss Borel hierarchy, not an all-observable state hierarchy.}}
\]

It sits on top of the proved joint limit as an initialization-only compression of its canonical orbit. An all-observable uniform hierarchy would be a deeper and substantially stronger next conjecture.
