[278 | 2026-09-04T21:16:53.957Z | assistant | None]
I completed two blind review rounds, with three fresh reviewers in each. The final three reported no remaining mathematical gaps. The [complete proof file](/tmp/l2-proof-review-wBzgFS/TWO_HIDDEN_LAYER_PROOF.md) and [review record](/tmp/l2-proof-review-wBzgFS/REVIEW.md) are saved.

We work at width \(n\), keeping the width dependence implicit in the network arrays. Every normalization by width is written explicitly.

There is one input, equal to \(1\), and target \(1\). The parameters are

\[
z^{(1)}=W^{(1)}\in\mathbb R^n,
\qquad
W^{(2)}\in\mathbb R^{n\times n},
\qquad
W^{(3)}\in\mathbb R^n.
\]

Throughout, \(W^{(3)}\) means the rescaled output weight. The network is

\[
h^{(1)}=\phi(z^{(1)}),
\qquad
z^{(2)}=W^{(2)}h^{(1)},
\qquad
h^{(2)}=\phi(z^{(2)}),
\qquad
\phi(s)=\arctan s,
\]

\[
f_n=\frac{(W^{(3)})^\top h^{(2)}}n,
\qquad
r_n=f_n-1,
\qquad
L_n=r_n^2.
\]

All initial entries are independent, with

\[
z_{0,i}^{(1)}\sim N(0,1),
\qquad
W_{0,ji}^{(2)}\sim N(0,1/n),
\qquad
W_{0,j}^{(3)}\sim N(0,1/n^2).
\]

Define the backpropagation derivatives without the loss residual:

\[
\delta^{(2)}
=
W^{(3)}\odot\phi'(z^{(2)}),
\]

\[
\delta^{(1)}
=
\phi'(z^{(1)})
\odot
(W^{(2)})^\top\delta^{(2)}.
\]

Thus \(\delta^{(\ell)}=n\,\partial f_n/\partial z^{(\ell)}\). The exact GD updates are

\[
\begin{aligned}
z_{k+1}^{(1)}
&=
z_k^{(1)}-2\eta_n r_{n,k}\delta_k^{(1)},\\
W_{k+1}^{(2)}
&=
W_k^{(2)}
-\frac{2\eta_n r_{n,k}}n
\delta_k^{(2)}(h_k^{(1)})^\top,\\
W_{k+1}^{(3)}
&=
W_k^{(3)}-2\eta_n r_{n,k}h_k^{(2)}.
\end{aligned}
\tag{1}
\]

Use the clock \(t=k\eta_n\), interpolate these three parameters linearly, and recompute the remaining quantities from them. Assume the positive learning rates satisfy

\[
\eta_n\sqrt n\longrightarrow0.
\]

In particular, this includes the original choice \(\eta_n=n^{-2}\).

We will construct an autonomous population gradient flow and prove convergence to it on every fixed interval \([0,T]\). The convergence will retain both directions of the learned matrix action, the hidden trajectory laws, predictions, losses, kernel blocks, and integrated squared hidden velocities. We will also verify that both hidden layers move and that the limiting kernel changes.

Capital letters \(Z^{(\ell)},H^{(\ell)},X^{(1)}\) denote typical population coordinates. We retain \(W^{(2)},W^{(3)},\delta^{(\ell)}\) for their population counterparts, identifying explicitly when we pass to the population system. For a scalar population variable \(V\), write

\[
\|V\|_{L^2}=\sqrt{\mathbb E[V^2]}.
\]

This is its root-mean-square size. The corresponding finite-vector size is always written as \(\|v\|_2/\sqrt n\).

First consider the finite-width continuous flow associated with (1). Define

\[
F(s)=s+\frac{s^3}{3},
\qquad
x^{(1)}=F(z^{(1)}).
\]

Since \(F'(s)\phi'(s)=1\), the flow becomes

\[
\begin{aligned}
\dot x^{(1)}
&=-2r_n(W^{(2)})^\top\delta^{(2)},\\
\dot W^{(2)}
&=-\frac{2r_n}{n}\delta^{(2)}(h^{(1)})^\top,\\
\dot W^{(3)}
&=-2r_nh^{(2)}.
\end{aligned}
\tag{2}
\]

This coordinate change is exact in continuous time. Euler with an auxiliary mesh \(\Delta\) replaces each derivative in (2) by its forward difference. In particular,

\[
x_{k+1}^{(1)}
=
x_k^{(1)}
-
2\Delta r_{n,k}(W_k^{(2)})^\top\delta_k^{(2)}.
\tag{3}
\]

Transforming the actual GD iterates produces an additional error, which we estimate later.

Iterating the Euler matrix update gives

\[
W_k^{(2)}
=
W_0^{(2)}
-
\frac{2\Delta}{n}
\sum_{s<k}
r_{n,s}\delta_s^{(2)}(h_s^{(1)})^\top.
\tag{4}
\]

Therefore every forward action can be written as

\[
W_k^{(2)}v
=
W_0^{(2)}v
-
2\Delta\sum_{s<k}
r_{n,s}\delta_s^{(2)}
\frac{(h_s^{(1)})^\top v}{n},
\tag{5}
\]

and every backward action as

\[
(W_k^{(2)})^\top u
=
(W_0^{(2)})^\top u
-
2\Delta\sum_{s<k}
r_{n,s}h_s^{(1)}
\frac{(\delta_s^{(2)})^\top u}{n}.
\tag{6}
\]

Training therefore adds a finite sum of rank-one matrices. For fixed \(\Delta\) and \(T\), there are only finitely many calls to the initial Gaussian matrix and finitely many scalar contractions.

The coefficients in (5)–(6) are random empirical quantities. We first analyze an auxiliary finite system—the oracle—that uses deterministic population residuals and contractions instead. Those numbers will be constructed from the population action below. We then prove that the oracle’s empirical quantities approach the supplied numbers and compare it with the actual Euler system.

The first matrix call is

\[
z_0^{(2)}=W_0^{(2)}h_0^{(1)}.
\]

Conditional on \(h_0^{(1)}\), its coordinates are independent centered Gaussians with variance \(\|h_0^{(1)}\|_2^2/n\). Thus

\[
Z_0^{(1)}\sim N(0,1),
\qquad
H_0^{(1)}=\phi(Z_0^{(1)}),
\]

\[
Z_0^{(2)}
\sim
N\!\left(0,\mathbb E[(H_0^{(1)})^2]\right).
\tag{7}
\]

The finite vectors in the following first-call examples are oracle vectors. The oracle starts with \(W_0^{(3)}=0\), so \(r_0=-1\). Its first update leaves the hidden parameters unchanged and gives

\[
W_1^{(3)}=2\Delta\phi(z_0^{(2)}),
\]

\[
\delta_1^{(2)}
=
2\Delta\phi(z_0^{(2)})
\odot\phi'(z_0^{(2)}).
\]

The next transpose call uses the same matrix that produced \(z_0^{(2)}\). For a nonzero vector \(b\), define

\[
P_{b^\perp}
=
I-\frac{bb^\top}{b^\top b}.
\]

Condition each Gaussian row of \(W_0^{(2)}\) on its observed dot product with \(h_0^{(1)}\), then sum the rows with coefficients \(\delta_{1,j}^{(2)}\). This gives

\[
\begin{aligned}
(W_0^{(2)})^\top\delta_1^{(2)}
\overset d={}&
\frac{(z_0^{(2)})^\top\delta_1^{(2)}}
{\|h_0^{(1)}\|_2^2}
h_0^{(1)}
\\
&+
\frac{\|\delta_1^{(2)}\|_2}{\sqrt n}
P_{(h_0^{(1)})^\perp}\gamma^{(1)},
\end{aligned}
\tag{8}
\]

where the equality is conditional in distribution and \(\gamma^{(1)}\sim N(0,I_n)\) is independent of the preceding history.

The first term supplies exactly the overlap required by

\[
(h_0^{(1)})^\top
(W_0^{(2)})^\top\delta_1^{(2)}
=
(z_0^{(2)})^\top\delta_1^{(2)}.
\]

The new Gaussian part is perpendicular to \(h_0^{(1)}\), so it cannot supply that overlap.

The next first-layer update is

\[
x_2^{(1)}
=
x_0^{(1)}
-
2\Delta r_1(W_0^{(2)})^\top\delta_1^{(2)},
\qquad
h_2^{(1)}=\phi(F^{-1}(x_2^{(1)})),
\]

where

\[
r_1
=
2\Delta\mathbb E[\phi(Z_0^{(2)})^2]-1.
\]

Now condition on both the previous forward and transpose observations. The next initial-matrix call satisfies

\[
\begin{aligned}
W_0^{(2)}h_2^{(1)}
\overset d={}&
\frac{(h_0^{(1)})^\top h_2^{(1)}}
{\|h_0^{(1)}\|_2^2}
z_0^{(2)}
\\
&+
\frac{
[P_{(h_0^{(1)})^\perp}
(W_0^{(2)})^\top\delta_1^{(2)}]^\top h_2^{(1)}
}{
\|\delta_1^{(2)}\|_2^2
}
\delta_1^{(2)}
\\
&+
\frac{
\|P_{(h_0^{(1)})^\perp}h_2^{(1)}\|_2
}{\sqrt n}
P_{(\delta_1^{(2)})^\perp}\gamma^{(2)}.
\end{aligned}
\tag{9}
\]

Here \(\gamma^{(2)}\sim N(0,I_n)\) is independent of the previous history. The denominators are positive almost surely for \(\Delta>0\).

The first response retains the old forward answer. The second supplies the remaining overlap required by the old transpose answer. The Gaussian term disturbs neither observation. Formula (5), with the oracle’s population coefficients, then adds the learned rank-one correction to obtain its preactivation node.

We now prove that this calculation can be repeated through any fixed finite sequence of the operations needed here.

Consider a finite calculation whose initial coordinate tuples are iid with finite second moments and independent of \(W_0^{(2)}\). Its instructions are globally Lipschitz coordinate functions, linear combinations with fixed deterministic coefficients, and calls to \(W_0^{(2)}\) or its transpose. The initial tuples may include both \(z_0^{(1)}\) and \(F(z_0^{(1)})\), since the latter has a finite second moment.

The claim is that every same-layer joint empirical law has a deterministic limit, including its second moments.

We use the distance \(\mathcal W_2\): the smallest root-mean-square distance between random vectors with two specified laws. Matching corresponding coordinates gives

\[
\mathcal W_2\!\left(
\frac1n\sum_i\delta_{v_i},
\frac1n\sum_i\delta_{\widetilde v_i}
\right)^2
\le
\frac1n\sum_i\|v_i-\widetilde v_i\|_2^2.
\tag{10}
\]

Here \(\delta_a\) denotes a unit probability mass at \(a\). Convergence in \(\mathcal W_2\) means weak convergence together with convergence of second moments. It therefore includes convergence of all dot products within a fixed joint tuple.

First, the initial matrix has a uniform operator bound. A \(1/4\)-net of the Euclidean unit sphere contains at most \(9^n\) points. Using two such nets and the Gaussian tail of each bilinear form gives

\[
\mathbb P\!\left(\|W_0^{(2)}\|_{\mathrm{op}}>M\right)
\le
2\,9^{2n}e^{-nM^2/8}.
\tag{11}
\]

Indeed, the operator norm is at most twice the largest bilinear form on the two nets, and each such form is \(N(0,1/n)\). Choose a fixed sufficiently large \(M\).

The remaining difficulty is that previous query directions may become linearly dependent. To handle this without assuming stable inverses of singular Gram matrices, temporarily add \(\varepsilon\xi\) to the input of each matrix call, where each \(\xi\) is a fresh independent standard Gaussian vector. Fix \(\varepsilon>0\); we will remove it after proving convergence.

Suppose previous calls have revealed

\[
W_0^{(2)}V=Y,
\qquad
(W_0^{(2)})^\top U=R.
\]

The columns of \(V\) are previous first-layer input vectors, and those of \(U\) are previous second-layer input vectors. The columns of \(Y,R\) are their answers. Their numbers of columns are fixed.

Conditioning on the roots and previous answers leaves

\[
\begin{aligned}
W_0^{(2)}
\overset d={}&
Y(V^\top V)^{-1}V^\top
\\
&+
U(U^\top U)^{-1}R^\top P_{V^\perp}
\\
&+
P_{U^\perp}\widetilde W_0^{(2)}P_{V^\perp},
\end{aligned}
\tag{12}
\]

where

\[
P_{V^\perp}=I-V(V^\top V)^{-1}V^\top,
\]

and similarly for \(U\). Terms associated with empty column lists are zero.

The first two terms satisfy both recorded equations. The last term lies precisely in the matrix directions that preserve both equations. Orthogonal components of an isotropic Gaussian are independent, which proves the formula. It also applies to adaptive queries: each new input is determined before its answer is observed, using previous answers and independent roots.

For the next first-layer input \(v\), multiplication gives

\[
W_0^{(2)}v
\overset d=
Y\alpha_n+U\beta_n
+
\frac{\|P_{V^\perp}v\|_2}{\sqrt n}
P_{U^\perp}\gamma,
\tag{13}
\]

where

\[
\alpha_n
=
(V^\top V/n)^{-1}(V^\top v/n),
\]

\[
\beta_n
=
(U^\top U/n)^{-1}
(R^\top P_{V^\perp}v/n).
\]

The transpose case exchanges the two layers.

The induction statement is joint \(\mathcal W_2\) convergence of every previously constructed tuple on each layer. Equivalently, its empirical averages converge for every continuous measurement \(\psi\) satisfying

\[
|\psi(s)|\le C(1+\|s\|_2^2).
\]

The choices \(\psi(s)=s^2\) and \(\psi(s,t)=st\) give the squared norms and contractions in (13). Revealing a new independent input noise also preserves this statement by conditional Gaussian averaging.

The perturbation ensures that the limiting input Gram matrices are invertible. When a new column \(a+\varepsilon\xi\) is added to a previous input list,

\[
\frac{
\|P_{V^\perp}(a+\varepsilon\xi)\|_2^2
}{n}
=
\frac{\|P_{V^\perp}a\|_2^2}{n}
+
\varepsilon^2
+
o_{\mathbb P}(1).
\tag{14}
\]

The cross term has conditional variance at most

\[
\frac{4\varepsilon^2\|a\|_2^2}{n^2},
\]

and the projection removes only a fixed number of Gaussian directions. Thus the new column has limiting squared distance at least \(\varepsilon^2\) from the old span. Each enlarged limiting Gram matrix is consequently positive definite, and its inverse converges.

Therefore the response coefficients and Gaussian variance in (13) converge. Also, writing \(P_U=I-P_{U^\perp}\),

\[
\mathbb E\!\left[
\frac{\|P_U\gamma\|_2^2}{n}
\,\middle|\,
\text{history}
\right]
=
\frac{\operatorname{rank}(U)}n
\longrightarrow0.
\tag{15}
\]

We may therefore remove the projection at a vanishing mean-square cost. Replacing the convergent coefficients by their limits also has vanishing mean-square cost.

What remains is an old coordinate function plus independent scalar Gaussians. For a bounded Lipschitz measurement, its empirical average has conditional variance bounded by a constant divided by \(n\). Its conditional expectation is an empirical average of a function of the old coordinates, obtained by averaging over one scalar Gaussian; the previous induction hypothesis controls it.

The new squared norm also concentrates: its conditional variance is

\[
O\!\left(
\frac{1+\|Y\alpha_n+U\beta_n\|_2^2/n}{n}
\right).
\]

Thus both the joint law and its second moments converge. Globally Lipschitz coordinate operations preserve this convergence, closing the induction for fixed \(\varepsilon\).

Finally, couple the perturbed and unperturbed calculations using the same roots and matrix. On the event in (11), and the event that the finitely many \(\|\xi\|_2/\sqrt n\) are bounded, induction through the instructions gives

\[
\max_{\text{constructed }v}
\frac{\|v^\varepsilon-v\|_2}{\sqrt n}
\le C\varepsilon.
\tag{16}
\]

Each coordinate map multiplies an error by its fixed Lipschitz constant; each matrix call multiplies it by at most \(M\), plus the newly injected error. The constant depends on the fixed calculation, but not on \(n\) or \(0<\varepsilon\le1\).

Both events have probability tending to one. By (10), the deterministic limiting laws for positive \(\varepsilon\) form a Cauchy family in \(\mathcal W_2\). Its limit is also the limit of the unperturbed empirical laws: first take \(n\to\infty\), then \(\varepsilon\to0\).

This proves the finite Gaussian statement, including dependent unperturbed query directions. No central limit theorem is needed.

We next turn these finite calculations into one population matrix action.

Take a countable collection of calculations closed under rational linear combinations, initial-matrix calls in both directions, the Lipschitz maps just used, and a countable family of bounded Lipschitz functions of finite coordinate lists that is dense among continuous functions on compact sets. Include constants and clipping at every integer level.

Every product included in this calculation class must have both varying factors bounded—either intrinsically or by clipping—so that the complete coordinate function is globally Lipschitz. Products involving an unbounded backward field will be handled separately by truncation.

The finite joint limiting laws are consistent because any finite union of calculations is another finite calculation. They therefore define two countable random coordinate collections, one per layer. Take their probability spaces to contain precisely the information generated by those coordinates.

Identify two first-layer probes when

\[
v\sim\widetilde v
\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad
\frac{\|v-\widetilde v\|_2^2}{n}
\longrightarrow0
\quad\text{in probability},
\tag{17}
\]

and do the same separately on the second layer. The limiting squared distance is \(\mathbb E[(V-\widetilde V)^2]\), so these classes correspond to population random variables identified when equal almost surely.

Define

\[
W_0^{(2)}[v]:=[W_0^{(2)}v].
\]

This is independent of the representative because, on the event in (11),

\[
\frac{
\|W_0^{(2)}v-W_0^{(2)}\widetilde v\|_2^2
}{n}
\le
M^2\frac{\|v-\widetilde v\|_2^2}{n}.
\]

Linearity passes directly from the finite matrix, as does the bound

\[
\mathbb E[(W_0^{(2)}V)^2]
\le
M^2\mathbb E[V^2].
\tag{18}
\]

Taking mean-square limits extends the action uniquely to all square-integrable variables on the generated first-layer probability space. The included bounded coordinate functions give the required density: finite-coordinate functions approximate variables on the generated space, and bounded continuous functions approximate those in mean square.

Construct the reverse action from \((W_0^{(2)})^\top\) in the same way. At finite width,

\[
\frac{u^\top W_0^{(2)}v}{n}
=
\frac{[(W_0^{(2)})^\top u]^\top v}{n}.
\]

Passing to the limit proves

\[
\mathbb E[U\,W_0^{(2)}V]
=
\mathbb E[((W_0^{(2)})^*U)V].
\tag{19}
\]

Thus the reverse action is the adjoint of the same population operator. Arbitrary fixed real coefficients are obtained by rational approximation, using the preceding stability bounds.

On these fixed spaces, the population state consists of

\[
X^{(1)},\qquad W^{(2)},\qquad W^{(3)}.
\]

Its derived quantities and dynamics are

\[
\begin{gathered}
Z^{(1)}=F^{-1}(X^{(1)}),
\qquad
H^{(1)}=\phi(Z^{(1)}),
\\
Z^{(2)}=W^{(2)}H^{(1)},
\qquad
H^{(2)}=\phi(Z^{(2)}),
\\
f=\mathbb E[W^{(3)}H^{(2)}],
\qquad
r=f-1,
\qquad
\delta^{(2)}=W^{(3)}\phi'(Z^{(2)}),
\\[2mm]
\dot X^{(1)}
=
-2r(W^{(2)})^*\delta^{(2)},
\\
\dot W^{(2)}
=
-2r\,\delta^{(2)}\otimes H^{(1)},
\\
\dot W^{(3)}
=
-2rH^{(2)}.
\end{gathered}
\tag{20}
\]

The rank-one action means

\[
(\delta^{(2)}\otimes H^{(1)})V
=
\delta^{(2)}\mathbb E[H^{(1)}V].
\]

Initially,

\[
X_0^{(1)}=F(Z_0^{(1)}),
\qquad
W^{(2)}(0)=W_0^{(2)},
\qquad
W^{(3)}(0)=0.
\]

There are three evolving objects, but the spaces of population coordinates are infinite dimensional.

We now prove that this evolution exists uniquely for all finite times and that Euler approximates it uniformly. The same estimates will work at finite width.

Measure the distance between two population states by

\[
\begin{aligned}
d={}&
\|X^{(1)}-\widetilde X^{(1)}\|_{L^2}
\\
&+
\|W^{(2)}-\widetilde W^{(2)}\|_{\mathrm{op}}
+
\|W^{(3)}-\widetilde W^{(3)}\|_{L^2}.
\end{aligned}
\tag{21}
\]

At finite width replace each variable’s \(L^2\) norm by its Euclidean norm divided by \(\sqrt n\); call the resulting distance \(d_n\).

Both \(F^{-1}\) and \(\phi\circ F^{-1}\) are 1-Lipschitz. The functions \(\phi,\phi'\) are bounded and Lipschitz. If both operator norms are at most \(M\), and both output weights are pointwise bounded by \(B\), then

\[
\begin{aligned}
\|Z^{(2)}-\widetilde Z^{(2)}\|_{L^2}
\le{}&
\frac\pi2
\|W^{(2)}-\widetilde W^{(2)}\|_{\mathrm{op}}
\\
&+
M\|X^{(1)}-\widetilde X^{(1)}\|_{L^2},
\end{aligned}
\]

\[
\begin{aligned}
\|\delta^{(2)}-\widetilde\delta^{(2)}\|_{L^2}
\le{}&
\|W^{(3)}-\widetilde W^{(3)}\|_{L^2}
\\
&+
B\operatorname{Lip}(\phi')
\|Z^{(2)}-\widetilde Z^{(2)}\|_{L^2},
\end{aligned}
\]

\[
\begin{aligned}
&
\|(W^{(2)})^*\delta^{(2)}
-(\widetilde W^{(2)})^*\widetilde\delta^{(2)}\|_{L^2}
\\
&\le
\|W^{(2)}-\widetilde W^{(2)}\|_{\mathrm{op}}
\|\delta^{(2)}\|_{L^2}
+
M\|\delta^{(2)}-\widetilde\delta^{(2)}\|_{L^2}.
\end{aligned}
\tag{22}
\]

Cauchy–Schwarz gives the corresponding output estimate. For the matrix update,

\[
\begin{aligned}
\|a\otimes b-\widetilde a\otimes\widetilde b\|_{\mathrm{op}}
\le{}&
\|a-\widetilde a\|_{L^2}\|b\|_{L^2}
\\
&+
\|\widetilde a\|_{L^2}\|b-\widetilde b\|_{L^2}.
\end{aligned}
\]

Together these make the vector field Lipschitz in (21) on the indicated bounded sets, with constants independent of width.

For local existence, replace a candidate path by the initial state plus the integral of the right-hand side of (20). On a sufficiently short interval this map preserves a closed set of continuous paths with the stated bounds and contracts the supremum of (21). The set is complete: mean-square limits preserve a common pointwise bound on \(W^{(3)}\). Iteration therefore converges to a unique solution.

To continue the solution, differentiate the output using (19):

\[
\dot f=-2rK,
\qquad
\dot L=-4r^2K,
\qquad
K=K^{(1)}+K^{(2)}+K^{(3)},
\tag{23}
\]

where

\[
K^{(1)}
=
\mathbb E\!\left[
\bigl(
\phi'(Z^{(1)})(W^{(2)})^*\delta^{(2)}
\bigr)^2
\right],
\]

\[
K^{(2)}
=
\mathbb E[(H^{(1)})^2]\,
\mathbb E[(\delta^{(2)})^2],
\qquad
K^{(3)}
=
\mathbb E[(H^{(2)})^2].
\tag{24}
\]

These equations also verify the gradient-flow interpretation. In the untransformed population coordinates, the loss gradients are

\[
2r\delta^{(1)},
\qquad
2r\,\delta^{(2)}\otimes H^{(1)},
\qquad
2rH^{(2)},
\]

with

\[
\delta^{(1)}
=
\phi'(Z^{(1)})(W^{(2)})^*\delta^{(2)}.
\]

For matrix changes, the population counterpart of the ordinary sum-of-squared-entries geometry is defined on rank-one changes by

\[
\langle a\otimes b,c\otimes d\rangle
=
\mathbb E[ac]\,\mathbb E[bd],
\]

then extended by linearity and completion. In particular, for a matrix-action perturbation \(A\),

\[
\langle\delta^{(2)}\otimes H^{(1)},A\rangle
=
\mathbb E[\delta^{(2)}\,AH^{(1)}].
\]

This verifies the matrix gradient. The change \(W^{(2)}(t)-W_0^{(2)}\) belongs to this completed space because it is the integral of rank-one changes of bounded norm.

The chain rules here hold along the constructed mean-square differentiable paths. Bounded scalar derivatives justify difference quotients first for bounded directions, then for general square-integrable directions by approximation.

Since \(K\ge0\),

\[
|r(t)|\le |r(0)|.
\]

Consequently, on \([0,T]\),

\[
\|W^{(3)}(t)\|_\infty
\le
\|W^{(3)}(0)\|_\infty+\pi|r(0)|T,
\]

\[
\|\dot W^{(2)}\|_{\mathrm{op}}
\le
\pi|r(0)|\|W^{(3)}\|_{L^2},
\]

\[
\|\dot X^{(1)}\|_{L^2}
\le
2|r(0)|\|W^{(2)}\|_{\mathrm{op}}
\|W^{(3)}\|_{L^2}.
\tag{25}
\]

These bounds prevent finite-time blow-up. At finite width, the same bounds hold on an event of probability tending to one, using (11), the initial law of large numbers for \(F(z_0^{(1)})\), and

\[
\|W_0^{(3)}\|_\infty\longrightarrow0
\quad\text{in probability}.
\]

Let \(M_T\) bound the vector field and let \(L_T\) be its Lipschitz constant on a slightly larger bounded set. The local Euler error satisfies

\[
\begin{aligned}
&
\left\|
\int_t^{t+\Delta}
\bigl(\dot\Theta(s)-\dot\Theta(t)\bigr)\,ds
\right\|
\\
&\le
\int_t^{t+\Delta}L_TM_T(s-t)\,ds
=
\frac12L_TM_T\Delta^2.
\end{aligned}
\]

Thus the accumulated error obeys

\[
e_{k+1}
\le
(1+L_T\Delta)e_k+C_T\Delta^2,
\]

and summing the geometric series gives

\[
\max_{k\Delta\le T}e_k\le C_T'\Delta.
\tag{26}
\]

To justify the bounded set for Euler, stop at its first exit. The estimate keeps \(X^{(1)}\) and \(W^{(2)}\) within their margins. The output supremum requires a separate observation:

\[
\|W_k^{(3)}\|_\infty
\le
\|W_0^{(3)}\|_\infty
+
\pi\Delta\sum_{s<k}|r_s|.
\]

Before the stop, (22) and (26) give

\[
|r_s|\le |r(0)|+C_T\Delta.
\]

Choose the output supremum margin larger than \(\pi T(|r(0)|+1)\). For small \(\Delta\), no first exit can occur. This proves (26), including between grid times, both for finite-width Euler and population Euler.

We can now define the oracle precisely. Add the subscript “oracle” to distinguish its finite arrays; this changes no normalization.

For fixed population Euler mesh \(\Delta\), the numbers

\[
r_k=\mathbb E[W_k^{(3)}H_k^{(2)}]-1,
\]

\[
\mathbb E[H_s^{(1)}H_k^{(1)}],
\qquad
\mathbb E[\delta_s^{(2)}\delta_k^{(2)}]
\tag{27}
\]

are deterministic. Start the oracle from the same \(z_0^{(1)},W_0^{(2)}\) as the finite network and from \(W_{\mathrm{oracle},0}^{(3)}=0\). At each step set

\[
h_{\mathrm{oracle},k}^{(1)}
=
\phi(F^{-1}(x_{\mathrm{oracle},k}^{(1)})),
\]

\[
\begin{aligned}
z_{\mathrm{oracle},k}^{(2)}
={}&
W_0^{(2)}h_{\mathrm{oracle},k}^{(1)}
\\
&-
2\Delta\sum_{s<k}
r_s\delta_{\mathrm{oracle},s}^{(2)}
\mathbb E[H_s^{(1)}H_k^{(1)}],
\end{aligned}
\tag{28}
\]

\[
h_{\mathrm{oracle},k}^{(2)}
=
\phi(z_{\mathrm{oracle},k}^{(2)}),
\]

\[
\delta_{\mathrm{oracle},k}^{(2)}
=
W_{\mathrm{oracle},k}^{(3)}
\odot\phi'(z_{\mathrm{oracle},k}^{(2)}),
\]

and then

\[
\begin{aligned}
x_{\mathrm{oracle},k+1}^{(1)}
={}&x_{\mathrm{oracle},k}^{(1)}
\\
&-
2\Delta r_k
\Bigg[
(W_0^{(2)})^\top\delta_{\mathrm{oracle},k}^{(2)}
\\
&\hspace{17mm}
-
2\Delta\sum_{s<k}
r_sh_{\mathrm{oracle},s}^{(1)}
\mathbb E[\delta_s^{(2)}\delta_k^{(2)}]
\Bigg],
\\
W_{\mathrm{oracle},k+1}^{(3)}
={}&
W_{\mathrm{oracle},k}^{(3)}
-
2\Delta r_kh_{\mathrm{oracle},k}^{(2)}.
\end{aligned}
\tag{29}
\]

This is a fixed finite Gaussian calculation. Its output coordinates are bounded by the deterministic number

\[
\pi\Delta\sum_{s<k}|r_s|.
\]

The product defining \(\delta_{\mathrm{oracle},k}^{(2)}\) can therefore be replaced, without changing any value, by a globally Lipschitz map that clips its first argument above this bound. All remaining coordinate operations meet the finite Gaussian statement.

Its limiting variables are exactly the population Euler variables: apply the definition of the population action instruction by instruction, together with the population versions of (5)–(6).

Record the oracle’s finite matrix memory as

\[
W_{\mathrm{oracle},k}^{(2)}
=
W_0^{(2)}
-
\frac{2\Delta}{n}
\sum_{s<k}
r_s\delta_{\mathrm{oracle},s}^{(2)}
(h_{\mathrm{oracle},s}^{(1)})^\top.
\]

Its actual action differs from the oracle preactivation by the exact identity

\[
\begin{aligned}
&
W_{\mathrm{oracle},k}^{(2)}
h_{\mathrm{oracle},k}^{(1)}
-
z_{\mathrm{oracle},k}^{(2)}
\\
&=
-2\Delta\sum_{s<k}
r_s\delta_{\mathrm{oracle},s}^{(2)}
\left(
\frac{
(h_{\mathrm{oracle},s}^{(1)})^\top
h_{\mathrm{oracle},k}^{(1)}
}{n}
-
\mathbb E[H_s^{(1)}H_k^{(1)}]
\right).
\end{aligned}
\tag{30}
\]

The backward discrepancy has the same form, with \(h_{\mathrm{oracle},s}^{(1)}\) multiplying the empirical-minus-population contraction of the two \(\delta^{(2)}\) vectors.

Let \(\zeta_n\) be the largest absolute error among these finitely many contractions, the output averages, and the squared normalized norms of the oracle vectors. The finite Gaussian result gives

\[
\zeta_n\longrightarrow0
\quad\text{in probability}.
\]

In particular, the oracle vector norms remain bounded.

Compare the actual Euler state and the oracle memory through

\[
\begin{aligned}
e_{n,k}
={}&
\frac{
\|x_k^{(1)}-x_{\mathrm{oracle},k}^{(1)}\|_2
}{\sqrt n}
\\
&+
\|W_k^{(2)}-W_{\mathrm{oracle},k}^{(2)}\|_{\mathrm{op}}
+
\frac{
\|W_k^{(3)}-W_{\mathrm{oracle},k}^{(3)}\|_2
}{\sqrt n}.
\end{aligned}
\]

Equations (22) and (30) bound the forward, backward, and residual differences by

\[
C_{T,\Delta}(e_{n,k}+\zeta_n).
\]

Subtracting the updates gives

\[
e_{n,k+1}
\le
(1+C_{T,\Delta}\Delta)e_{n,k}
+
C_{T,\Delta}\Delta\zeta_n.
\tag{31}
\]

Initially,

\[
e_{n,0}
=
\frac{\|W_0^{(3)}\|_2}{\sqrt n}
=
O_{\mathbb P}(n^{-1}).
\]

Since \(N=\lceil T/\Delta\rceil\) is fixed, summing the recurrence proves

\[
\max_{k\le N}e_{n,k}\longrightarrow0.
\]

This establishes the actual finite Euler width limit.

The argument also controls any fixed finite collection of additional forward and backward measurements. Expand each learned-matrix call by (5) or (6), construct its oracle version with the population contraction, and include the new empirical contraction errors in \(\zeta_n\). There are finitely many.

For the continuity estimates, permit finite compositions of globally Lipschitz coordinate maps, \(F^{-1}\), linear combinations, and the current matrix and transpose actions. Both varying factors in a product must be bounded, intrinsically or by clipping. Induction through these operations, using (22), bounds each measurement difference by a constant times \(d_n\), or times \(d\) on the population spaces.

Choose a countable collection of such measurements containing the coordinate functions used in the population construction. For each finite same-layer list, compare its joint laws in \(\mathcal W_2\). Define the action-law distance by truncating each such distance at \(1\) and summing with weights \(2^{-j}\).

For any fixed list, the finite-flow versus population-flow comparison is

\[
\underbrace{C_T\Delta}_{\text{finite flow versus Euler}}
+
\underbrace{o_{\mathbb P,n\to\infty;\,\Delta}(1)}
_{\text{finite Euler versus population Euler}}
+
\underbrace{C_T\Delta}_{\text{population Euler versus flow}}.
\tag{32}
\]

The constants here may depend on the fixed measurement list.

Choose \(\Delta\) small, then take \(n\to\infty\). Uniform time continuity of each fixed measurement, supplied by the state bounds and (22), makes the convergence uniform on \([0,T]\). The summable weights extend it to the action-law distance.

The same reasoning applies jointly at finitely many different times: append all those measurements to one finite oracle calculation before taking the width limit.

The limiting evolution is also autonomous at the level of this measured law. Start the measurement collection at a current state and take all its mean-square limits on each layer. These spaces are preserved by the current action, its adjoint, and the coordinate operations.

If two current measured laws agree, sending each measurement to its counterpart preserves all squared norms and inner products. This extends to an invertible linear map preserving distances between the two generated spaces. It commutes with coordinate functions, intertwines the current matrix and adjoint, and sends each rank-one update to its counterpart.

The integral iteration proving existence stays within these generated spaces. Uniqueness therefore identifies the future laws of the two states. Restarting at time \(s\) and evolving for time \(t\) gives the same law as evolving for \(s+t\).

We now return to exact GD.

For this cubic calculation only, let

\[
b=-2r_n(W^{(2)})^\top\delta^{(2)}
\]

be the transformed first-layer velocity. A GD step updates \(z^{(1)}\) by \(\eta_n\phi'(z^{(1)})\odot b\). Since \(F\) is cubic,

\[
\begin{aligned}
&
F\!\left(
z^{(1)}+\eta_n\phi'(z^{(1)})\odot b
\right)
\\
&=
F(z^{(1)})+\eta_nb
+
\eta_n^2z^{(1)}
\odot\phi'(z^{(1)})^2\odot b^2
\\
&\quad+
\frac{\eta_n^3}{3}
\phi'(z^{(1)})^3\odot b^3.
\end{aligned}
\tag{33}
\]

On the bounded state set,

\[
\frac{\|b\|_2}{\sqrt n}\le C_T,
\qquad
\|b\|_\infty\le C_T\sqrt n.
\]

Also \(\sup_s|s|\phi'(s)^2<\infty\). Consequently, the extra terms in (33) have root-mean-square size at most

\[
C_T(\eta_n^2\sqrt n+\eta_n^3n).
\]

The other two updates are already Euler updates. Add the \(O(\eta_n^2)\) local flow error and sum the recurrence:

\[
\sup_{t\le T}
d_n\!\left(
\Theta_n^{\mathrm{GD}}(t),
\Theta_n^{\mathrm{flow}}(t)
\right)
\le
C_T\left(
\eta_n+\eta_n\sqrt n+\eta_n^2n
\right)
\longrightarrow0.
\tag{34}
\]

As in the Euler proof, first stop on a larger bounded set. The output supremum is controlled separately by its sum of bounded activation updates, using the residual estimate transferred from the flow. This closes the stop.

At intermediate times, apply (33) with a partial step between \(0\) and \(\eta_n\). Thus (34) holds for the specified interpolation of the original parameters.

It remains to justify quantities containing an unbounded backward coordinate. To keep the following tail calculation readable, temporarily write

\[
p=(W^{(2)})^\top\delta^{(2)},
\qquad
P=(W^{(2)})^*\delta^{(2)}
\]

for the finite and population backward fields.

Equations (22), (32), and (34) show uniformly in time that the empirical joint law of \((z^{(1)},p)\) converges in \(\mathcal W_2\) to the law of \((Z^{(1)},P)\). The population variables are continuous in mean square, so their laws on \([0,T]\) form a compact \(\mathcal W_2\) family.

Their squared tails therefore vanish uniformly:

\[
\lim_{A\to\infty}\limsup_{n\to\infty}
\mathbb P\!\left(
\sup_{t\le T}
\frac1n\sum_i
p_i(t)^2\mathbf1_{\{|p_i(t)|>A\}}
>
\varepsilon
\right)
=0.
\tag{35}
\]

Here is the explicit estimate. For any coupling of \(p,P\), splitting according to \(|P|\le A/2\) gives

\[
\mathbb E[p^2\mathbf1_{\{|p|>A\}}]
\le
4\mathbb E[(p-P)^2]
+
2\mathbb E[P^2\mathbf1_{\{|P|>A/2\}}].
\]

A finite cover of the compact population family makes the population tails uniformly small. Uniform \(\mathcal W_2\) convergence then proves (35).

Clip \(p\) to \([-A,A]\). For fixed \(A\), both

\[
\phi'(z^{(1)})\operatorname{clip}_A(p),
\qquad
\phi'(z^{(1)})^2\operatorname{clip}_A(p)
\]

are globally Lipschitz measurements. Their squared errors from the unclipped fields are bounded by the tail in (35), since \(|\phi'|\le1\).

Applying \(W^{(2)}\) multiplies the root-mean-square error by at most its operator bound. Thus, first taking \(n\to\infty\), then \(A\to\infty\), proves uniform convergence of all three kernel blocks in (24). The finite blocks are obtained by replacing each expectation with the corresponding normalized coordinate sum.

The same argument controls the squared velocities given by

\[
\dot Z^{(1)}
=
-2r\phi'(Z^{(1)})(W^{(2)})^*\delta^{(2)},
\]

\[
\begin{aligned}
\dot Z^{(2)}
=
-2r\Bigl\{
&
\mathbb E[(H^{(1)})^2]\delta^{(2)}
\\
&+
W^{(2)}
\bigl[
\phi'(Z^{(1)})^2
(W^{(2)})^*\delta^{(2)}
\bigr]
\Bigr\}.
\end{aligned}
\tag{36}
\]

Predictions and losses converge uniformly directly from the bounded activations and output-weight bound.

For GD, the interpolated first-layer slope is its update velocity at the left endpoint. Differentiating

\[
z^{(2)}(t)=W^{(2)}(t)\phi(z^{(1)}(t))
\]

uses the constant parameter slopes and the current interpolated activation.

On coordinates where \(|p_i|\le A\), the change in \(\phi'(z_i^{(1)})\) multiplied by the first-layer slope is bounded by \(C_T\eta_nA^2\). On the complement, both terms are bounded by \(C_T|p_i|\). The other product-rule errors are \(O(\eta_n)\) in root-mean-square norm, using the bounded matrix slopes.

Equation (35), followed by \(n\to\infty\) at fixed \(A\), then \(A\to\infty\), therefore gives

\[
\int_0^T
\frac{
\|\partial_tz_n^{(\ell),\mathrm{GD}}(t)\|_2^2
}{n}\,dt
\longrightarrow
\int_0^T
\mathbb E[(\dot Z^{(\ell)}(t))^2]\,dt,
\qquad
\ell=1,2.
\tag{37}
\]

All these convergence statements are in probability.

For the trajectory laws, use \(\mathcal W_2\) with the supremum distance between continuous scalar paths. Let \(I_\pi z\) be polygonal interpolation on a time partition of mesh at most \(a\). Absolute continuity and Cauchy–Schwarz give

\[
\|z-I_\pi z\|_\infty^2
\le
4a\int_0^T|\dot z(t)|^2\,dt.
\tag{38}
\]

After coordinate averaging, (37) bounds the empirical distance to this finite-grid representation by \(O_{\mathbb P}(\sqrt a)\). The population bound is identical. The population paths have absolutely continuous versions: integrate the mean-square continuous velocities and apply Fubini.

At a fixed partition, joint grid-law convergence, including second moments, follows from (32) and (34). Polygonal interpolation is a continuous linear function of the finite grid vector. Taking \(n\to\infty\), then \(a\to0\) in (38), proves separately for both layers that

\[
\frac1n\sum_i
\delta_{z_{n,i}^{(\ell),\mathrm{GD}}(\cdot)}
\longrightarrow
\operatorname{Law}(Z^{(\ell)}(\cdot))
\quad\text{in }\mathcal W_2(C([0,T])).
\tag{39}
\]

Finally, we verify that the limit exhibits feature learning. For this initial-time calculation, define the following fixed population quantities, each of which will recur:

\[
m=\mathbb E[(H_0^{(1)})^2]>0,
\]

\[
U=H_0^{(2)}\phi'(Z_0^{(2)}),
\qquad
\nu=\mathbb E[U^2]>0,
\qquad
P_0=(W_0^{(2)})^*U.
\]

The row-conditioning calculation (8), without its \(2\Delta\) factor, gives

\[
P_0
=
\frac{\mathbb E[Z_0^{(2)}U]}{m}H_0^{(1)}
+
\sqrt\nu\,\Gamma^{(1)},
\tag{40}
\]

where \(\Gamma^{(1)}\sim N(0,1)\) is independent of the first-layer root.

Define the initial motion coefficient and second-layer response

\[
d=\mathbb E[(\phi'(Z_0^{(1)})P_0)^2]>0,
\]

\[
S_0
=
mU
+
W_0^{(2)}
\bigl[\phi'(Z_0^{(1)})^2P_0\bigr].
\]

The coefficient \(d\) is positive because (40) has positive independent Gaussian variance and \(\phi'>0\). Adjunction gives

\[
\mathbb E[US_0]=m\nu+d>0,
\tag{41}
\]

so \(S_0\) is nonzero in mean square.

Initially \(r=-1\) and \(W^{(3)}=0\). Dividing the integral equations by successive powers of \(t\) gives

\[
W^{(3)}(t)
=
2tH_0^{(2)}+O_{L^2}(t^2),
\qquad
\delta^{(2)}(t)
=
2tU+O_{L^2}(t^2),
\]

\[
Z^{(1)}(t)-Z_0^{(1)}
=
2t^2\phi'(Z_0^{(1)})P_0
+
o_{L^2}(t^2),
\]

\[
W^{(2)}(t)-W_0^{(2)}
=
2t^2U\otimes H_0^{(1)}
+
o_{\mathrm{op}}(t^2),
\]

\[
Z^{(2)}(t)-Z_0^{(2)}
=
2t^2S_0+o_{L^2}(t^2).
\tag{42}
\]

To check these orders, (25) first gives \(W^{(3)}=O(t)\), then \(\delta^{(2)}=O(t)\). Hence the changes in \(X^{(1)},W^{(2)},Z^{(2)}\) are \(O(t^2)\). Substitution into the output-weight equation and the definition of \(\delta^{(2)}\) gives the first line. Substitution once more gives the remaining lines. For instance,

\[
\frac{\dot X^{(1)}(t)}t\longrightarrow4P_0,
\]

and the bounded derivative of \(F^{-1}\) gives the first preactivation expansion.

Put \(k_3=\mathbb E[(H_0^{(2)})^2]>0\). Equations (24), (41), and (42) yield

\[
K^{(1)}(t)=4dt^2+o(t^2),
\qquad
K^{(2)}(t)=4m\nu t^2+o(t^2),
\]

\[
K^{(3)}(t)
=
k_3+4(m\nu+d)t^2+o(t^2),
\]

\[
K(t)
=
k_3+8(m\nu+d)t^2+o(t^2).
\tag{43}
\]

The squared hidden displacements have leading terms

\[
4dt^4,
\qquad
4\mathbb E[S_0^2]t^4,
\]

and their squared speeds have leading terms

\[
16dt^2,
\qquad
16\mathbb E[S_0^2]t^2.
\]

Both hidden layers therefore move, and every kernel block has a positive integral on sufficiently short nonzero intervals. Also,

\[
f(t)=2k_3t+o(t),
\qquad
L(t)=1-4k_3t+o(t).
\]

Both initial preactivation laws in (7) are nondegenerate Gaussians. Their variances remain positive by mean-square continuity.

For either layer, the error of the best affine fit to the activation is

\[
\operatorname{Var}(\phi(Z^{(\ell)}))
-
\frac{
\operatorname{Cov}(Z^{(\ell)},\phi(Z^{(\ell)}))^2
}{
\operatorname{Var}(Z^{(\ell)})
}.
\tag{44}
\]

It is initially positive: equality would force \(\arctan\) to agree with an affine function on a Gaussian law with full support, and therefore everywhere by continuity. The moments in (44) are continuous under mean-square convergence because \(\phi\) is bounded and Lipschitz. This error remains positive for both layers on a common interval \([0,T_*]\).

[556 | 2026-09-05T07:06:51.037Z | assistant | None]
I have not resolved the unconditional \(L=3\) theorem. The independent proof searches exposed a substantive missing argument; I cannot honestly turn it into a “complete proof” by assuming that argument.

Here is precisely what changes.

With three hidden layers, \(W^{(3)}\) is now a hidden-layer matrix and \(W^{(4)}\) is the rescaled output vector. Backpropagation gives
\[
\delta^{(3)}
=
W^{(4)}\odot\phi'(z^{(3)}),
\qquad
\delta^{(2)}
=
\phi'(z^{(2)})\odot
\bigl(W^{(3)}\bigr)^\top\delta^{(3)}.
\]

The output weights remain bounded coordinate by coordinate on every fixed time interval. Consequently, so does \(\delta^{(3)}\). But applying the transpose matrix gives only a bound on the **average squared size** of
\[
p^{(2)}
:=
\bigl(W^{(3)}\bigr)^\top\delta^{(3)}.
\]
It does not bound every coordinate of \(p^{(2)}\).

That distinction matters when comparing two nearby states. Exactly,
\[
\begin{aligned}
\delta^{(2)}-\widetilde\delta^{(2)}
={}&
\phi'(z^{(2)})\odot
\bigl(p^{(2)}-\widetilde p^{(2)}\bigr)
\\
&+
\bigl[\phi'(z^{(2)})-\phi'(\widetilde z^{(2)})\bigr]
\odot\widetilde p^{(2)}.
\end{aligned}
\]
The second line is the unresolved term. A small preactivation error can occur exactly where the backward signal is large, producing a nonvanishing update error.

This is not merely a concern about loose estimates. I constructed two network-consistent states whose parameter distance tends to zero like \(n^{-1/2}\), while the difference between their \(W^{(2)}\) velocities stays bounded away from zero. All the usual operator-norm, root-mean-square, and output-coordinate bounds hold.

Crucially, those states are **not shown to be reachable from the prescribed Gaussian initialization**. Thus this construction does not disprove your theorem. It proves that the bounded-state estimates used in the two-layer argument cannot, by themselves, supply the required vector-field continuity estimate.

The missing work is to establish stronger control along the actual Gaussian-initialized training trajectories, uniformly as the auxiliary Euler mesh is refined—or find another mechanism that makes that control unnecessary. Fixed-step Gaussian calculations do not establish this uniform passage to continuous time.

I did verify the exact finite-width equations, global finite-width flow, loss dissipation, and width-independent compact-time norm bounds. They are written out, together with the explicit obstruction, in the [checked mathematical note](/tmp/l3-arctan-audit-sZue2v/EXACT_RESULTS_AND_GAP.md). Two fresh, isolated reviewers passed the corrected note; the [review record](/tmp/l3-arctan-audit-sZue2v/REVIEW.md) explicitly limits those verdicts to its actual claims.

**The requested population-flow and joint-limit theorem remains unproved by this work—not disproved, and not completed conditionally.**

[335 | 2026-09-05T07:23:16.126Z | assistant | None]
I have not fully resolved the \(L=3\) theorem. I have neither a complete affirmative proof nor a counterexample. The independent checks identified a genuine obstruction to extending the two-layer proof, but that obstruction is **not** a proof that the desired limit is impossible.

Here is the precise mathematical situation.

With three hidden layers, the rescaled readout is \(W^{(4)}\), and
\[
f_n=\frac{(W^{(4)})^\top h^{(3)}}n.
\]
The last two backward derivatives are
\[
\delta^{(3)}
=
W^{(4)}\odot\phi'(z^{(3)}),
\qquad
\delta^{(2)}
=
\phi'(z^{(2)})
\odot
(W^{(3)})^\top\delta^{(3)}.
\]

On every bounded time interval, the readout is bounded coordinate by coordinate. Consequently, so is \(\delta^{(3)}\). But the additional matrix multiplication means that
\[
(W^{(3)})^\top\delta^{(3)}
\]
need not be bounded coordinate by coordinate. We can bound its mean square; that is not enough for the stability argument previously used.

To see why, compare two nearby states, writing a tilde for the second state. One term in their backward-derivative difference is
\[
\bigl[\phi'(z^{(2)})-\phi'(\widetilde z^{(2)})\bigr]
\odot
(\widetilde W^{(3)})^\top\widetilde\delta^{(3)}.
\]
The activation-derivative difference is small in mean square. However, it can be concentrated precisely on coordinates where the backward signal is large. Therefore, the earlier estimates do not give
\[
\frac{\left\|
\bigl[\phi'(z^{(2)})-\phi'(\widetilde z^{(2)})\bigr]
\odot
(\widetilde W^{(3)})^\top\widetilde\delta^{(3)}
\right\|_2}{\sqrt n}
\le
C_T\frac{\|z^{(2)}-\widetilde z^{(2)}\|_2}{\sqrt n}
\]
with a width-independent constant \(C_T\).

This is not merely a missing justification: **that uniform local-Lipschitz estimate is false near natural three-layer Euler states.**

For example, start the oracle readout at zero. After its first Euler update,
\[
W_1^{(4)}=2\Delta\phi(z_0^{(3)}),
\qquad
\delta_1^{(3)}
=
2\Delta\phi(z_0^{(3)})\odot\phi'(z_0^{(3)}).
\]
Gaussian conditioning shows that the population coordinate of
\((W_0^{(3)})^\top\delta_1^{(3)}\) has the form
\[
bH_0^{(2)}+\sigma G,
\qquad \sigma>0,
\]
where \(G\) is a standard Gaussian independent of \(Z_0^{(2)}\).

Thus there are coordinates where \(z_0^{(2)}\in[1,2]\) while the backward signal is arbitrarily large. On this interval, \(|\phi''|\) is bounded away from zero. A rank-one perturbation of \(W^{(2)}\) localized to such a coordinate makes the ratio
\[
\frac{\text{change in the matrix-update velocity}}
     {\text{change in the matrix}}
\]
arbitrarily large. The complete calculation, including the changing residual, is in the [checked mathematical note](/tmp/l3-extension-check-mj4s9q/VERIFIED_SCOPE.md).

What remains valid is global existence at each finite width, loss dissipation, and width-uniform mean-square and operator-norm bounds. What remains unresolved is the stronger control needed to turn the finite-step population construction into a unique autonomous evolution on **every** finite time interval, and then establish the prescribed joint limit with \(\eta_n=n^{-2}\). A finite-step tensor-program theorem does not supply this continuous-time step; Tensor Programs IV explicitly distinguishes these issues. [Appendix A](https://proceedings.mlr.press/v139/yang21c/yang21c-supp.pdf)

Two isolated reviewers accepted the narrower findings above; neither certified a complete \(L=3\) theorem. Their [review scope is recorded here](/tmp/l3-extension-check-mj4s9q/REVIEW.md).

Finally, “the proof is incomplete,” “the theorem is false,” and “the theorem is unprovable” are different claims. I established the first, not either of the others. Presenting this as a fully resolved affirmative or negative result would be mathematically incorrect.

[628 | 2026-09-05T07:51:53.970Z | assistant | None]
The full \(L=3\) mean-field/gradient-flow theorem is **not resolved yet**. I have neither a complete affirmative proof nor a counterexample. I’m keeping the goal active; the findings below do not justify marking it complete.

There is substantial unconditional progress for your exact arctangent model and Gaussian initialization:

- [Finite gradient-flow proof](/tmp/l3-full-resolution-9nbD4z/READOUT_COERCIVITY.md): with probability tending to one, the output kernel stays uniformly positive, loss decays exponentially, and every parameter converges to an interpolating endpoint.
- [Exact-GD proof](/tmp/l3-full-resolution-9nbD4z/EXACT_GD_COERCIVITY.md): at \(\eta_n=n^{-2}\), the prediction never overshoots the target and converges exponentially; every parameter also converges.

Both complete finite-width proofs passed two independent reviews. These are optimization theorems—not certifications of the requested population limit.

The unresolved issue remains the middle-layer backward vector:
\[
\delta^{(2)}
=
\phi'(z^{(2)})\odot
\bigl(W^{(3)}\bigr)^\top\delta^{(3)}.
\]
Comparing two evolving states produces the term
\[
\bigl[\phi'(z^{(2)})-\phi'(\widetilde z^{(2)})\bigr]
\odot
\bigl(\widetilde W^{(3)}\bigr)^\top\widetilde\delta^{(3)}.
\]
The proved energy bounds control average squared sizes, but do not yet control this product sufficiently to establish population uniqueness and convergence as the time mesh vanishes.

Thus successful training of every finite network does not yet prove that their evolving states approach one autonomous population flow. Conversely, failure of this comparison estimate does not prove that your theorem is false or unprovable. The precise remaining obligation is recorded [here](/tmp/l3-full-resolution-9nbD4z/NEXT_PROOF_OBLIGATION.md).

[6653 | 2026-09-05T13:01:28.148Z | assistant | None]
Yes—as a **complementary attempt**, not a replacement. A fresh thread may explore different approaches, although it may also rediscover old ones. ## Conjecture: autonomous three-hidden-layer mean-field gradient flow

Let \(\phi(z)=\arctan z\). Consider a network with one input and target, both equal to \(1\), and three hidden layers of width \(n\). Its parameters are
\[
z^{(1)}\in\mathbb R^n,\qquad
W^{(2)},W^{(3)}\in\mathbb R^{n\times n},
\qquad W^{(4)}\in\mathbb R^n.
\]
Here \(z^{(1)}\) is the first-layer weight vector evaluated at the input, and \(W^{(4)}\) is the rescaled output weight.

At step \(k\), define
\[
h_k^{(\ell)}=\phi(z_k^{(\ell)}),\qquad
z_k^{(2)}=W_k^{(2)}h_k^{(1)},\qquad
z_k^{(3)}=W_k^{(3)}h_k^{(2)},
\]
\[
f_{n,k}=\frac{(W_k^{(4)})^\top h_k^{(3)}}n,\qquad
r_{n,k}=f_{n,k}-1,\qquad L_{n,k}=r_{n,k}^2,
\]
and
\[
\begin{aligned}
\delta_k^{(3)}
&=W_k^{(4)}\odot\phi'(z_k^{(3)}),\\
\delta_k^{(2)}
&=\phi'(z_k^{(2)})\odot
       (W_k^{(3)})^\top\delta_k^{(3)},\\
\delta_k^{(1)}
&=\phi'(z_k^{(1)})\odot
       (W_k^{(2)})^\top\delta_k^{(2)}.
\end{aligned}
\]
The activation and its derivative act coordinatewise; \(\odot\) denotes coordinatewise multiplication.

Initialize all entries independently, across and within parameter blocks:
\[
z_{0,i}^{(1)}\sim N(0,1),\qquad
W_{0,ij}^{(2)},W_{0,ij}^{(3)}\sim N(0,n^{-1}),\qquad
W_{0,i}^{(4)}\sim N(0,n^{-2}).
\]
With \(\eta_n=n^{-2}\), train every block by the exact updates
\[
\begin{aligned}
z_{k+1}^{(1)}
&=z_k^{(1)}-2\eta_n r_{n,k}\delta_k^{(1)},\\
W_{k+1}^{(\ell)}
&=W_k^{(\ell)}
-\frac{2\eta_n r_{n,k}}n
 \delta_k^{(\ell)}(h_k^{(\ell-1)})^\top,
&&\ell=2,3,\\
W_{k+1}^{(4)}
&=W_k^{(4)}-2\eta_n r_{n,k}h_k^{(3)}.
\end{aligned}
\]
Use physical time \(t=k\eta_n\), interpolate the raw parameters linearly, and recompute the hidden quantities from them. The corresponding finite-width gradient flow replaces these updates by their differential equations. These dynamics use normalized Euclidean squared lengths for the vector blocks and ordinary Frobenius squared lengths for the matrix blocks.

**Conjecture.** The following unconditional statement holds.

There exist three fixed probability spaces \((\Omega_\ell,\mu_\ell)\), independent of width and time horizon, supporting a population state consisting of exactly
\[
Z^{(1)}(t),\qquad W^{(2)}(t),\qquad
W^{(3)}(t),\qquad W^{(4)}(t).
\]
Here \(Z^{(1)}\) and \(W^{(4)}\) are square-integrable scalar fields on \(\Omega_1\) and \(\Omega_3\), respectively, and
\[
W^{(\ell)}(t):L^2(\Omega_{\ell-1})\longrightarrow L^2(\Omega_\ell),
\qquad \ell=2,3,
\]
are bounded linear operators. Write \(\mathbb E_\ell\) for integration over \(\Omega_\ell\), and \((W^{(\ell)})^*\) for the adjoint, characterized by
\[
\mathbb E_\ell[U\,W^{(\ell)}V]
=
\mathbb E_{\ell-1}[V\,(W^{(\ell)})^*U].
\]

Define the population forward and backward quantities by the finite-network formulas above, replacing \(z,h\) by \(Z,H\), transposes by adjoints, and
\[
f(t)=\mathbb E_3[W^{(4)}(t)H^{(3)}(t)],
\qquad r(t)=f(t)-1,\qquad L(t)=r(t)^2.
\]
The evolution is
\[
\begin{aligned}
\dot Z^{(1)}&=-2r\,\delta^{(1)},\\
\dot W^{(\ell)}U
&=-2r\,\delta^{(\ell)}
       \mathbb E_{\ell-1}[H^{(\ell-1)}U],
&&\ell=2,3,\quad U\in L^2(\Omega_{\ell-1}),\\
\dot W^{(4)}&=-2r\,H^{(3)}.
\end{aligned}
\]
Initially \(Z_0^{(1)}\sim N(0,1)\) and \(W_0^{(4)}=0\). The joint initial operator actions must reproduce the Gaussian initialization through the empirical-convergence requirement below at \(t=0\), including arbitrary finite reuse of both matrices and their transposes. Each probability space is generated by these initial measurement fields; it may not contain additional information encoding the future training trajectory.

On these fixed spaces, the equations have a unique global continuous integral solution, with locally bounded field second moments and operator norms. The solution is uniquely restartable from every reached state. The four displayed objects constitute the entire evolving state: no external time-dependent coefficients or additional history are supplied.

Moreover, this is the gradient flow of \(L\), with squared length of a parameter variation given by
\[
\mathbb E_1[(dZ^{(1)})^2]
+\|dW^{(2)}\|_{\mathrm{HS}}^2
+\|dW^{(3)}\|_{\mathrm{HS}}^2
+\mathbb E_3[(dW^{(4)})^2].
\]
Here \(\|A\|_{\mathrm{HS}}^2=\sum_j\mathbb E_\ell[(Ae_j)^2]\) for an orthonormal basis of the input space—the operator counterpart of squared Frobenius norm. The trained matrix increments, not necessarily the initial operators themselves, have finite such norm on every compact time interval.

The finite GD and finite gradient-flow systems converge jointly to this same population evolution as \(n\to\infty\), along the full sequence, in the following sense.

Take any fixed finite collection of same-layer vector measurements formed from the parameter fields and constants using:

- fixed globally Lipschitz coordinate functions and multiplication by bounded globally Lipschitz coordinate functions;
- normalized inner products;
- any finite, layer-compatible composition of the current matrices and their transposes.

The instructions are independent of \(n\). The collection may also include the named backward fields, hidden preactivation and activation velocities, and measurements at finitely many other fixed times. Population measurements use the same instructions, with expectations and adjoints replacing normalized inner products and transposes.

For every such collection \(v_n^1(t),\ldots,v_n^m(t)\), every continuous \(\psi:\mathbb R^m\to\mathbb R\) satisfying
\[
|\psi(a)|\le C\left(1+\sum_{j=1}^m a_j^2\right),
\]
and every fixed \(T<\infty\),
\[
\sup_{0\le t\le T}
\left|
\frac1n\sum_{i=1}^n
\psi\!\left(v_{n,i}^1(t),\ldots,v_{n,i}^m(t)\right)
-
\mathbb E_\ell
\psi\!\left(V^1(t),\ldots,V^m(t)\right)
\right|
\xrightarrow{\mathbb P}0.
\]
This applies jointly to GD and finite gradient flow started from the same initialization, with corresponding limiting fields identified. GD velocities use right derivatives at mesh points and the left derivative at a terminal mesh point. No coordinatewise pairing between different neuron layers is asserted.

The empirical laws of entire hidden preactivation paths also converge: for every continuous function \(\Psi\) on \(C([0,T])\) of at most quadratic growth in the supremum norm,
\[
\frac1n\sum_{i=1}^n\Psi(z_{n,i}^{(\ell)}(\cdot))
\xrightarrow{\mathbb P}
\mathbb E_\ell[\Psi(Z^{(\ell)}(\cdot))].
\]
In particular, predictions, losses, integrated squared hidden velocities, and all four kernel contributions
\[
\left(
\frac{\|\delta^{(1)}\|_2^2}{n},\
\frac{\|\delta^{(2)}\|_2^2\|h^{(1)}\|_2^2}{n^2},\
\frac{\|\delta^{(3)}\|_2^2\|h^{(2)}\|_2^2}{n^2},\
\frac{\|h^{(3)}\|_2^2}{n}
\right)
\]
converge to their corresponding population expressions; the time-dependent quantities converge uniformly on \([0,T]\).

Finally, every hidden layer undergoes nontrivial feature learning:
\[
0<
\lim_{t\downarrow0}
\frac{\mathbb E_\ell[
|H^{(\ell)}(t)-H^{(\ell)}(0)|^2]}{t^4}
<\infty,
\qquad \ell=1,2,3.
\]

Prove or refute this conjecture as stated. A refutation must contradict a specified conclusion for the prescribed Gaussian-initialized system; failure of a particular proof strategy or a counterexample with different initialization does not suffice.
